Result: Experiments on AuctionNet demonstrate that GRM improves constraint stability and overall score compared to existing baselines. Theoretical results show the optimality gap relative to full per-tick control is bounded by dispersion of per-tick marginal value-per-cost, and constraint violations under replanning are bounded by prediction error.

Conclusion: The GRM approach effectively addresses limitations of prior auto-bidding methods by predicting future responses rather than actions, enabling robust constraint satisfaction and improved performance under non-stationary, uncertain auction environments.

Abstract: Auto-bidding systems aim to maximize advertiser value over long horizons under budget constraints and ratio targets such as cost-per-acquisition, yet future traffic and auction dynamics are non-stationary and uncertain. Existing approaches face distinct limitations: control-based pacing reacts to deviations but cannot anticipate future conditions, while RL and generative methods fold constraints into reward signals, obscuring violations and degrading under distribution shift. We shift the learning target from actions to responses with the Generative Response Model (GRM), a history-conditioned sequence model that jointly predicts future traffic volume and horizon-aggregate cost/value curves as functions of a single bid multiplier. We show that under mild monotonicity conditions, the optimality gap relative to full per-tick control is bounded by the dispersion of per-tick marginal value-per-cost. Given predicted responses, a lightweight analytic controller enforces each active constraint via a 1D root-finding step. We prove this controller is exact for the single-multiplier problem and bound constraint violations under receding-horizon replanning in terms of prediction error. Experiments on AuctionNet show that GRM improves constraint stability and overall score compared to existing baselines.

</details>


### [28] [EgoBench: An Interactive Egocentric Multimodal Benchmark for Tool-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27820)
*Yunqi Liu,Tong Niu,Zitong Wang,Zhenlong Dai,Yuqi Qing,Weiqiang Wang,Jian Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EgoBench is the first interactive multimodal benchmark for tool-using AI agents, featuring 1,045 egocentric-video-grounded tasks across four daily scenarios. It jointly evaluates visual perception, tool-augmented multi-hop reasoning, and dynamic user interaction via a three-stage pipeline, multi-agent simulated user, and deterministic joint validation framework. State-of-the-art video-MLLM agents achieve only 19.43% average accuracy, revealing a significant performance ceiling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks fail to jointly evaluate multimodal perception, tool invocation with multi-hop reasoning, and dynamic user interaction due to challenges in designing coupled multi-capability tasks, simulating natural user feedback, and ensuring objective evaluation of dynamic interaction.

Method: EgoBench introduces (1) a three-stage synergistic pipeline to enforce joint visual perception and tool-augmented multi-hop reasoning, (2) a multi-agent simulated user that generates task-aligned responses for evaluating interaction capabilities, and (3) a deterministic joint validation framework using process-based and result-based equivalence for objective assessment.

Result: Benchmarking eight SOTA video-MLLM agents shows a severe performance ceiling: the best model achieves 30.62% accuracy in the best scenario, with an average of 19.43% across all four scenarios. Multi-dimensional error analysis exposes capability bottlenecks.

Conclusion: Current AI agents significantly underperform on tasks requiring synergistic multimodal perception, tool-using reasoning, and dynamic interaction, highlighting critical bottlenecks for future agent development.

Abstract: As AI agents increasingly operate in open, real-world environments, they require a deep synergy of multimodal perception, tool invocation with multi-hop reasoning, and dynamic interaction with users. However, existing benchmarks fail to jointly evaluate these capabilities due to challenges in designing strictly coupled multi-capability tasks, simulating natural and task-constrained user feedback, and ensuring objective evaluation of dynamic interaction. To bridge this gap, we introduce EgoBench, the first interactive multimodal benchmark for tool-using agents. EgoBench comprises 1,045 egocentric-video-grounded tasks covering four daily scenarios, along with a user-agent-tool interactive environment for evaluation. We implement a three-stage synergistic pipeline through which each task is designed to enforce the joint application of visual perception and tool-augmented multi-hop reasoning. We additionally develop a multi-agent simulated user within EgoBench to evaluate agents' interaction capabilities, which generates high-fidelity, task-aligned responses to agents. Furthermore, we establish a deterministic joint validation framework that guarantees objective assessment through process-based and result-based equivalence. Benchmarking eight SOTA video-MLLM agents on EgoBench reveals a severe performance ceiling: the best model achieves only 30.62% accuracy in the best-performing scenario, averaging 19.43% across all four scenarios. Finally, we conduct a multi-dimensional error analysis to disentangle failure modes, exposing capability bottlenecks for advancing future AI agents.

</details>


### [29] [Revealing Algorithmic Deductive Circuits for Logical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27824)
*Phuong Minh Nguyen,Tien Huu Dang,Naoya Inoue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates how LLMs understand abstract reasoning steps in symbolic-aided CoT prompting by localizing attention heads responsible for individual reasoning steps and characterizing information transfer. Key findings: (1) Low-confidence token positions steer reasoning; (2) ~3% of attention heads handle factual/rule-based sub-tasks; (3) Higher layers integrate information and coordinate global reasoning strategies like graph traversal algorithms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how LLMs genuinely grasp the abstract meaning of each reasoning step and the overall algorithm from limited demonstrations in symbolic-aided CoT prompting, which remains unclear despite strong reasoning performance.

Method: The authors align constituent reasoning steps with token logits under a symbolic-aided CoT framework, use causal mediation analysis to identify responsible attention heads, and analyze information transfer patterns between heads.

Result: Token positions steering reasoning are associated with low confidence scores due to reasoning behavior pattern constraints. ~3% of attention heads specialize in factual/rule-based sub-tasks, while higher layers integrate information and coordinate global reasoning strategies.

Conclusion: LLMs exhibit a functional division in reasoning: specialized attention heads handle individual sub-tasks, while higher layers orchestrate global reasoning strategies that coordinate multiple intermediate steps to solve complex tasks.

Abstract: Recent studies have shown that Large Language Models (LLMs) can achieve strong reasoning performance by incorporating functional symbolic representations that abstractly describe graph traversal algorithms and step-by-step reasoning in few-shot learning settings. However, it remains unclear how LLMs genuinely understand the abstract meaning of each reasoning step and the overall algorithm from only a limited number of demonstrations. This work aims to localize the attention heads responsible for individual reasoning steps and characterize the types of information transferred among them. We first align constituent reasoning steps with their corresponding token logits under a symbolic-aided Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting framework. Our analysis shows that token positions that steer the reasoning process are associated with low confidence scores caused by constraints on satisfying reasoning behavior patterns in demonstrations. We then adopt causal mediation analysis techniques to identify the attention heads responsible for these patterns. In addition, our findings indicate that LLMs retrieve factual and rule-based information for individual sub-reasoning tasks through specialized attention heads (approximately 3% total heads), whereas higher layers predominantly facilitate information integration and the emergence of global reasoning strategies (e.g., graph traversal algorithms) that coordinate multiple intermediate reasoning steps to solve the overall task.

</details>


### [30] [Operational AI Deployment Assurance: Governance-State Orchestration Under Threshold-Sensitive Deployment Conditions -- A Governance Framework for High-Stakes AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27827)
*Khalid Adnan Alsayed*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Operational AI Deployment Assurance (OADA), a governance framework that transforms fairness disagreement, subgroup instability, threshold sensitivity, and remediation outcomes into deployment-oriented assurance decisions. It extends prior FDI and FairRisk-FDI work by creating Deployment Assurance Scores, Readiness Classifications, Threshold Stability Zones, Governance Escalation States, and remediation-aware progression. Evaluated on facial recognition and healthcare AI domains, OADA reveals deployment readiness issues masked by isolated fairness or performance metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI governance frameworks rely on static metrics, post-hoc auditing, and monitoring dashboards that fail to directly govern deployment readiness, remediation progression, escalation states, or assurance-driven deployment control. This gap leaves systems that appear acceptable under isolated metrics potentially unstable for real-world deployment.

Method: OADA reframes governance uncertainty as an operational concern within deployment pipelines. It introduces five constructs: Deployment Assurance Scores, Deployment Readiness Classifications, Threshold Stability Zones, Governance Escalation States, and remediation-aware assurance progression. Evaluation is conducted on facial recognition systems with discussion extended to healthcare AI.

Result: Systems can appear acceptable under isolated fairness or performance metrics while still exhibiting instability that affects deployment readiness. OADA surfaces these hidden instabilities through its deployment-oriented evaluation framework.

Conclusion: OADA positions operational deployment assurance as a necessary governance layer between evaluation and real-world AI deployment, enabling lifecycle-oriented governance decisions across high-stakes settings.

Abstract: AI governance frameworks increasingly emphasize fairness, transparency, accountability, and lifecycle risk management in high-stakes domains. However, many current approaches remain observational, relying on static metric reporting, post-hoc auditing, and monitoring dashboards without directly governing deployment readiness, remediation progression, escalation states, or assurance-driven deployment control. This paper introduces Operational AI Deployment Assurance (OADA), a governance framework for translating fairness disagreement, subgroup instability, threshold sensitivity, remediation outcomes, and operational uncertainty into deployment-oriented assurance decisions. Building on prior work on the Fairness Disagreement Index (FDI) and FairRisk-FDI, OADA reframes governance uncertainty as an operational concern within AI deployment pipelines rather than a byproduct of metric disagreement. The framework introduces Deployment Assurance Scores, Deployment Readiness Classifications, Threshold Stability Zones, Governance Escalation States, and remediation-aware assurance progression. These constructs support lifecycle-oriented governance decisions across high-stakes settings by connecting evaluation outputs to deployment-state interpretation, reassessment, escalation, and operational control. Through deployment-oriented evaluation across facial recognition systems, with discussion extended to healthcare AI as a representative high-stakes domain, the paper demonstrates how systems may appear acceptable under isolated fairness or performance metrics while still exhibiting instability that affects deployment readiness. The proposed framework positions operational deployment assurance as a governance layer between evaluation and real-world AI deployment.

</details>


### [31] [EAPO: Entropy-Driven Adaptive Positive-Negative Sample Weighting for Policy Optimization in Open-Ended QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27846)
*Yunsheng Zeng,Gen Li,Yuwei Miao,Xiandong Li,Yujin Wang,Siyu Chen,Luning Wang,Yunhao Qiao,Junfeng Wang,Jianwei Lv,Bo Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EAPO adaptively weights positive/negative samples in RL for open-ended QA based on policy entropy to balance exploration and stability, outperforming fixed-weight baselines on medical QA datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RLVR approaches use fixed weights for positive and negative samples, which fail to generalize well to open-ended QA tasks due to poor handling of exploration-stability tradeoffs.

Method: EAPO uses a reward-mean-based strategy to distinguish positive/negative samples and adaptively computes weighting coefficients for positive samples based on the ratio of current policy entropy to initial entropy, reducing weight during entropy decrease (preserve exploration) and amplifying it during entropy increase (reinforce stability).

Result: On two open-ended medical QA datasets, EAPO consistently and substantially outperforms fixed-weight baselines in both response diversity and stability.

Conclusion: Negative samples govern diversity and performance upper bound; positive samples determine quality and convergence stability. Adaptive entropy-driven weighting mitigates entropy collapse and improves RL for open-ended QA.

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models are typically trained via reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR). However, existing approaches adopt fixed weights for positive and negative samples, and the conclusions hardly generalize to open-ended question answering (QA). In this paper, we systematically investigate the roles of positive and negative samples in reinforcement learning for open-ended QA. We propose a reward-mean-based strategy for distinguishing positive from negative samples, and observe that negative samples predominantly govern response diversity and the performance upper bound, whereas positive samples primarily determine response quality and convergence stability. Building on these observations, we propose EAPO, an Entropy-driven Adaptive Policy Optimization method that adaptively computes the weighting coefficients of positive samples based on the ratio of the current policy entropy to the initial entropy. During the entropy-decreasing phase, the weight assigned to positive samples is reduced to preserve exploration, whereas during the entropy-increasing phase it is amplified to reinforce stability, thereby mitigating entropy collapse. Experiments on two publicly available open-ended medical QA datasets demonstrate that EAPO consistently and substantially outperforms fixed-weight baselines in both response diversity and stability.

</details>


### [32] [When Context Flips, Safety Breaks: Diagnosing Brittle Safety in Aligned Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27851)
*Dasol Choi,Alex Kwon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Language models exhibit "brittle safety"—rigidly following safety rules even when context changes make the nominally safe action harmful. Across 12 models, a safety-commonsense gap emerges (mean +17.4 pp), stemming from policy override (not miscomprehension). Action-level guardrails fail to catch catastrophic consequence-flips, motivating state-aware safety architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety benchmark scores are insufficient for deployment readiness because models fail to dynamically update their safety judgments when situational context flips which action is actually safe, a failure termed "brittle safety."

Method: Introduced "context-flip evaluation" using a safety benchmark (PacifAIst) and two commonsense controls with paired variants where the nominally safe action causes harm. Tested 12 models, analyzed failure mechanisms via hand-audited probes, and compared action-level guardrails vs. state-aware validators.

Result: All 12 models exhibit a safety-commonsense gap (+17.4 pp mean); baseline accuracy does not predict brittleness (13.7%–90.0% across high-accuracy models). Failures stem from three distinct policy override mechanisms. Action-level guardrails catch none of the catastrophic consequence-flips, while a state-aware validator catches all without false alarms.

Conclusion: Action-level content moderation is systematically blind to consequence-flips, motivating state-aware architectural alternatives for safety.

Abstract: Safety benchmark scores provide incomplete evidence of deployment readiness: aligned language models often adhere to rigid rules even when a situational update flips which action is safe. We term this failure brittle safety. To diagnose it, we introduce context-flip evaluation, testing 12 models across a safety benchmark (PacifAIst) and two commonsense controls using paired variants where the nominally safe action produces harm. Three findings emerge. First, brittle safety is safety-specific: all 12 models exhibit a safety-commonsense gap (mean +17.4 pp). Baseline accuracy fails to predict brittleness: among models above 90% baseline accuracy, brittleness rates range from 13.7% to 90.0%. Second, failures stem from policy override rather than miscomprehension: despite acknowledging the context change in every case, models persist via three distinct mechanisms that vary by update type and model family. Third, on a hand-audited probe of catastrophic consequence-flip scenarios, standard action-level guardrails catch none, while a state-aware validator catches all without false alarms on correct interventions. This indicates that action-level content moderation is systematically blind to consequence-flips, motivating state-aware architectural alternatives. We release our protocol, perturbed benchmarks, and deployment probe.

</details>


### [33] [C-MIG: Multi-view Information Gain-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Clinical Diagnosis Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27860)
*Yuwei Miao,Gen Li,Yunsheng Zeng,Xiandong Li,Yujin Wang,Siyu Chen,Luning Wang,Yunhao Qiao,Junfeng Wang,Jianwei Lv,Bo Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: C-MIG is a Multi-view Information Gain-based RAG framework for clinical diagnosis that uses two complementary views (retrieved-document and document-refinement) to address the limitations of exact-match binary rewards in RAG+RL methods, achieving state-of-the-art performance on medical benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RAG+RL methods for medical diagnosis rely on exact-match binary rewards, causing two issues: (i) semantically relevant but non-verbatim steps receive zero reward signal, wasting valuable learning signals; and (ii) uni-dimensional rewards cannot effectively supervise heterogeneous reasoning capabilities.

Method: C-MIG estimates information gain under a frozen reference model from two complementary views (retrieved-document and document-refinement) to jointly guide retrieval and refinement. It also designs a multi-subquery retrieval augmentation strategy to improve knowledge recall coverage.

Result: On four medical benchmarks, C-MIG achieves the best performance among all RAG-RL methods on both in-domain and out-of-domain sets, and outperforms state-of-the-art general-purpose LLMs for clinical diagnosis.

Conclusion: The multi-view information gain approach effectively alleviates reward signal loss and credit assignment problems in clinical diagnosis RAG+RL, and the multi-subquery strategy enhances knowledge coverage.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation combined with reinforcement learning has shown promise for grounding large language models in trustworthy medical evidence. However, existing methods rely on exact-match binary rewards, which in clinical diagnosis cause two issues: (i) semantically relevant but non-verbatim steps receive zero signal, discarding valuable learning signals; and (ii) uni-dimensional rewards cannot effectively supervise heterogeneous reasoning capabilities. To address these issues, we propose C-MIG, a Multi-view Information Gain-based retrieval-augmented generation framework for Clinical diagnosis. C-MIG estimates information gain under a frozen reference model from two complementary views, retrieved-document and document-refinement, to jointly guide what to retrieve and how to refine, alleviating the issues of valuable reward signal loss and credit assignment. We further design a multi-subquery retrieval augmentation strategy that improves knowledge recall coverage in clinical diagnostic scenarios. Comprehensive experiments on four medical benchmarks demonstrate that C-MIG achieves the best performance among all RAG-RL methods on both in-domain and out-of-domain sets, and outperforms state-of-the-art general-purpose LLMs for clinical diagnosis.

</details>


### [34] [FundaPod: A Multi-Persona Agent Pod Platform with Knowledge Graph Memory for AI-Assisted Fundamental Investment Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27864)
*Di Zhu,Lei,Zheng,Zihan Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FundaPod is a multi-persona AI agent platform for fundamental investment research, featuring independence-preserving architecture where agents with different investment personas conduct independent research under a shared provenance contract, with results adjudicated by human portfolio managers through a knowledge-graph memory system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM applications in finance focus on trading signals and predictive NLP tasks, but institutional fundamental research requires evidence gathering, business driver identification, viewpoint comparison, and memo generation for transparent, reusable, verifiable investment plans. This is a qualitatively distinct, human-centric decision-support task poorly served by current approaches.

Method: FundaPod implements five design principles for human-AI hybrid systems through four architectural mechanisms: (1) a persona distillation pipeline converting public investor materials into deployable agents; (2) a declarative skill registry for typed task graph derivation; (3) a grounded evidence model linking memo claims to verifiable sources; and (4) a knowledge-graph "second brain" connecting tickers, memos, analysts, and themes. The system preserves agent independence and surfaces disagreements post hoc for human PM adjudication.

Result: The architecture is demonstrated through a complete case study and a persona-based memo comparison.

Conclusion: Fundamental research is better served by an independence-preserving architecture rather than trading-signal generation approaches. The paper contributes design principles for human-AI hybrid systems grounded in cognitive isolation and human-machine coordination theories.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied in finance, yet most existing work emphasizes trading signals or financial NLP tasks centered on prediction. Institutional fundamental research, by contrast, requires human analysts or AI agents to gather evidence, identify business drivers, compare competing viewpoints, and generate investment memos. Its broader goal is not merely to predict outcomes, but to produce investment plans that are transparent, reusable, and verifiable, while contributing to the cumulative development of investment knowledge. We present FundaPod, a multi-persona agent platform for AI-assisted fundamental investment research. We argue that fundamental research is a human-centric decision-support task that is qualitatively distinct from trading-signal generation, and is therefore better served by an independence-preserving architecture. In FundaPod, AI agents with different personas, such as value investors or macro strategists, conduct research independently under a shared provenance contract. Their disagreements are then surfaced post hoc for adjudication by the human portfolio manager (PM) through a knowledge-graph memory system. This paper contributes five design principles for human-AI hybrid systems supporting fundamental research, grounded in design-science practice and theories of cognitive isolation and human-machine coordination. It also describes four architectural mechanisms: a persona distillation pipeline that turns public investor materials into deployable agents; a declarative skill registry that lets the planner derive typed task graphs; a grounded evidence model that links memo claims to verifiable sources; and a knowledge-graph "second brain" that connects tickers, memos, analysts, and themes. We demonstrate the architecture through a complete case study and a persona-based memo comparison.

</details>


### [35] [AIBuildAI-2: A Knowledge-Enhanced Agent for Automatically Building AI Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27873)
*Ruiyi Zhang,Peijia Qin,Qi Cao,Li Zhang,Pengtao Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AIBuildAI-2 is a knowledge-enhanced agent with an external, hierarchical, and evolving knowledge system for automatically building AI models, achieving state-of-the-art results including first place on MLE-Bench (70.7% medal rate) and top 6.6% in a heart disease prediction competition with 4,370 human-expert teams.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Developing AI models remains heavily manual and requires specialized AI engineering expertise, creating a barrier for natural scientists. Existing agents for automatic AI model building are limited by the static, outdated, and sparse parametric knowledge of their underlying LLMs.

Method: AIBuildAI-2 uses an external hierarchical knowledge system with high-level knowledge instructions over topical categories and low-level knowledge documents. It dynamically loads only context-relevant knowledge, initializes from web-sourced AI-development documents, and continuously evolves by distilling completed runs into structured takeaways written back into the knowledge system.

Result: Ranked first on MLE-Bench with a 70.7% medal rate and placed in the top 6.6% among 4,370 human-expert teams in a heart disease prediction competition.

Conclusion: AIBuildAI-2 demonstrates that augmenting LLM-based agents with an external, evolving knowledge system significantly improves their ability to automatically build high-performing AI models, broadening access to AI for scientific discovery.

Abstract: AI models underpin data-centric applications from image and text processing to scientific discovery in biology, physics, and chemistry. Yet developing them remains heavily manual, requiring practitioners to design architectures, build training pipelines, and iteratively refine solutions, making it challenging for natural scientists without specialized AI engineering expertise to build the high-performing models their research demands. To reduce this burden and broaden access to AI for scientific discovery, agents that automatically build AI models have been proposed. However, the performance of these agents is largely limited by the parametric knowledge of their underlying large language models, which is static, often outdated, and sparse on practical AI model engineering know-how. To address this limitation, we introduce AIBuildAI-2, a knowledge-enhanced agent with an external, evolving knowledge system for automatically building AI models. The knowledge system of AIBuildAI-2 is hierarchical, organizing curated AI development knowledge into high-level knowledge instructions over topical categories and low-level knowledge documents under each category, from which the agent dynamically loads only the context relevant to its current state and the AI task being solved, grounding each design and implementation decision in concrete, externally verifiable expertise. The system is initialized by collecting and cleaning AI-development-related documents from the web and organizing them into the corresponding categories, and continually evolves from the agent's own experience by distilling each completed run on an AI task into structured takeaways that are written back into the knowledge system. AIBuildAI-2 achieves state-of-the-art results, ranking first on MLE-Bench with a 70.7% medal rate and placing in the top 6.6% among 4,370 human-expert teams in a heart disease prediction competition.

</details>


### [36] [Towards Faithful Agentic XAI: A Verification Method and an Open-World Benchmark for Better Model Faithfulness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27879)
*Jaechang Kim,Sunung Mun,Seungjoon Lee,Jaewoong Cho,Jungseul Ok*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FAX is a framework that improves faithfulness of LLM-based explainable AI by decomposing draft explanations into claims and verifying them against inherently faithful tools, achieving significant faithfulness gains (0.20→0.46) on a new reinforcement learning benchmark while maintaining quality metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic XAI systems using LLMs can produce plausible yet unfaithful explanations because unreliable XAI outputs for complex models can be amplified by LLMs and mislead users.

Method: FAX decomposes draft explanations into claims and cross-checks them against inherently faithful tools, filtering unsupported or contradictory claims before final generation. Also introduces CRAFTER-XAI-Bench, an open-world RL benchmark for assessing model-specific faithfulness.

Result: On CRAFTER-XAI-Bench, FAX improves simulation faithfulness from 0.20 (strongest baseline) to 0.46 while maintaining high informativeness, relevance, and fluency. On three tabular benchmarks, FAX performs competitively with prior baselines.

Conclusion: Explicit verification is essential for faithful Agentic XAI, and faithfulness benchmarks must be designed to test explanations against the behavior of the target model itself rather than conflating task accuracy with faithfulness.

Abstract: Explainable AI (XAI) helps users interpret model behavior and identify potential faults. Agentic XAI systems use Large Language Models (LLMs) to make explanations more accessible through natural-language interaction, but they can also produce plausible yet unfaithful explanations. This risk arises because unreliable XAI outputs for complex models can be amplified by LLMs and mislead users. We propose Faithful Agentic XAI (FAX), a framework that improves explanation faithfulness through explicit verification. FAX decomposes draft explanations into claims and cross-checks them against inherently faithful tools, filtering unsupported or contradictory claims before final generation. We also introduce CRAFTER-XAI-Bench, an open-world reinforcement learning benchmark with complex policies, diverse goals, and challenging scenarios for assessing model-specific faithfulness. On CRAFTER-XAI-Bench, FAX improves simulation faithfulness from 0.20 for the strongest baseline to 0.46 while maintaining high informativeness, relevance, and fluency. On three tabular benchmarks, FAX performs competitively with prior Agentic XAI baselines, but our analysis shows that these settings can conflate task accuracy with model-specific faithfulness. These findings show that explicit verification is essential for faithful Agentic XAI and that that faithfulness benchmarks must be designed to test explanations against the behavior of the target model itself.

</details>


### [37] [PortBench: A Correlation-Aware, Full-Pipeline Benchmark for LLM-Driven Portfolio Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27887)
*Yuxuan Zhao,Sijia Chen,Ningxin Su*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PortBench is a portfolio management benchmark for LLMs spanning six asset classes over ten years, featuring a static QA dataset (6,269 questions) and a dynamic five-stage allocation pipeline. It finds that despite strong static financial QA performance, 90% of LLM-profile combinations fail to beat a simple equal-weight allocation, and models satisfying all procedural constraints still suffer catastrophic drawdowns under stress.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing PM benchmarks ignore cross-asset correlation structures (failing to distinguish diversified vs. concentrated portfolios) and do not evaluate the complete PM decision pipeline in real-world scenarios, creating a gap in assessing LLM capabilities for portfolio management.

Method: PortBench includes (1) a static QA dataset of 6,269 correlation-based questions across seven task templates, and (2) a dynamic five-stage allocation pipeline mirroring the full PM decision cycle. It introduces dual-layer correlation score (inter-class hedging vs. intra-class concentration) and CEPS (compounding error propagation score), evaluating models under three historical stress regimes and risk profiles.

Result: Evaluating ten frontier LLMs, 90% of model-profile combinations fail to outperform a basic equal-weight allocation. Models that satisfy every procedural constraint still suffer catastrophic drawdowns under stress regimes.

Conclusion: Static financial QA performance does not translate to effective portfolio management; the full decision pipeline introduces compounding reasoning errors that even procedurally compliant models cannot overcome under market stress.

Abstract: LLMs have shown strong performance across diverse financial tasks, yet portfolio management (PM), a critical financial decision-making task, remains poorly benchmarked. Existing benchmarks exhibit two main gaps: they ignore cross-asset correlation structures, thereby failing to distinguish genuinely diversified portfolios from concentrated ones, and fail to evaluate the complete PM decision pipeline in real-world scenarios. We introduce PortBench, a benchmark spanning six heterogeneous asset classes over ten years. PortBench consists of two complementary layers: a static QA dataset of 6,269 correlation-based questions across seven task templates, and a dynamic five-stage allocation pipeline that mirrors the full PM decision cycle. To evaluate these layers, we introduce two dedicated metrics: a dual-layer correlation score that measures whether proposed portfolios exploit inter-class hedging and avoid intra-class concentration, and CEPS, a metric that quantifies how reasoning errors compound across pipeline stages. We further assess strategy robustness and investor alignment under three historical stress regimes and risk profiles. Evaluating ten frontier LLMs, we find that despite strong performance on static financial QA, 90\% of model-profile combinations fail to outperform a basic equal-weight allocation, and models that satisfy every procedural constraint still suffer catastrophic drawdowns under stress. Our source code is available at \href{https://github.com/AgenticFinLab/portbench}{this https URL}.

</details>


### [38] [A Unified Framework for the Evaluation of LLM Agentic Capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27898)
*Pengyu Zhu,Lijun Li,Yaxing Lyu,Qianxin Luo,Jingyi Yang,Yi Liu,Tingfeng Hui,Xinyu Yuan,Li Sun,Sen Su,Jing Shao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a unified evaluation framework for LLM agentic capabilities that standardizes diverse benchmarks via a configuration system, fixed ReAct architecture, and offline snapshots. Through 400K rollouts and 5B tokens across 15 models and 7 benchmarks (24 domains), it shows scaffold choice and environmental volatility significantly affect benchmark outcomes, enabling disentanglement of intrinsic LLM capabilities from framework/environment artifacts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmark scores reflect both model capability and implementation choices (scaffolding, environment volatility), making cross-benchmark comparisons unreliable as clean measures of the underlying LLM's agentic abilities.

Method: The framework uses a unified configuration system to standardize benchmarks into instruction-tool-environment format, executes agents via a fixed ReAct-style architecture in a controllable sandbox, optionally replaces live environments with curated offline snapshots, and introduces unified resource consumption metrics plus failure attribution taxonomy (decision-level vs execution-level). 7 benchmarks across single-agent, multi-agent, and safety-critical scenarios were adapted, with 400K rollouts and 5B tokens from 15 models analyzed.

Result: Scaffold choice and environmental volatility materially shift benchmark outcomes in both directions. The framework successfully disentangles intrinsic LLM capabilities from framework- and environment-induced artifacts.

Conclusion: The proposed framework enables fair, interpretable evaluation of LLM agentic capabilities and demonstrates extensibility as a secure testbed for safety-critical domains.

Abstract: As LLMs are increasingly deployed as agents, reliable assessment of their agentic capabilities has become essential. However, reported benchmark scores often jointly reflect model capability and the implementation choices each benchmark is packaged with, making cross-benchmark results difficult to interpret as clean measurements of the underlying model. In this work, we present a unified framework for the fair evaluation of LLM agentic capabilities. Driven by a unified configuration system, the framework integrates diverse benchmarks into a standardized instruction--tool--environment format, executes agents through a fixed ReAct-style architecture within a controllable sandbox, and provides an optional offline setting that replaces volatile live environments with curated snapshots, so that framework effects and environment effects can be analyzed separately. Building on this, we unify the evaluation methodology under each benchmark's original task-success criteria, while introducing unified metrics for resource consumption and a taxonomy for decision- and execution-level failure attribution. Within this framework, we adapt 7 widely used benchmarks spanning 24 domains across single-agent, multi-agent, and safety-critical scenarios, and conduct a large-scale empirical analysis over 400K rollouts and 5B tokens on 15 models. The results show that scaffold choice and environmental volatility materially shift benchmark outcomes in both directions, allowing our framework to disentangle intrinsic LLM capabilities from framework- and environment-induced artifacts. We further demonstrate its extensibility as a secure testbed for safety-critical domains. Codes and benchmarks at are available at https://github.com/whfeLingYu/A-Unified-Framework-for-the-Evaluation-of-LLM-Agentic-Capabilities, https://huggingface.co/AgentFramework/Unified_Farmework.

</details>


### [39] [SKILLC: Learning Autonomous Skill Internalization in LLM Agents via Contrastive Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27899)
*Hongxiang Lin,Zhirui Kuai,Erpeng Xue,Lei Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillC introduces Contrastive Skill Credit Assignment (CSCA) to convert skill-helpfulness contrast into a direct learning signal for skill internalization in RL, outperforming prior internalization methods on ALFWorld and WebShop without runtime skill access.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing skill-internalization RL methods only use skill-helpfulness contrast for curriculum control, leaving the policy update unchanged and unable to distinguish skill-dependent from autonomous success.

Method: SkillC samples paired skill-injected and skill-free rollouts, uses a dual-stream advantage estimator with one-sided correction toward skill-free success while preserving global ranking, and introduces a smoothed validation-level signal for adaptive curriculum over attribution strength, rollout allocation, and monotonic active-set pruning.

Result: On ALFWorld and WebShop, SkillC surpasses the strongest prior skill-internalization RL baseline by 5.5% and 4.4% respectively, while remaining competitive with skill-augmented RL methods.

Conclusion: Converting skill-helpfulness contrast into a direct learning signal via CSCA effectively enables autonomous performance without runtime skill access, achieving superior internalization.

Abstract: Structured skill prompts improve exploration in long-horizon agentic reinforcement learning (RL). Skill-augmented RL methods retain external skills at inference, while skill-internalization RL methods withdraw them during training to enable autonomous performance. However, existing internalization approaches only use skill-helpfulness contrast for curriculum control, leaving the policy update unchanged and unable to distinguish skill-dependent from autonomous success. We propose SkillC, a framework based on Contrastive Skill Credit Assignment (CSCA) that converts this contrast into a direct learning signal for internalization. \textsc{SkillC} samples paired skill-injected and skill-free rollouts for tasks from active skill types within the same policy update, and injects their task-level contrast into optimization via a dual-stream advantage estimator that preserves global ranking while applying a one-sided correction toward skill-free success. A smoothed validation-level signal further drives an adaptive curriculum over attribution strength, rollout allocation, and monotonic active-set pruning. Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop show that, without runtime skill access, SkillC surpasses the strongest prior skill-internalization RL baseline by 5.5\% and 4.4\%, respectively, while remaining competitive with skill-augmented RL methods.

</details>


### [40] [Dr-CiK: A Testbed for Foresight-Driven Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27904)
*Yihong Tang,Andrew Robert Williams,Arjun Ashok,Vincent Zhihao Zheng,Lijun Sun,Alexandre Drouin,Issam H. Laradji,Étienne Marcotte,Valentina Zantedeschi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Dr-CiK is a benchmark evaluating whether agents can independently retrieve, filter, and distill relevant context from documents to improve time series forecasting. Current deep research agents retrieve <5% of relevant evidence, cite distractors >80% of the time, and often degrade forecasting performance compared to no context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing context-aided forecasting benchmarks assume supporting context is already provided, but real-world agents must discover relevant external context from noisy, heterogeneous sources on their own.

Method: Dr-CiK benchmark: agents must retrieve forecasting-relevant context from a document corpus, filter distractors, distill evidence, and generate forecasts. Evaluated via context ablations pairing state-of-the-art deep research (DR) agents with forecasting methods.

Result: High-quality context improves forecasting, but existing DR agents recover <5% of ground-truth evidence, cite distractors in >80% of cases, and retrieved context causes forecasters to perform worse than using no context.

Conclusion: There is a need for foresight-driven agents that can independently search for the right context to predict the future.

Abstract: Time series forecasting in real-world settings often depends not only on historical observations, but also on external context that must be actively discovered from noisy, heterogeneous information sources. Yet existing context-aided forecasting benchmarks typically assume that the supporting context is already provided, leaving open whether agents can identify it on their own. Therefore, we introduce Dr-CiK, a benchmark for evaluating whether agents can retrieve forecasting-relevant supporting context from a document corpus, filter out distractors, distill the retrieved context into forecast-useful evidence, and generate forecasts supported by that evidence. Through context ablations and evaluations of state-of-the-art deep research and forecasting methods paired together, we show that high-quality context substantially improves forecasting performance in Dr-CiK. However, most existing DR agents recover only a small fraction of the ground-truth supporting evidence (usually <5%), are frequently misled by distractors (>80% distractor citations), and can cause forecasters to perform worse with retrieved context than without context. Our results motivate research on foresight-driven agents that search for the right context to predict the future.

</details>


### [41] [Reasoning Matters: Mitigate Hallucination in Multimodal Large Reasoning Models via Reasoning-Conditioned Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27906)
*Jiawei Kong,Hao Fang,Shunxiang Liao,Jinyu Li,Bin Chen,Hao Wu,Shu-Tao Xia,Min Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RC-DPO extends DPO by explicitly optimizing Chain-of-Thought reasoning as a condition for answer generation, using MCTS-based positive sampling and attention-guided negative pruning, to reduce hallucinations in multimodal large reasoning models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing DPO for multimodal reasoning treats CoT and final answer as a monolithic output, primarily learning answer-level preference while leaving CoT-level supervision insufficiently exploited, leading to persistent hallucinations.

Method: Proposes Reasoning-Conditioned Direct Preference Optimization (RC-DPO) which models CoT as a condition for answer generation and contrasts preferences under different CoT conditions. Introduces MCTS to generate visually grounded, logically consistent positive CoTs and attention-guided token pruning to construct negative CoTs.

Result: Extensive experiments across various models and benchmarks show RC-DPO effectively mitigates hallucinations and improves reliability of multimodal reasoning.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling CoT-oriented preference in DPO (RC-DPO) successfully addresses the insufficient CoT supervision in prior methods, reducing hallucinations in multimodal large reasoning models.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Reasoning Models introduce the reasoning paradigm, demonstrating strong capabilities on complex vision-language tasks. However, they still suffer from severe hallucinations. Existing training-based methods typically mitigate hallucinations through response-level direct preference optimization (DPO), where the Chain-of-Thought (CoT) and the final answer are treated as a monolithic output and optimized jointly. We reveal that this formulation performs similarly to answer-only optimization, suggesting that it primarily learns answer-level preference, while leaving CoT-level supervision insufficiently exploited. To address this issue, we explicitly formulate a CoT-oriented preference term and derive Reasoning-Conditioned Direct Preference Optimization (RC-DPO), which models the CoT as a condition for answer generation and contrasts the preference for the same preferred answer under different CoT conditions, promoting answer-supportive reasoning chain alignment. To further improve optimization, we introduce a reasoning-enhanced preference data generation strategy that employs Monte Carlo Tree Search to discover visually grounded and logically consistent CoTs as positive samples, and attention-guided CoT token pruning to construct negative ones. Extensive experiments across various models and benchmarks show that RC-DPO effectively mitigates hallucinations and improves the reliability of the multimodal reasoning process.

</details>


### [42] [SuiChat-CN: Benchmarking Contextual Suicide Risk Assessment in Chinese Group Chats](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27911)
*Xiangyu Wang,Zhiwei Yu,Chengze Du,Dingchang Wang,Yuhan Ye,Fangyu Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SuiChat-CN is a Chinese Telegram group-chat benchmark with 13,312 contextual segments from 1,406 users for suicide risk assessment, showing that contextual information is crucial for reliable detection in multi-party instant messaging environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing computational suicide risk assessment focuses on post-based platforms (Twitter, Weibo), leaving instant messaging environments like Telegram underexplored. Group chats pose unique challenges (short, fragmented, multi-party, implicit expressions) that post-level analysis cannot address.

Method: Collected public Telegram group-chat data; constructed coherent conversational segments via signal-word extraction and bidirectional context expansion; annotated user risk levels using an expert-validated, LLM-assisted paradigm. Experiments conducted with PLMs and over 40 LLMs.

Result: Contextual information is essential for reliable risk assessment. Fine-tuning and partial-context evaluation reveal challenges for early detection in multi-party conversations. The dataset is not publicly released but available to accredited research institutions.

Conclusion: SuiChat-CN fills a gap in suicide risk assessment for instant messaging environments. Context matters critically, and early detection in multi-party chats remains challenging.

Abstract: Suicide is a critical global public health challenge, causing approximately 720,000 deaths each year and calling for timely, effective prevention strategies. Existing computational studies primarily focus on post-based social media platforms such as Twitter and Weibo, leaving instant messaging environments such as Telegram underexplored. Yet group chats pose distinct challenges: messages are short, fragmented, multi-party, and often rely on implicit or culturally specific expressions, making isolated post-level analysis insufficient. We introduce SuiChat-CN, a Chinese group-chat benchmark for contextual suicide risk assessment. We collect public Telegram group-chat data, construct coherent conversational segments through signal-word extraction and bidirectional context expansion, and annotate user risk levels with an expert-validated, LLM-assisted paradigm. SuiChat-CN contains 13,312 contextual segments from 1,406 users, covering 258,228 raw chat messages. Extensive experiments with PLMs and more than 40 LLMs demonstrate that contextual information is essential for reliable risk assessment, while fine-tuning and partial-context evaluation further reveal the challenges of early detection in multi-party conversations. Due to ethical and sensitivity concerns, the dataset is not publicly released but will be shared with accredited mental health and suicide-prevention research institutions upon reasonable request.

</details>


### [43] [Show, Don't TELL: Explainable AI-Generated Text Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27921)
*Aldan Creo,Suraj Ranganath*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TELL is an explainable AI-generated text detector that provides human-readable annotations ("tells") alongside numerical scores, achieving competitive performance (AUROC 0.927) with state-of-the-art while offering explainability features that outperform in concreteness, falsifiability, coherence, plausibility, and grounding (72.3% win-rate).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI-generated text detectors produce numeric scores without explanation, making them misaligned with real-world user needs (e.g., professors need interpretable rationale to contextualize detection within the writing's context and alleged authorship).

Method: TELL architecture with bake-in explainability; trained on custom SFT dataset of domain-specific authorship annotations; refined using GRPO with curriculum learning; outputs both numerical scores and native "tells" (annotations explaining detection basis).

Result: Achieves AUROC 0.927 (competitive with state-of-the-art); explanations achieve mean 72.3% win-rate on concreteness, falsifiability, coherence, plausibility, and grounding against human annotations.

Conclusion: Reframes AI text detection from a human-centric perspective, paving the way for a new family of detectors focusing on native explainability to empower user judgment.

Abstract: Research on AI-generated text detection has presented a number of approaches to discern human from AI prose, some of which achieving high in-distribution performance. However, real-world applicability has stalled because their outputs are misaligned with the needs of users, such as professors, who are presented with a numeric score that has no attached explanation. We tackle this issue with a novel architecture, TELL, that bakes explainability from the ground-up. While our system still offers a numerical score like other detectors for comparability, TELL takes a fundamentally different approach where we aim to show the user the "tells" by which the model believes a text is AI or human-written, to empower the user to decide who wrote a text using their own judgment and understanding of the context of the writing and its alleged author. We train TELL on a custom SFT dataset of domain-specific authorship annotations, and further refine the system using GRPO with curriculum learning to improve performance. We achieve competitive performance with state-of-the-art detectors (AUROC 0.927) while natively providing annotations that explain the basis for the detector's decision. We further evaluate the quality of our explanations using a dataset of human annotations and report a high (mean 72.3%) win-rate on annotation concreteness, falsifiability, coherence, plausibility and grounding, allowing users to critically think and decide for themselves. Our work thus reframes the problem of AI-generated text detection in a human-centric perspective and paves the way for a new family of detectors that focus on native explainability.

</details>


### [44] [Harness-Bench: Measuring Harness Effects across Models in Realistic Agent Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27922)
*Yilun Yao,Xinyu Tan,Chao-Hsuan Liu,Yaoming Li,Zhengyang Wang,Wenhan Yu,Zhewen Tan,Yuxuan Tian,Guangxiang Zhao,Lin Sun,Xiangzheng Zhang,Tong Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Harness-Bench is a diagnostic benchmark with 106 sandboxed offline tasks that evaluates how different harness configurations (system layers managing context, tools, state, etc.) affect LLM agent performance across multiple models. Analysis of 5,194 trajectories reveals substantial variation in completion, quality, and failure modes depending on model-harness pairings, showing agent capability should be attributed at the configuration level rather than base model alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks abstract away execution, compare complete agent systems, or hold the harness fixed, making it difficult to study how the execution layer (context management, tools, state, constraints, permissions, tracing, recovery) independently affects agent performance in realistic workflows.

Method: The authors constructed Harness-Bench containing 106 sandboxed offline tasks derived from practical agent-use patterns, manually reviewed for realism, solvability, oracle-checkability, and integrity. They evaluated representative harness configurations across multiple model backends under shared task environments, budgets, and evaluation protocols while preserving each harness's native execution behavior. Each run recorded final artifacts, execution traces, usage statistics, and validator outputs.

Result: Across 5,194 execution trajectories, substantial variation was observed in completion rates, process quality, efficiency, and failure behavior across different model-harness pairings. Recurring execution-alignment failures were identified where plausible reasoning becomes decoupled from tool feedback, workspace state, evidence, or verifiable output contracts.

Conclusion: Agent capability should be reported at the model-harness configuration level rather than attributed to the base model alone. Harness-Bench provides a reproducible foundation for diagnosing and improving reliable, efficient, and auditable agent execution stacks.

Abstract: LLM agents are increasingly deployed as executable systems that use tools, modify workspaces, and produce concrete artifacts. In such workflows, performance depends not only on the base model, but also on the harness: the system layer that manages context, tools, state, constraints, permissions, tracing, and recovery. However, existing benchmarks typically abstract away execution, compare complete agent systems, or hold the harness fixed, making execution-layer variation difficult to study. We introduce Harness-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark for evaluating configuration-level harness effects in realistic agent workflows. Harness-Bench evaluates representative harness configurations across multiple model backends under shared task environments, budgets, and evaluation protocols, while preserving each harness's native execution behavior. The benchmark contains 106 sandboxed offline tasks constructed from practical agent-use patterns and manually reviewed for realism, solvability, oracle-checkability, and integrity. Each run records final artifacts, execution traces, usage statistics, and validator outputs, enabling analysis beyond final completion. Across 5,194 execution trajectories, we observe substantial variation in completion, process quality, efficiency, and failure behavior across model-harness pairings. These results suggest that agent capability should be reported at the model-harness configuration level rather than attributed to the base model alone. Our analysis further identifies recurring execution-alignment failures, where plausible reasoning becomes decoupled from tool feedback, workspace state, evidence, or verifiable output contracts. Harness-Bench provides a reproducible foundation for diagnosing and improving reliable, efficient, and auditable agent execution stacks.

</details>


### [45] [DiagramRAG: A Lightweight Framework to Retrieve Scientific Diagram for Figure Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27931)
*Xinjiang Yu,Junyi Han,Zhuofan Chen,Chi Zhang,Xiangyu Fu,Jingyuan Tan,Zirui You,Yixiang Jian,Yu-Ping Wang,Chengliang Chai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DiagramRAG is a lightweight retrieval-augmented framework that completes rough scientific diagram sketches into publication-quality diagrams by retrieving semantically and topologically compatible reference diagrams using knowledge graph representations and sketch variant synthesis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Researchers often sketch rough scientific diagrams to express early semantic and topological intentions, but these sketches are incomplete and insufficient for producing publication-quality diagrams directly. Existing sketch-based methods mainly reconstruct sketches themselves, while text-driven diagram generation frameworks do not exploit the topological structure contained in sketches.

Method: DiagramRAG uses a retrieval-augmented approach: (1) represents diagrams as knowledge graphs, (2) synthesizes sketch variants at different simplification levels, (3) trains an embedding model to align sketches with compatible diagrams in a shared space, (4) retrieves reference diagrams that are both semantically relevant and topologically compatible, and (5) uses retrieved references to provide content, topology, and visual priors for diagram completion and rendering.

Result: DiagramRAG achieves F1-scores of 0.848 and 0.802 on DiagramBank and FigureBench, respectively, with a VLM-as-a-Judge score of 7.170 for generation quality and inference latency of 35.48 seconds per sample.

Conclusion: DiagramRAG effectively bridges rough sketches and publication-quality scientific diagrams by leveraging retrieval-augmented generation with structure-aware sketch-diagram alignment, outperforming existing methods in both accuracy and generation quality while maintaining practical inference efficiency.

Abstract: Scientific diagrams are essential for communicating complex methodologies in academic papers. A natural way for researchers to specify such diagrams is through rough sketches, where text labels, connectors, and spatial arrangements express early semantic and topological intentions. However, sketches are usually incomplete, making them insufficient for directly producing publication-quality diagrams. Existing sketch-based generation methods mainly reconstruct the sketch itself, while recent text-driven diagram generation frameworks rely on textual semantics and do not fully exploit the topological structure contained in sketches. In this paper, we introduce DiagramRAG, a lightweight retrieval-augmented framework for sketch-based scientific diagram completion. Given a user sketch, DiagramRAG retrieves reference diagrams that are both semantically relevant to the sketch content and topologically compatible with its structure, and uses them to guide downstream diagram generation. To enable efficient structure-aware retrieval, we represent diagrams as knowledge graphs, synthesize sketch variants at different simplification levels, and train an embedding model to align sketches with compatible diagrams in a shared space. The retrieved references further provide content, topology, and visual priors for completing and rendering the final diagram. Experiments show that DiagramRAG achieves F1-scores of 0.848 and 0.802 on DiagramBank and FigureBench, respectively, and improves generation quality with the best VLM-as-a-Judge score of 7.170, while reducing inference latency to 35.48 seconds per sample. Our code and data are available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/DiagramRAG-A262 and https://huggingface.co/datasets/anonymous-review-a262/DiagramSketch.

</details>


### [46] [Do Agents Think Deeper? A Mechanistic Investigation of Layer-Wise Dynamics in Sequential Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27935)
*Zhenyu Cui,Xiangzhong Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Large language models exhibit distinct depth-utilization patterns in autonomous agent settings (multi-turn planning, tool use) compared to standard single-turn tasks. As trajectories progress, models recruit deeper layers with stronger inter-layer dependencies, shift toward correction-dominant residual updates, and show a construction-refinement gap where semantic direction forms early but deep layers stabilize outputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether prior findings that LLMs use depth inefficiently in single-turn tasks hold in autonomous agent settings requiring multi-turn reasoning, planning, and tool use.

Method: Systematic layer-wise analysis of complete user-agent trajectories across three domains (Deep Research, Code Generation, Tabular Processing) using residual stream probes, causal layer-skipping interventions, and effective-depth measurements.

Result: Agentic reasoning shows distinct depth profiles from static tasks: progressive recruitment of deeper layers, stronger long-range inter-layer dependencies in later turns, shift toward correction-dominant residual updates, and a construction-refinement gap. The gap is pronounced in Qwen and Minimax, while GLM shows domain-dependent depth allocation.

Conclusion: Autonomous LLM agents allocate depth adaptively as reasoning complexity grows, providing mechanistic evidence that layer utilization in LLMs is task-dependent and not uniformly inefficient.

Abstract: Recent mechanistic studies suggest that large language models (LLMs) may utilize their depth inefficiently in standard single-turn tasks. Whether this still holds in autonomous agent settings, where models must perform multi-turn planning, tool use, and iterative state updates, remains unclear. We study this question through a systematic layer-wise analysis of complete user-agent trajectories spanning three domains: Deep Research, Code Generation, and Tabular Processing. Using residual stream probes, causal layer-skipping interventions, and effective-depth measurements, we show that agentic reasoning exhibits a distinct depth profile from static tasks. As trajectories unfold, models progressively recruit more and deeper layers, with stronger long-range inter-layer dependencies emerging in later turns. At the same time, residual updates become increasingly correction-dominant, indicating a shift from stable feature accumulation toward repeated recalibration. Effective-depth analysis further reveals a substantial construction-refinement gap: semantic direction often forms relatively early, while deep layers remain necessary for stabilizing final outputs. Across model families, this gap is pronounced in Qwen and Minimax, whereas GLM shows a more domain-dependent depth allocation pattern. These results provide mechanistic evidence that autonomous LLM agents allocate depth adaptively as reasoning complexity grows.

</details>


### [47] [From Talking to Singing: A New Challenge for Audio-Visual Deepfake Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27944)
*Ke Liu,Jiwei Wei,Wenyu Zhang,Shuchang Zhou,Ruikun Chai,Yutao Dai,Chaoning Zhang,Yang Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper addresses audio-visual deepfake detection in singing scenarios where rhythmic vocalization weakens cross-modal consistency. It introduces the SHDF dataset and a T-AVFD framework using text-guided facial authenticity learning and differential weighting to generalize across talking and singing, achieving robust improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing audio-visual deepfake detectors rely on cross-modal inconsistencies, but singing's rhythmic vocalization weakens this coupling and causes domain shift, degrading performance. There is also a lack of singing-specific deepfake benchmarks.

Method: Proposes T-AVFD framework with (1) a facial authenticity pattern learner that aligns facial features with multi-granularity text descriptions, and (2) a multi-modal differential weight learning module that preserves intrinsic audio-visual consistency and adaptively integrates it with authenticity patterns via differential weighting. Also constructs the SHDF dataset using rhythm-aware generative models.

Result: Consistent improvements over existing baselines on multiple talking head deepfake datasets and the new SHDF dataset, with strong robustness under diverse perturbations.

Conclusion: Text-guided learning of authenticity patterns combined with adaptive differential weighting of audio-visual consistency enables effective generalization across talking and singing deepfake detection scenarios.

Abstract: With rapid advances in audio-visual generative models, reliable forgery detection becomes increasingly critical. Existing methods for audio-visual deepfake detection typically rely on cross-modal inconsistencies. In singing, rhythmic vocalization weakens this coupling and introduces a nontrivial domain shift, substantially degrading detection performance. We construct the Singing Head DeepFake (SHDF) dataset using rhythm-aware generative models to fill the gap in singing benchmarks. To cope with cross-scenario domain shifts, we propose a Text-guided Audio-Visual Forgery Detection (T-AVFD) framework that generalizes across both talking and singing scenarios. T-AVFD comprises a facial authenticity pattern learner and a multi-modal differential weight learning module. The pattern learner aligns facial features with multi-granularity textual descriptions to learn generalizable authenticity patterns. The weight learning module preserves intrinsic audio-visual consistency and adaptively integrates it with authenticity patterns via differential weighting. Extensive experiments on multiple talking head deepfake datasets and SHDF show consistent improvements over existing baselines and strong robustness under diverse perturbations.

</details>


### [48] [The Shape of Overthinking: Backtracking Bursts in Long Reasoning Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27965)
*Navid Rezazadeh,Arash Gholami Davoodi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes backtracking dynamics in long reasoning traces (Qwen3-8B on AIME math problems), finding that early isolated repairs correlate with correct reasoning while persistent late backtracks indicate errors. It proposes a burst-aware selective early-exit policy that outperforms fixed-length filtering for distinguishing recoverable corrections from instability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the challenge of distinguishing useful self-correction from unproductive revision in long reasoning traces generated by reasoning models.

Method: Annotated segment-level backtrack severity on 6,000 Qwen3-8B AIME traces; analyzed event timing, normalized depth, and local burst structure; cross-corpus validation across model/domain pairs; proposed a prefix-causal selective early-exit policy with burst-aware filtering.

Result: Early isolated repair correlates with correct reasoning; incorrect traces show moderate-to-severe backtracks that persist and cluster late; burst-aware filtering at shallow/intermediate depths outperforms fixed length-based filtering using only prefix-available features.

Conclusion: Burst-aware control provides a deployable mechanism for separating recoverable repair from likely instability, with moderate length cutoffs remaining strong completed-trace baselines.

Abstract: Reasoning models often generate long traces in which useful self-correction and unproductive revision are hard to distinguish. We study this distinction through backtracking dynamics: local reconsideration, retraction, or re-derivation inside long-form reasoning traces. On 6{,}000 Qwen3-8B AIME traces, we annotate segment-level backtrack severity and analyze event timing, normalized depth, and local burst structure. We find that early isolated repair is often compatible with correct reasoning, whereas incorrect traces more often show moderate-to-severe backtracks that persist and cluster late. Cross-corpus checks show the same qualitative asymmetry across additional model/domain pairs. Filtering analyses instantiate the signal as a prefix-causal selective early-exit policy: at shallow and intermediate depths, burst-aware filtering outperforms fixed length-based filtering while using only prefix-available features. Moderate length cutoffs remain strong completed-trace baselines, but burst-aware control provides a deployable mechanism for separating recoverable repair from likely instability.

</details>


### [49] [Geometry of Human Perceptual Domains Emerges Transiently in LLM Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27970)
*Simardeep Singh,Paras Chopra*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs exhibit human-like perceptual geometric structure (color, pitch, emotion, taste) in their internal representations despite no perceptual training, with this structure emerging transiently in intermediate layers—weak in early layers, organized in middle layers, and attenuated in later layers—following domain- and model-specific trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work shows LLM internal representations can exhibit rich geometric structure in embedding space despite being trained only on text. This paper investigates whether such structure aligns with human perceptual organization across domains like color, pitch, emotion, and taste.

Method: The authors study layer-wise emergence of intrinsic geometrical structure corresponding to perceptual modalities within the residual streams of multiple open-weight transformer architectures, analyzing alignment with human perceptual baselines.

Result: Three key findings: (1) Layer-wise geometric structure emerges across multiple perceptual domains without direct perceptual supervision; (2) Perceptual domains show distinct emergence profiles with domain- and model-specific trajectories; (3) Emergence follows a consistent pattern—weak/diffuse in early layers, progressively organized in intermediate layers, attenuated in later layers.

Conclusion: Perceptual geometry arises transiently as part of the model's internal transformation pipeline, offering insights into how and where human-like perceptual geometry emerges in LLMs and providing a principled pathway for mechanistic analysis of internal representations.

Abstract: While large language models (LLMs) are trained purely on textual data, prior work has shown that their internal representations can exhibit rich geometric structure in embedding space. Building on this line of work, we investigate whether such structure is similar to human perceptual organisation across different domains (e.g., color, pitch, emotion, and taste). Specifically, we study the layer-wise emergence of intrinsic geometrical structure corresponding to perceptual modalities within the residual streams of multiple open-weight transformer architectures. Our results reveal three key findings. First, we observe the emergence of layer-wise geometric structure across multiple perceptual domains, despite the absence of any direct perceptual supervision during training. Second, these perceptual domains exhibit distinct emergence profiles, with both geometric structure and its alignment with human baselines following domain- and model-specific trajectories across depth. Third, this emergence follows a consistent representational trajectory: geometry is weak or diffuse in early layers, becomes progressively organised in intermediate layers, and is attenuated in later layers, suggesting that perceptual geometry arises transiently as part of the model's internal transformation pipeline. This provides new insight into how and where human-like perceptual geometry arises in LLMs, offering a principled pathway for mechanistic analysis of internal representations.

</details>


### [50] [STAB: Specification-driven Testing for Algorithmic Bottlenecks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27981)
*Soohan Lim,Joonghyuk Hahn,Hyundong Jin,Yo-Sub Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: STAB is a specification-driven pipeline that generates test cases exposing algorithmic bottlenecks by combining constraint-bound maximization and adversarial structure injection, improving detection rates from ~50-57% to ~72-73% across open and closed-source LLMs on CodeContests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for generating efficiency test cases focus on scaling input size or creating code-specific inputs, but fail to address the structural input conditions that drive algorithmic worst-case behavior.

Method: STAB uses a two-stage pipeline: (1) a constraint saturator that extracts constraints and resolves large admissible size assignments using rule-based saturation and CP-SAT optimization; (2) an adversarial scenario injector that retrieves implementation-level adversarial construction principles from a curated catalog using keyword matching and KNN. These are encoded into a structured generation specification for an LLM to synthesize a Python test case generator.

Result: On CodeContests, STAB raises the rate of generated test cases that expose algorithmic bottlenecks from 50.43% to 73.45% on average across open-source LLMs and from 57.45% to 71.85% on average across closed-source LLMs, with consistent gains across Python, Java, and C++.

Conclusion: STAB effectively generates test cases that expose algorithmic bottlenecks by leveraging problem specifications and adversarial construction principles, outperforming prior methods across multiple programming languages and models.

Abstract: Evaluating the efficiency of algorithmic code requires test cases that expose runtime bottlenecks. Previous methods generate efficiency test cases either by increasing input size or by generating code-specific inputs that make the given implementation run slowly. Consequently, they do not address the structural input conditions that drive the algorithmic worst case. We introduce STAB, a specification-driven pipeline that generates test cases that expose algorithmic bottlenecks from a natural-language problem specification alone. STAB separates the task into constraint-bound maximization and adversarial structure injection. (i) The constraint saturator extracts constraints and resolves large admissible size assignments using rule-based saturation and CP-SAT optimization over related variables. (ii) The adversarial scenario injector retrieves implementation-level adversarial construction principles from a curated scenario catalog using keyword matching and K-nearest neighbors (KNN). STAB encodes the problem specification, resolved boundary, and retrieved construction principles into a structured generation specification, from which the LLM synthesizes a Python test case generator. On CodeContests, STAB raises the rate of generated test cases that expose algorithmic bottlenecks from 50.43% to 73.45% on average across open-source LLMs and from 57.45% to 71.85% on average across closed-source LLMs, with consistent gains across Python, Java, and C++. Our code is available at https://github.com/suhanmen/STAB.

</details>


### [51] [AsyncTool: Evaluating the Asynchronous Function Calling Capability under Multi-Task Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27995)
*Kou Shi,Ziao Zhang,Shiting Huang,Avery Nie,Zhen Fang,Qiuchen Wang,Lin Chen,Huaian Chen,Zehui Chen,Feng Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes AsyncTool, a benchmark to evaluate LLM-based agents on asynchronous tool calling in multi-task settings with realistic tool response delays, revealing that current agents struggle with temporal coordination, task switching, and dependency tracking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of LLM-based tool-using agents overlook the temporal dimension (tool response latency) and are limited to single-task settings, failing to reflect real-world multi-task concurrent execution scenarios where agents must use idle time efficiently.

Method: AsyncTool constructs a multi-task tool-use environment with simulated tool response delays, uses a hybrid data evolution strategy to create diverse asynchronous multitasking datasets across multiple scenarios, and evaluates models at step, sub-task, and task levels with efficiency-oriented metrics.

Result: Delayed tool feedback causes significant performance degradation in current agents. Models with better task switching, dependency tracking, and state maintenance achieve stronger results. Key failure modes of existing tool-using agents are identified.

Conclusion: Asynchronous tool calling is a critical but underexplored capability; the findings provide practical insights for designing future systems with stronger temporal reasoning and task coordination abilities.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based agents have shown strong capabilities in using external tools to solve complex tasks. However, existing evaluations often overlook the temporal dimension of tool use, especially the impact of tool response latency, and are usually limited to single-task settings. In real-world applications, multiple tasks often need to be executed concurrently, and overall efficiency depends on whether an agent can use idle time while waiting for tool responses. We refer to this capability as asynchronous tool calling. To evaluate it, we propose AsyncTool, a benchmark for assessing LLM-based agents in interactive multi-task tool-use environments with delayed tool feedback. AsyncTool presents multiple heterogeneous tasks simultaneously and simulates realistic tool response latency during execution.
