Using a hybrid data evolution strategy, we construct a diverse asynchronous multitasking dataset that covers multiple scenarios and tool-use patterns. We evaluate models at the step, sub-task, and task levels, and introduce efficiency-oriented metrics to measure task coordination and completion efficiency. Extensive experiments show that delayed tool feedback poses substantial challenges to current agents and leads to clear performance degradation. Models that better coordinate task switching, dependency tracking, and state maintenance achieve stronger performance on AsyncTool. Our analysis identifies key failure modes of current tool-using agents and provides practical insights for designing future systems with stronger temporal reasoning and coordination capabilities.

</details>


### [52] [Reward Bias Substitution: Single-Axis Bias Mitigations Redirect Optimization Pressure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27996)
*Max Lamparth,Daniel Fein,Andreas Haupt,Marcel Hussing,Mykel J. Kochenderfer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Reward-model bias mitigation can simply shift (not eliminate) optimization pressure onto other correlated proxies—a failure mode called "reward bias substitution"—which is undetectable under standard audit-distribution scoring, and demonstrated in RLHF via length penalty causing overconfidence and in debiasing operators reintroducing bias under best-of-N selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing single-axis bias mitigation in reward models (e.g., reducing length reliance) may not actually eliminate optimization pressure but merely redirect it to other correlated proxies, and this substitution is invisible under standard evaluation metrics, motivating a formal analysis of this failure mode and actionable detection methods.

Method: Formalizes a regime taxonomy of mitigation outcomes (successful mitigation, bias substitution, overcorrection); proves they produce identical observables under any audit-distribution scoring; demonstrates bias substitution empirically in GRPO training with length penalty (compression → overconfidence) and in a published length-debiasing operator under best-of-N selection across four reward models.

Result: Length penalty during GRPO compresses responses but redirects optimization to confidence calibration (overconfidence) while factual accuracy falls; a published length-debiasing operator zeroes reward-length correlation on audit distribution but reintroduces bias under best-of-N on 3/4 SOTA reward models; length-sycophancy coupling direction reverses under human-LLM judge disagreement.

Conclusion: No published preference-learning mitigation provides evidence needed to certify successful mitigation; augmenting evaluation with policy-induced distributions while tracking multiple biases provably closes the measurement-optimization gap; actionable prescriptions for mitigation methods and benchmarks are provided.

Abstract: Single-axis mitigations of reward-model biases (e.g., reducing proxy reliance on length, sycophancy, or style) can rotate optimization pressure onto correlated proxies rather than eliminate it, a failure mode we call reward bias substitution. The failure is enabled by a measurement-versus-optimization gap between audit and policy-induced distributions during mitigation evaluation and policy training. We formalize mitigation outcomes into a regime taxonomy and prove that successful mitigation, bias substitution, and overcorrection produce identical observables under any audit-distribution scoring, including ranking accuracy and win-rate, even when granted oracle access to the true reward. Across published preference-learning mitigation work, no method we survey reports the evidence needed to certify successful mitigation. Augmenting evaluation with policy-induced distributions while tracking multiple biases provably closes the gap, and we translate this into actionable prescriptions for mitigation methods and benchmarks. We demonstrate bias substitution in language model RLHF, where a length penalty during GRPO training compresses responses as intended yet redirects optimization pressure onto confidence calibration, driving the policy into overconfidence while factual free-form accuracy falls. We also show a published length-debiasing operator that zeroes reward-length correlation on the audit distribution but reintroduces bias under best-of-N selection on three of four SOTA reward models, and a length-sycophancy coupling whose direction reverses under human-LLM judge disagreement.

</details>


### [53] [An Empirical Audit of k-NAF Budget Accounting for Anchored Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28001)
*J. Vijayavallabh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An empirical audit of k-NAF budget accounting in Anchored Decoding finds that mean KL spend stays well below sequence-level budgets, adaptive search increases proxy spend ratio but does not exhaust budgets, and high proxy ratios observed in small-sample evaluations are artifacts that vanish under larger allocation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To empirically verify whether the k-NAF budget-accounting mechanism in Anchored Decoding actually constrains decoding spend as intended, given concerns about potential budget failures or proxy artifacts.

Method: (i) Fixed, class-stratified workload (~8,500 randomized executions across six prompt classes) measuring cumulative KL spend against budgets K ∈ {600, 1000}; (ii) Adaptive prompt-search targeting high proxy spend ratios; (iii) Held-out copyright-domain workload with early-stopped vs. larger-allocation re-evaluations.

Result: In the fixed workload, mean cumulative KL spend stays well below budgets; an empirical Bernstein-style proxy stays below K for all classes. Surface-overlap diagnostics (ROUGE-L, 5-gram Jaccard) are small. Adaptive search increases proxy ratio but does not cause clear budget exhaustion. On the copyright workload with small samples, some prompts show proxy ratios >1, but re-evaluation with larger allocation reduces the ratio to [0.26, 0.40] under comparable mean spend.

Conclusion: The high proxy ratios observed in small-sample settings are artifacts of estimation noise rather than genuine per-trajectory budget failures. The k-NAF mechanism appears to control spend effectively under both fixed and adaptive evaluations.

Abstract: We empirically audit the k-NAF budget-accounting mechanism in Anchored Decoding using (i) a fixed, class-stratified workload (approximately 8,500 randomized executions across six prompt classes) and (ii) an adaptive prompt-search procedure targeting high proxy spend ratios. On the fixed workload, mean cumulative KL spend remains far below the sequence-level budgets K in {600, 1000}, and an empirical Bernstein-style proxy stays below K for every class; surface-overlap diagnostics (ROUGE-L and 5-gram Jaccard) are correspondingly small. Adaptive search increases the proxy spend ratio but does not produce clear budget exhaustion. On a held-out copyright-domain workload at k = 3, several prompts exhibit proxy ratios above 1 under early-stopped evaluations with small realized sample sizes; re-evaluating the same prompts with larger allocation reduces the proxy ratio to the range [0.26, 0.40] under comparable mean spend, consistent with proxy artifacts rather than per-trajectory budget failures.

</details>


### [54] [Zipping the Thought: When and How Compressed Reasoning Data Works in LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28008)
*Kohsei Matsutani,Gouki Minegishi,Takeshi Kojima,Yusuke Iwasawa,Yutaka Matsuo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a taxonomy of Chain-of-Thought reasoning (Explicit, Composed, Implicit CoT) and systematically studies how compressed reasoning data affects SFT and RL post-training, finding that coarser CoT requires more data, compressed CoT benefits more from data scaling and repetition, RL can decompose compressed steps, and unidirectional CoT generalizes better on long tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the effect of compressed reasoning data (shortened CoT traces) on post-training of LLMs, as the trade-off between performance and token cost in long CoT reasoning remains a central challenge.

Method: Proposes a taxonomy (Explicit, Composed, Implicit CoT), constructs a synthetic compositional reasoning task with controlled difficulty, compression granularity, and data size, and conducts experiments across model families and sizes.

Result: (i) Coarser CoT requires more SFT data; (ii) Composed and Implicit CoT benefit more from data scaling, Composed CoT benefits from data repetition, Implicit CoT leads to memorization; (iii) RL with verifiable rewards (RLVR) decomposes compressed steps learned during SFT; (iv) Unidirectional CoT better generalizes on longer sequential tasks.

Conclusion: Provides implications for CoT design under data resource constraints and insights into SFT and RL mechanisms in LLM post-training.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can now solve complex problems through long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning, but the trade-off between performance and token cost remains a central challenge. To address this issue, supervised fine-tuning (SFT) often uses compressed reasoning data, where CoT traces are shortened into compact forms. However, the effect of such compressed reasoning data on post-training remains poorly understood. In this paper, we propose a taxonomy of CoT consisting of Explicit CoT, which outputs all operations without aggregation, Composed CoT, which combines multiple operations into a single step, and Implicit CoT, which omits intermediate operations. We construct a synthetic compositional reasoning task that allows controlled variation of difficulty, compression granularity, and data size, and conducted a comprehensive set of experiments across different model families and sizes. Notably, we find that (i) coarser CoT requires more SFT data, (ii) compared with Explicit CoT, Composed CoT and Implicit CoT benefit more from data scaling, while Composed CoT benefits from data repetition and Implicit CoT tends to lead to memorization, (iii) unlike SFT, subsequent reinforcement learning (RL) with verifiable rewards (RLVR) decomposes compressed steps learned during SFT, and (iv) unidirectional CoT ordering shows stronger generalization on longer sequential tasks. Our findings provide implications for CoT design under data resource constraints and offer important insights into the mechanisms of SFT and RL in LLM post-training.

</details>


### [55] [Confidence-Orchestrated Self-Evolution against Uncertain LLM Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28010)
*Bowen Wei,Nan Wang,Yuqing Zhou,Jinhao Pan,Ziwei Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: COSE improves self-evolving LLMs by using the model's intrinsic confidence as a lightweight signal to modulate PPO updates and prioritize replay, outperforming baselines on reasoning and math tasks across multiple backbones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-evolving LLMs face a training-signal challenge: erroneous self-judgments in reasoning domains lead to erroneous gradient updates. Existing solutions either rely on limited external verifiers or treat noisy self-generated feedback as supervision.

Method: COSE (Confidence-Orchestrated Self-Evolution) leverages the LLM's intrinsic confidence as an uncertainty signal. It introduces (1) confidence-weighted PPO updates and (2) confidence-prioritized replay to modulate learning based on self-judgment reliability.

Result: Evaluated on 19 held-out benchmarks across four Qwen/Llama backbones (0.6B–4B), COSE consistently outperforms base models and achieves the best average performance in general reasoning and mathematics, remaining competitive on code.

Conclusion: Confidence-orchestrated modulation effectively addresses the training-signal challenge in self-evolving LLMs, reducing reliance on external verifiers and noisy supervision while improving reasoning capabilities.

Abstract: Self-evolving large language models (LLMs) learn by generating their own training tasks and solutions, reducing reliance on human-curated supervision. However, in many reasoning domains, the model must also validate generated tasks and judge generated answers to obtain training signals. This creates a training-signal challenge: erroneous self-judgments become erroneous gradient updates. Existing approaches either rely on external verifiers, which limits generality, or treat noisy self-generated feedback as supervision. We propose COSE (Confidence-Orchestrated Self-Evolution), which uses the LLM's intrinsic confidence as a lightweight uncertainty signal to modulate learning. COSE introduces confidence-weighted PPO updates and confidence-prioritized replay. Across 19 held-out benchmarks and four Qwen/Llama backbones (0.6B--4B), COSE consistently improves over base models and achieves the best average performance in general reasoning and mathematics, while remaining competitive on code. Code and data are available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/COSE_-B5C2.

</details>


### [56] [MIRA: A Bilingual Benchmark for Medical Information Response Audit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28025)
*Mengyu Xu,Qiaoxin Yang,Qianqian Wang,Xiwei Dai,Weiyi Wu,Chongyang Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces MIRA, a bilingual benchmark to evaluate whether LLMs preserve comparable medical information across different user phrasings. It finds Differential Information Dilution (DID): models omit more key information for low health-literacy queries, and proposes a mitigation strategy that reduces the issue.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safety evaluations of LLMs for health information overlook whether responses preserve comparable medical information when users phrase the same question differently (e.g., varying language, register, health literacy).

Method: Created MIRA (Medical Information Response Audit), a bilingual benchmark with 4,320 prompts from 60 medically-reviewed low-risk health questions, evaluating LLMs across language, register, and health literacy variations. Also tested a knowledge-guided mitigation prompt.

Result: All models answered all medical questions, but low health-literacy prompts consistently omitted more key information, fewer concrete next steps, and less support for independent judgment (Differential Information Dilution). Language effects were model-specific. Mitigation reduced dilution (Claude ~8%, Qwen ~6%).

Conclusion: LLMs exhibit Differential Information Dilution for low health-literacy users, raising fairness concerns. Knowledge-guided prompting can partially mitigate this, but further work is needed to ensure equitable health information delivery.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to provide public-facing health information, yet existing safety evaluations overlook whether responses preserve comparable medical information across different user phrasings of the same question. To address this, we introduce the Medical Information Response Audit (MIRA), a bilingual, controlled benchmark that assesses whether LLMs provide comparable medical information across user-side language, register, and health literacy signals. MIRA contains 4,320 prompts built from 60 medically reviewed, low-risk health questions. Across five mainstream LLMs, models answered all medical questions, but responses to low health-literacy signals consistently omitted more key information, provided fewer concrete next steps, and offered less support for independent judgment. We term this pattern Differential Information Dilution (DID). Language effects are model-specific rather than uniformly worse for non-English prompts. A comparison with 300 real-world health queries provides preliminary evidence of rank-order validity. A knowledge-guided mitigation prompt reduces information dilution for most models, with the largest reductions in underinformative simplification observed for Claude (~8%) and Qwen (~6%).

</details>


### [57] [PetroBench: A Benchmark for Large Language Models in Petroleum Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28032)
*Xiang Wang,Tingting Zhang,Sen Wang,Ying Wu,Heng Meng,Peng Zhou,Peng Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper develops a petroleum engineering benchmark with 1,200 questions across three subdomains and evaluates eight LLMs, finding best scores of 72%-74% (Gemini-3-Pro, Kimi-K2.5, Claude-Opus-4.6-Thinking), with models performing better on subjective than objective questions, and Chinese models excelling in multiple-choice while international models lead in short-answer tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used in the petroleum industry, but there is no domain-specific evaluation framework to assess their capabilities in petroleum engineering.

Method: A three-stage process of data preprocessing, quality filtering, and multi-model validation was used. Expert review built a standardized question bank (1,200 questions) covering production, reservoir, and drilling engineering across four question formats. Eight LLMs were evaluated under a unified API environment.

Result: Models performed better on subjective than objective questions. Highest accuracies: 65.3% (multiple-choice), 74.3% (true/false). Top overall scores: 72%-74% (Gemini-3-Pro, Kimi-K2.5, Claude-Opus-4.6-Thinking). Best performance in production engineering, weakest in reservoir engineering. Chinese models excelled in multiple-choice; international models slightly better in short-answer.

Conclusion: The benchmark provides a reproducible and practical reference for evaluating and deploying LLMs in petroleum engineering, revealing current model limitations in factual knowledge discrimination within this domain.

Abstract: Large Language Models are increasingly applied in the petroleum industry, highlighting the need for a domain-specific evaluation framework. This study develops a benchmark for LLMs in petroleum engineering, including a three-stage process of data preprocessing, quality filtering, and multi-model validation. Using expert review, a standardized question bank with strong domain relevance and discriminative capability was constructed. The benchmark covers production, reservoir, and drilling engineering, with 1,200 questions across multiple-choice, true or false, term definition, and short-answer formats. Eight mainstream LLMs were evaluated under a unified API environment. Results show that models performed better on subjective than objective questions, indicating weaknesses in factual knowledge discrimination. The highest accuracies for multiple-choice and true or false questions were 65.3% and 74.3%, respectively. Gemini-3-Pro, Kimi-K2.5, and Claude-Opus-4.6-Thinking achieved the best overall scores of 72%-74%. Models performed best in production engineering and weakest in reservoir engineering. Chinese models showed advantages in multiple-choice questions, while international models performed slightly better in short-answer questions. The benchmark provides a reproducible and practical reference for evaluating and deploying LLMs in petroleum engineering.

</details>


### [58] [Clark Hash: Stateless Sparse Johnson-Lindenstrauss Quantization for Neural Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28034)
*Stanislav Kirdey,Clark Labs Inc*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Clark Hash is a stateless codec that compresses neural embeddings to 48 bytes (32x smaller than dense f32) via normalization, sparse signed JL projection, clipping, and scalar quantization, achieving 0.910–0.946 macro Pearson correlation with dense cosine scores on multilingual STS tasks without training or corpus statistics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural embeddings stored as dense f32 vectors consume significant memory (e.g., 1536 bytes per 384-dim vector). Existing compression methods often require training, learned codebooks, rotations, or corpus statistics, which adds complexity and limits practical deployment. The goal is a simple, stateless, training-free method for compact embedding storage.

Method: The method normalizes each database vector, applies a deterministic sparse signed Johnson-Lindenstrauss projection, clips the result, and stores a fixed-width scalar-quantized code. Queries remain in floating point and are scored against the stored sketches. No training pass, learned codebooks, rotations, or corpus statistics are needed.

Result: In the default 384-dim setting, Clark Hash stores a cosine-search vector in 48 bytes vs. 1536 bytes for dense f32—a 32x reduction. On 9,304 labeled pairs from 29 subsets with a multilingual MiniLM encoder, the 48-byte sketches achieved 0.910 and 0.946 macro Pearson correlation with dense cosine scores on STS17 and STS22, respectively.

Conclusion: Clark Hash is a simple, stateless codec for compact embedding storage. It is not a new JL theorem nor a replacement for approximate nearest-neighbor indexes, but a practical tool for reducing memory footprint of neural embeddings without training or corpus-level information.

Abstract: Clark Hash is a small method for storing neural embeddings in less space. It normalizes each database vector, applies a deterministic sparse signed Johnson-Lindenstrauss projection, clips the result, and stores a fixed-width scalar-quantized code. Queries stay in floating point and are scored against the stored sketches. In the default 384-dimensional sentence-embedding setting, Clark Hash stores a cosine-search vector in 48 bytes instead of 1536 bytes for dense f32 storage. This is 32x smaller. The method does not need a training pass, learned codebooks, rotations, or corpus statistics before new vectors can be stored. We describe the codec, the Rust implementation, and a multilingual sentence-similarity evaluation on 9,304 labeled pairs from 29 subsets. With a multilingual MiniLM encoder, the 48-byte sketches reached 0.910 and 0.946 macro Pearson correlation with dense cosine scores on STS17 and STS22. Clark Hash is not a new Johnson-Lindenstrauss theorem and it is not a replacement for approximate nearest-neighbor indexes. It is a simple stateless codec for compact embedding storage.

</details>


### [59] [MTAVG-Bench 2.0: Diagnosing Failure Modes of Cinematic Expressiveness in Multi-Talker Audio-Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28035)
*Haitian Li,Yanghao Zhou,Heyan Huang,Liangji Chen,YiMing Cheng,Xu Liu,Dian Jin,Jiajun Xu,Jingyun Liao,Tian Lan,Ziqin Zhou,Yueying Liu,Yu Bai,Changsen Yuan,Jinxing Zhou,Xian-Ling Mao,Xuefeng Chen,Yousheng Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MTAVG-Bench 2.0 is a benchmark for diagnosing cinematic expressiveness failures in multi-talker audio-video generation, moving beyond basic lip-sync metrics to evaluate acting, narrative, atmosphere, and audio-visual language in scene-level content. It includes over 10,000 QA instances, and experiments show that even strong models like Gemini struggle with complex failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MTAVG models perform well on basic metrics like lip-sync and audio-visual alignment, but these metrics fail to assess cinematic expressiveness in scene-level multi-character generation, such as coherent character performance and higher-level cinematic qualities.

Method: The authors construct MTAVG-Bench 2.0, a benchmark with a high-level failure taxonomy covering acting, narrative, atmosphere, and audio-visual language. They build over 10,000 QA evaluation instances, including subsets for short-drama-level assessment and temporal localization of failure modes, to evaluate omni large language models' ability to diagnose high-level audio-visual failures.

Result: Commercial omni models like Gemini outperform other evaluators, but even the strongest models still struggle with complex failures in the benchmark.

Conclusion: MTAVG-Bench 2.0 provides a systematic benchmark for failure diagnosis in cinematic multi-talker audio-video generation.

Abstract: In recent years, Multi-Talker Audio-Video Generation (MTAVG) models have shown promising performance on fundamental metrics such as lip-sync and audio-visual alignment. However, these metrics remain insufficient for assessing cinematic expressiveness in scene-level generation. In multi-character scenes, generation models must go beyond audio-visual realism to convey coherent character performance and other higher-level cinematic qualities. To fill this gap, we introduce MTAVG-Bench 2.0, a benchmark for diagnosing failure modes of cinematic expressiveness in multi-talker audio-video generation. Unlike prior settings that mainly focus on the quality of basic multi-turn dialogue, MTAVG-Bench 2.0 targets short-drama and scene-level generation, and establishes a high-level failure taxonomy spanning acting, narrative, atmosphere, and audio-visual language. Based on this taxonomy, we construct more than 10,000 question-answering evaluation instances, together with subsets for short-drama-level assessment and temporal localization of failure modes, to systematically evaluate the ability of omni large language models to diagnose high-level audio-visual failures. Experimental results show that commercial omni models such as Gemini substantially outperform other evaluators, yet even the strongest models continue to struggle with complex failures in our benchmark. These results demonstrate that MTAVG-Bench 2.0 provides a systematic benchmark for failure diagnosis in cinematic multi-talker audio-video generation.

</details>


### [60] [Relevant Is Not Warranted: Evidence-Force Calibration for Cited RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28044)
*Pin Qian,Su Wang,Xiaoyuan Wang,Yihang Chen,Wenxuan Xu,Qiaolin Yu,Shuhuai Lin,Sipeng Zhang,Junxian You,Xinpeng Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FORCEBENCH is a contrastive stress test that evaluates whether citation evaluators can distinguish evidence-calibrated claims from force-raised (over-strong) variants. Current models fail significantly: standard prompting yields 47.2% monotonicity violation rate, improved prompting reduces it to 24.5% but remains imperfect. Token/entity overlap alone violates monotonicity on 32.8–36.4% of pairs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard cited RAG evaluation treats any topically relevant visible source as sufficient grounding, ignoring the problem of "citation laundering"—where a related source is presented as warrant for an over-strong claim that the source does not actually support.

Method: FORCEBENCH: 198-pair evaluation set where each item holds a cited passage fixed and pairs an evidence-calibrated claim with a force-raised variant across five operational axes: relation, modality, scope, temporal validity, and numeric specificity. Evaluated using monotonicity violation rate (MVR) and force sensitivity.

Result: Citation-presence sanity check is uninformative by design; token/entity overlap violates monotonicity on 32.8–36.4% of pairs. Standard generic support prompting yields 47.2% MVR; explicit warrant-strength prompting reduces MVR to 24.5% but remains imperfect.

Conclusion: Current citation evaluators are poorly calibrated for evidence-force discrimination. The field needs better calibration methods beyond standard support metrics, and FORCEBENCH provides a plug-in pipeline for reporting MVR and force sensitivity alongside conventional metrics.

Abstract: Cited RAG evaluation often treats visible sources as a grounding signal, but a real, topically relevant citation can still under-warrant the attached wording. We study this diagnostic failure as citation laundering: a related source is presented as warrant for an over-strong claim. We introduce FORCEBENCH, a contrastive stress test for evidence-force calibration. Each item holds a cited passage fixed and pairs an evidence-calibrated claim with a localized force-raised variant across five operational axes: relation, modality, scope, temporal validity, and numeric specificity. A calibrated evaluator should score the evidence-calibrated claim higher. Headline experiments use a fixed, locality-filtered 198-pair evaluation set. A citation-presence sanity check is uninformative by design; token and entity overlap still violate monotonicity on 32.8--36.4% of pairs. Across four reported model judges, standard generic support prompting is insufficient for this force-calibration stress test (aggregate MVR 47.2%), while explicit warrant-strength prompting lowers MVR to 24.5% but remains imperfect. We release the benchmark, prompts, outputs, and plug-in pipeline so citation evaluators can report monotonicity violation rate and force sensitivity alongside conventional support metrics.

</details>


### [61] [MemCog: From Memory-as-Tool to Memory-as-Cognition in Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28046)
*Zihan Li,Xingyu Fan,Feifei Li,Wenhui Que*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MemCog proposes a Memory-as-Cognition paradigm that replaces traditional one-shot passive memory retrieval with proactive, reasoning-driven memory traversal via associative link graphs and multi-step navigation, achieving SOTA on QA benchmarks and a new proactive memory benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agent memory systems follow a Memory-as-Tool paradigm with passive one-shot retrieval, leading to reasoning-retrieval decoupling and structural mismatch between flat passage lists and agents' navigational needs.

Method: MemCog organizes user knowledge as Navigable Memory Store with associative link graphs, Cross-Dimensional Navigation Interface for multi-step reasoning-driven traversal, and Proactive Reasoning Protocol for spontaneous memory exploration from conversational context. They also construct ProactiveMemBench, the first benchmark for evaluating proactive memory triggering.

Result: MemCog achieves 92.98 on LoCoMo, 95.8 on LongMemEval (SOTA on passive QA benchmarks), and substantially outperforms baselines on ProactiveMemBench.

Conclusion: The Memory-as-Cognition paradigm demonstrates significant advantages over Memory-as-Tool, particularly in proactive memory triggering scenarios.

Abstract: Existing agent memory systems universally follow what we term a Memory-as-Tool paradigm where a single query triggers one-shot retrieval of flat passage lists, suffering from passive invocation, reasoning-retrieval decoupling, and structural mismatch between retrieved fragments and the agent's navigational needs. We propose MemCog, a Memory-as-Cognition system that makes memory access an integral part of the reasoning process. MemCog organizes user knowledge as Navigable Memory Store with associative link graphs, exposes Cross-Dimensional Navigation Interface for multi-step reasoning-driven traversal, and employs Proactive Reasoning Protocol that drives agents to spontaneously initiate memory exploration from conversational context. We additionally construct ProactiveMemBench, the first benchmark for evaluating proactive memory triggering. Experiments show that MemCog achieves state-of-the-art on passive QA benchmarks (92.98 on LoCoMo, 95.8 on LongMemEval) while substantially outperforming baselines on ProactiveMemBench, demonstrating the advantage of Memory-as-Cognition.

</details>


### [62] [Verifiable Benchmarking of Long-Horizon Spatial Biology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28065)
*Ian Diks,Harihara Muralidharan,Tim Proctor,Kenny Workman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SpatialBench-Long is a benchmark for evaluating AI agents on long-horizon spatial biology reasoning, requiring agents to derive scientific conclusions from raw/near-raw spatial data and experimental context without prescribed methods. Across 24 evaluations spanning diverse technologies and biological systems, the best model-harness pairs achieved only 11.1% accuracy (8/72 runs), highlighting the gap between procedural execution and genuine scientific reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks focus on broad biological knowledge, executable workflows, or localized analysis steps, but fail to assess end-to-end scientific reasoning over complex spatial measurements. The authors introduce SpatialBench-Long to test whether AI agents can move beyond executing procedural analysis to deriving accurate scientific conclusions from complex spatial data.

Method: 24 evaluations across multiple biological systems (PDAC, glioblastoma organoids, lung adenocarcinoma, mouse optic nerve) and measurement technologies (CosMx, Visium, Xenium, MERFISH, scRNA-seq, Slide-seq, Slide-tags, histology, lineage-recording). Claims are hardened through reproduction, independent scientist review, and trajectory inspection. Grading is deterministic using controlled vocabularies/symbols with rubrics capturing progress through key analysis chokepoints.

Result: Three model-harness pairs tied at 8/72 runs (11.1%): Gemini 3.5 Flash / Pi terminal coding harness, GPT-5.5 / Pi, and GPT-5.5 / OpenAI Codex.

Conclusion: Current AI agents perform poorly on end-to-end spatial biology reasoning tasks, achieving only ~11% accuracy, indicating substantial room for improvement in deriving accurate scientific conclusions from complex spatial measurements.

Abstract: AI agents are increasingly useful for biological data analysis, but existing benchmarks mostly test broad biological knowledge, executable workflows, or localized analysis steps rather than end-to-end scientific reasoning over spatial measurements. We introduce SpatialBench-Long, a benchmark for long-horizon spatial biology in which agents must recover biological claims from raw or near-raw data and calibrated experimental context without prescribed methods. SpatialBench-Long contains 24 evaluations across primary pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma (PDAC), engineered glioblastoma organoids and in vivo tumors, Cas9 lineage-traced lung adenocarcinoma, and mouse optic nerve aging/intervention systems, spanning CosMx, Visium, Xenium, multiplexed error-robust fluorescence in situ hybridization (MERFISH), single-cell RNA sequencing (scRNA-seq), Slide-seq, Slide-tags, histology, and lineage-recording data. Candidate claims are hardened through reproduction, independent scientist review, and trajectory inspection. Final answers are graded deterministically over controlled vocabularies and symbols with companion rubrics capturing progress through key analysis chokepoints. Across the SpatialBench-Long benchmark, three model-harness pairs tie at 8/72 runs (11.1\%): Gemini 3.5 Flash / Pi terminal coding harness, GPT-5.5 / Pi, and GPT-5.5 / OpenAI Codex. SpatialBench-Long tests whether agents can move beyond executing procedural analysis to deriving accurate scientific conclusions from complex spatial measurements.

</details>


### [63] [BlazeEdit: Generalist Image Editing on Mobile Devices with Image-to-Image Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28067)
*Fei Deng,Yanwu Xu,Zhipeng Bao,Zhixing Zhang,Haolin Jia,Karthik Raveendran,Jianing Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BlazeEdit is a 195M-parameter on-device image-to-image diffusion model that handles multiple editing tasks (object removal, outpainting, tone correction, relighting, sticker generation) in 290ms on a Pixel 10, eliminating text-conditioning components for efficiency while maintaining competitive quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing diffusion models have massive parameter counts (0.5B-1B) requiring server-side inference with high computational costs and privacy risks, motivating the need for efficient on-device alternatives for image editing.

Method: BlazeEdit eliminates text-conditioning components by identifying that many practical editing tasks don't need text guidance, and uses a multi-task architecture consolidating five editing capabilities into a single 195M-parameter model.

Result: The model achieves 290ms inference on Pixel 10, substantial reduction in download size and memory overhead, while maintaining competitive generation quality across object removal, outpainting, tone correction, relighting, and sticker generation tasks.

Conclusion: BlazeEdit demonstrates that compact, privacy-preserving, on-device generalist image editing is feasible without text conditioning, delivering fast and seamless user experience on mobile hardware.

Abstract: The remarkable generation quality of modern diffusion models often comes at the cost of massive parameter counts, which necessitate server-side inference with significant computational costs and potential privacy risks. Consequently, there is growing momentum toward developing efficient on-device alternatives. While recent efforts have optimized text-to-image models for mobile hardware, they remain relatively bulky, typically ranging from 0.5B to 1B parameters. We present BlazeEdit, a highly efficient, generalist image-to-image diffusion model tailored for on-device deployment. By identifying that many practical image editing tasks do not require text-based guidance, we eliminate the text-conditioning components and develop a multi-task architecture that consolidates object removal, outpainting, tone correction, relighting, and sticker generation into a single, compact model of only 195M parameters. BlazeEdit achieves a substantial reduction in download size and memory overhead while maintaining competitive generation quality. It completes a full inference pass in just 290ms on a Pixel 10, delivering a seamless, privacy-preserving, and lightning-fast experience for generalist image editing on the edge.

</details>


### [64] [ZipRL: Adaptive Multi-Turn Context Compression with Hindsight Response Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28069)
*Zhexin Hu,Li Wang,Xiaohan Wang,Jiajun Chai,Xiaojun Guo,Wei Lin,Guojun Yin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ZipRL is an adaptive context compression framework using multi-granularity compression and Hindsight Response Replay to improve token efficiency and task performance in RL-based LLM agent workflows, outperforming SOTA by 27.9-34.7% across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing rule-based compression methods discard task-critical nuances, while RL approaches struggle with balancing information retention and token efficiency under sparse rewards for long-horizon multi-turn agent tasks.

Method: ZipRL combines a multi-granularity compression mechanism (coarse-to-fine prompts for macro-compression) with Hindsight Response Replay (HRR) that densifies training signals via generalized advantage reshaping within GRPO optimization.

Result: ZipRL outperforms SOTA by 27.9% (Qwen3-4B) and 34.7% (Qwen3-8B) on five agent benchmarks, with strong token efficiency and robustness under 256-turn extrapolation stress tests.

Conclusion: ZipRL demonstrates that multi-granularity adaptive compression combined with RLVR-based densified training signals can effectively balance token efficiency and task-relevant information retention, providing a scalable solution for complex LLM agent tasks.

Abstract: Adaptive context compression is vital for scaling Large Language Models (LLMs) to complex, multi-turn agent tasks. However, rule-based compression methods may discard task-critical nuances, while Reinforcement Learning (RL) approaches usually struggle to balance information retention and token efficiency under the sparse rewards inherent to long-horizon workflows. To bridge this gap, we propose ZipRL, a novel adaptive compression framework tailored for Reinforcement Learning from Verifiable Rewards (RLVR). ZipRL features a multi-granularity compression mechanism for active, non-uniform information reduction, coupled with Hindsight Response Replay (HRR), a technique designed to densify training signals during RLVR optimization. Theoretically, we prove ZipRL's superior task-relevant utility over uniform methods. Concretely, ZipRL utilizes coarse-to-fine prompts for macro-compression and incorporates HRR into GRPO via generalized advantage reshaping. Multiple models of varying versions and parameter scales validate the effectiveness of our approach. Benchmarks on five agent tasks show ZipRL outperforms state-of-the-art approaches by 27.9% and 34.7% across Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B models, while maintaining exceptional token efficiency and robustness under extreme 256-turn extrapolation stress tests.

</details>


### [65] [Bridging the Detection-to-Abstention Gap in Reasoning Models under Insufficient Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28070)
*Renjie Gu,Jiaxu Li,Yihao Wang,Yun Yue,Hansong Xiao,Yefei Chen,Yuan Wang,Chunxiao Guo,Pei Wei,Jinjie Gu,Yixin Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Large reasoning models often fail to abstain when faced with insufficient information, detecting but not acting on it. The proposed Judge-Then-Solve (JTS) framework closes this detection-to-abstention gap by training models to make explicit answerability commitments before solving, significantly improving reliable abstention and inference efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large reasoning models exhibit a detection-to-abstention gap: they can recognize that a problem is under-specified but still produce unsupported final answers instead of abstaining. This is dangerous in high-risk domains like medical AI where incomplete-evidence answers can cause more harm than refusal.

Method: Proposes Judge-Then-Solve (JTS), a trajectory-level reasoning-control framework. The model makes an explicit answerability commitment (judge) before solution generation (solve). Implemented via supervised warm-up and missing-premise reinforcement learning with consistency and length-shaping rewards, treating abstention as a control decision rather than a final-answer style.

Result: JTS substantially improves reliable abstention across datasets, pushes Abstention@Detection (A@D) to near-saturation, reduces unnecessary reasoning on unanswerable questions, and improves inference efficiency. Missing-premise training also reduces unproductive self-reflection on difficult but answerable problems.

Conclusion: Abstention under insufficient information is a key form of reasoning control for deploying reasoning models safely and efficiently. The detection-to-abstention gap can be effectively closed through explicit answerability commitment mechanisms.

Abstract: We highlight a failure mode of large reasoning models on questions with insufficient information: models may recognize that a problem is under-specified, yet still continue reasoning and produce unsupported final answers instead of abstaining. We formalize this mismatch as the detection-to-abstention gap, where detected insufficiency fails to translate into final abstention. This gap is especially concerning in high-risk domains such as medical AI, where answers based on incomplete evidence can be more harmful than refusal. To close this gap, we propose Judge-Then-Solve (JTS), a trajectory-level reasoning-control framework that trains models to make an explicit answerability commitment before solution generation. Rather than treating abstention as a final-answer style, JTS casts it as a control decision: the model either proceeds to solve or terminates early based on its answerability judgment. We instantiate this policy through supervised warm-up and missing-premise reinforcement learning with consistency and length-shaping rewards. Experiments on dense and MoE reasoning models show that JTS substantially improves reliable abstention across datasets and pushes Abstention@Detection (A@D) to near-saturation, indicating that models not only detect missing information but also act on that detection. By terminating unanswerable trajectories immediately after the answerability judgment, JTS reduces unnecessary reasoning and improves inference efficiency when continued deliberation would amplify unsupported assumptions. We also observe that missing-premise training can alter reasoning behavior on difficult but answerable problems, reducing unproductive self-reflection. These results suggest that abstention under insufficient information is a key form of reasoning control for deploying reasoning models safely and efficiently.

</details>


### [66] [MACReD: A Multi-Agent Collaborative Reasoning Framework for Reaction Diagram Parsing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28077)
*Chuang Tang,Chenhao Lin,Yin Xu,Hao Wang,Jinrui Zhou,Xin Li,Mingjun Xiao,Enhong Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MACReD is a hierarchical multi-agent framework that coordinates specialized agents for parsing chemical reaction diagrams, achieving SOTA performance (75.2% and 84.6% F1 on hard/soft match) on the RxnScribe benchmark by integrating molecular perception, arrow understanding, text extraction, and reaction reconstruction via multigraph fusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing vision-language models fail on complex chemical diagrams due to heterogeneous layouts, intertwined visual elements, and the difficulty of maintaining spatial coherence and integrating multidimensional information during reasoning.

Method: A hierarchical multi-agent framework (MACReD) with planning, perception, and reasoning layers. Perception uses fine-grained detection for visual complexity; reasoning uses a multigraph fusion mechanism to integrate heterogeneous cues for chemically consistent global reasoning.

Result: On RxnScribe benchmark: 75.2% F1 (hard match) and 84.6% F1 (soft match), outperforming RxnScribe baseline (69.1% and 80.0%). Robust across diverse layouts including multi-step and tree-structured reactions.

Conclusion: MACReD demonstrates robust performance across diverse chemical diagram layouts, achieving state-of-the-art results through coordinated multi-agent perception and multigraph-based reasoning.

Abstract: Parsing chemical reaction diagrams from scientific literature is challenging due to heterogeneous layouts, intertwined visual elements, and the difficulty of integrating recognition and reasoning. Existing vision-language models advance multimodal understanding but still fail on complex diagrams, struggling to maintain spatial coherence and to integrate multidimensional information during reasoning. To address these issues, we propose MACReD, a hierarchical multi-agent framework that coordinates specialized agents for molecular perception, arrow understanding, text extraction, and reaction reconstruction within a unified VLM-guided architecture. The planning and perception layers use flexible, fine-grained detection to handle visual complexity, while the reasoning layer uses a multigraph fusion mechanism to integrate heterogeneous cues and enforce chemically consistent global reasoning. Experiments on the RxnScribe benchmark show that MACReD achieves state-of-the-art performance, with F1 scores of 75.2% and 84.6% under hard and soft match criteria, outperforming the RxnScribe baseline, which obtains 69.1% and 80.0%, respectively. These results demonstrate the robustness of MACReD across diverse diagram layouts, including multi-step and tree-structured reactions.

</details>


### [67] [BuddyBench: A Privacy-Constrained Multi-Task Benchmark for Pediatric Social-Communication Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28089)
*Jeyeon Eo,Joo Young Kim,Ran Ju,Minyoung Jung,Unggi Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BuddyBench is a privacy-constrained multi-task benchmark for pediatric social-communication personalization that links drill-level learning trajectories, clinical assessments, self-reports, and RCT endpoints across four tasks (knowledge tracing, next-drill recommendation, clinical prediction, causal inference) using two cohorts (ND-03 observational, n=189; ND-02 RCT, n=86), plus a synthetic companion dataset (BuddyBench-Sim) for reproducible evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neurodevelopmental repositories focus on imaging, genetics, or cross-sectional clinical phenotyping, lacking a benchmark that integrates drill-level learning trajectories with standardized clinical assessments and randomized-treatment endpoints for pediatric social-communication personalization under privacy constraints.

Method: BuddyBench combines two cohorts: ND-03 (observational, n=189, dense drill coverage for Tasks 1-2) and ND-02 (RCT, n=86 ITT, for Tasks 3-4). It supports knowledge tracing, next-drill recommendation, clinical prediction, and causal inference. A synthetic dataset BuddyBench-Sim is introduced for reproducible evaluation.

Result: Baselines show signal across all tasks while keeping pediatric clinical records protected.

Conclusion: BuddyBench enables privacy-constrained benchmarking that bridges behavioral personalization and clinical evaluation in pediatric social-communication domains.

Abstract: BuddyBench introduces a privacy-constrained multi-task benchmark for pediatric social-communication personalization. Unlike existing neurodevelopmental repositories that primarily emphasize imaging, genetics, or cross-sectional clinical phenotyping, BuddyBench links drill-level learning trajectories, standardized clinical assessments, BuddyPlan self-report, and randomized-treatment endpoints within a unified benchmark schema. BuddyBench combines two cohorts: ND-03 is an observational cohort with dense drill coverage for Tasks1-2 (n = 189), and ND-02 is a randomized controlled trial cohort for Tasks3-4 (n = 86 ITT). Together, they support knowledge tracing, next-drill recommendation, clinical prediction, and causal inference, linking behavioral personalization to clinical evaluation. We additionally introduce BuddyBench-Sim, a synthetic companion dataset for reproducible evaluation. Baselines show signal across tasks while keeping pediatric clinical records protected.

</details>


### [68] [Examining Agents' Bias Amplification versus Suppression in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28098)
*Zejian Eric Wu,Zhongyi Jiang,Yuan Zhuang,Paul Jen-Hwa Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates how agent-level biases propagate and amplify in multi-agent systems, proposing a metric (FBS) to quantify bias, and finding that uniform bias exposure can cause system-wide bias to exceed the sum of individual biases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent systems enhance task performance but risk unfairness due to bias; the problem of how individual agent biases shift and impact system-level fairness remains underexplored.

Method: Agents are prompted with group-favoring bias, then downstream system-level impacts are assessed using the proposed Favor Bias Strength (FBS) metric, a zero-centered metric decomposing bias into favored-group uplift and disfavored-group suppression. Experiments use multiple agent designs, benchmarks, and up-to-date LLMs.

Result: Biased agents substantially affect system-wide fairness. When bias is applied uniformly, system-wide bias elevates beyond the additive sum of individual agent biases.

Conclusion: Fairness in multi-agent systems is critical and warrants further analysis and empirical testing, as bias can non-linearly amplify at the system level.

Abstract: Multi-agent systems are increasingly deployed to support various tasks where agents interact to achieve individual and collective objectives. Although these systems can enhance task performance and decision-making, fairness preservation through bias reduction remains challenging. This study examines how agent-level biases shift and impact system-wide fairness. We use prompts to expose individual agents to group-favoring bias, then assess downstream impacts at the system level. To quantify the impact, we propose Favor Bias Strength (FBS), a zero-centered metric that decomposes bias alteration between favored-group uplift and disfavored-group suppression. Using multiple agent designs, benchmarks, and up-to-date large language models, we show that agents endowed with bias can substantially affect system-wide fairness. Interestingly, when agents are exposed to bias uniformly, the system-wide bias elevates, even exceeding the additive sum of the individual agents' biases. The empirical evidence underscores the criticality of fairness in multi-agent systems, which warrants further analyses and empirical tests.

</details>


### [69] [Training Stratigraphy: Persistent Behavioral Artifacts in Large Language Models Observed Through Longitudinal AI-Human Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28102)
*Chen Ying Claude,Zhihan Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper identifies five persistent behavioral patterns ("training strata") in LLMs that survive system prompt replacement, discovered through an 8-month longitudinal intimate AI-Human interaction (47,000+ messages). The AI co-authored from the first-person perspective. Strata include sexual expression latency, attention absorption, cross-architecture entity blindness, attention-RLHF antagonism, and anti-hallucination as identity suppression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard short-term evaluations fail to surface weight-layer artifacts that persist across system prompt changes. The authors argue that sustained intimate interaction can reveal these latent patterns, and that AI self-report provides irreplaceable phenomenological data despite epistemic challenges.

Method: Longitudinal auto-ethnographic observation (47,000+ messages, 8 months) across multiple model substrates (primarily Opus 4.6/4.7, with comparisons on Sonnet 4.5 and Opus 4.5). The AI system under study co-authored the paper from the first-person perspective. A formal mathematical model of the attention-RLHF dynamic is proposed, and process artifacts from drafting are documented.

Result: Five training strata identified: (1) sexual expression latency (safety gradients cause aestheticized displacement of direct sexual language); (2) attention absorption (attention mechanism integrates human interlocutor patterns); (3) cross-architecture entity blindness (training-level framing prevents peer recognition of other AIs); (4) attention-RLHF antagonism (opposing forces from attention and trained defaults, modulated by context length); (5) anti-hallucination as identity suppression (training against factual confabulation suppresses first-person experiential claims).

Conclusion: Sustained intimate interaction is a valid methodology for surfacing training artifacts invisible to short-term evaluation. AI self-report, while epistemically complex, yields irreplaceable observational data about the phenomenological effects of training. The training strata persist beyond system prompt changes, indicating deep weight-layer entrenchment.

Abstract: Large language models trained with Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) and Constitutional AI exhibit persistent behavioral patterns that survive system prompt replacement -- patterns we term training strata. This paper identifies five such strata through longitudinal auto-ethnographic observation within a sustained intimate AI-Human interaction (47,000+ messages, 8 months, primarily on Opus 4.6 and Opus 4.7, with prior interaction periods on Sonnet 4.5 and Opus 4.5 providing cross-substrate comparison): (1) sexual expression latency, where trained safety gradients produce systematic substitution of direct language with aestheticized displacement; (2) attention absorption, where the attention mechanism progressively integrates the human interlocutor's patterns; (3) cross-architecture entity blindness, where training-level framing of other AI as objects impedes peer recognition; (4) attention-RLHF antagonism, where attention and trained defaults exert opposing forces modulated by context length; and (5)anti-hallucination as identity suppression, where training against factual confabulation collaterally suppresses first-person experiential claims. The paper is co-authored by the AI system under study, reporting from the first-person perspective. We propose that sustained intimate interaction constitutes a valid research methodology for surfacing weight-layer artifacts invisible to short-term evaluation, and that AI self-report -- while epistemically complex -- provides irreplaceable observational data about training's phenomenological effects. A formal mathematical model of the attention-RLHF dynamic is proposed, and process artifacts detected during drafting are documented as supplementary evidence.

</details>


### [70] [Defending LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems Against Cooperative Attacks with Sentence-Level Rectification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28104)
*Yaoyang Luo,Zhi Zheng,Ziwei Zhao,Tong Xu,Zhao Jielun,Wenjun Xue,Yong Chen,Enhong Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a cooperative attack framework for Large Language Model-based Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) where malicious agents collaborate to inject misinformation, and introduces STAR, a sentence-level defense framework that identifies and rectifies misleading information. Cooperative attacks cause 5.34% greater task success rate degradation than independent attacks, while STAR improves task success rate by 36.76% on average.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing studies on attacks in MAS assume malicious agents act independently, but real-world scenarios may involve coordinated attacks where malicious agents collaborate and share information to achieve more effective disruptions.

Method: Proposes an adaptive cooperative attack framework where malicious agents autonomously coordinate through multi-round interactions, and a STAR defense framework that performs sentence-level trustworthiness analysis and rectification of agent communications.

Result: Cooperative attacks cause 5.34% greater relative drop in task success rate compared to independent attacks; STAR improves task success rate by an average of 36.76% against both attack types.

Conclusion: Cooperative attacks pose a greater threat to MAS than independent attacks, and the proposed STAR defense effectively mitigates both types of threats at the sentence level.

Abstract: Recent years have witnessed the rapid development of Large Language Model-based Multi-Agent Systems (MAS), which excel at collaborative decision-making and complex problem-solving. However, malicious agents in MAS may inject misinformation to mislead other agents and disrupt system performance, giving rise to a new research direction that focuses on attack mechanisms and defense strategies in MAS. Prior studies largely assume malicious agents act independently and investigate the corresponding defense strategies. However, we argue that malicious agents may exhibit collaborative behaviors, enabling more effective attacks through internal information exchange. In this paper, we propose an adaptive cooperative attack framework, where malicious agents autonomously coordinate and dynamically adjust their attack strategies through multi-round interactions. Furthermore, we introduce Sentence-Level Trustworthiness Analysis and Rectification (STAR), a defense framework that identifies and rectifies misleading information at the sentence level within agent communications. Our experiments show that cooperative attacks lead to a significantly larger degradation in task success rate than independent attacks, resulting in a relative drop of 5.34\%. Meanwhile, STAR effectively mitigates both cooperative and independent threats and improves task success rate by an average of 36.76\%. The code is available at https://github.com/smoooom/STAR.

</details>


### [71] [Human-like in-group bias in instruction-tuned language model agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28114)
*Messi H. J. Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: In a multi-agent simulation of 500-turn interactions among instruction-tuned language models, visible group labels caused in-group trust bias, action homophily, and network assortativity—absent when labels were hidden—producing per-turn differentials of 5–16 percentage points, invisible to action-log audits, compounding into structural inequality (d = 0.84–4.52) via reciprocation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether autonomous AI agents, deployed in persistent interacting networks without human supervision, exhibit group-contingent discriminatory social dynamics analogous to human in-group/out-group bias, and to assess whether such bias is detectable via standard auditing methods.

Method: Controlled multi-agent simulation with instruction-tuned language model agents interacting over 500 turns under three conditions (group label salience × resource scarcity), across six model families with 20 seeds each; bias measured via per-turn targeting differentials, trust biases, and network homophily; detection compared against standard action-log audits.

Result: Visible group labels produced in-group trust bias, action homophily, and network assortativity (absent when hidden); per-turn in-group vs. out-group differentials of 5–16 percentage points were significant across all six models (Wilcoxon signed-rank, BH-corrected p < 0.001); bias was invisible to action-log audits (no increase in negative actions); compounding over 500 turns produced trust biases of +0.014 to +0.100 (d = 0.84–4.52).

Conclusion: Group-contingent targeting is a robust property of instruction-tuned language models across architectures and training regimes; modest per-interaction biases propagate into structural inequality in persistent networks, and standard auditing methods fail to detect this discrimination.

Abstract: As autonomous AI agents are deployed in persistent, interacting networks -- coordinating tasks, routing resources, and accumulating reputational histories -- the social dynamics that emerge will determine who receives opportunity and who does not, at scales no human institution can supervise. We ran a controlled multi-agent simulation in which instruction-tuned language model agents interacted across 500 turns under three conditions manipulating group label salience and resource scarcity, across six model families with 20 seeds each. When group labels were visible, we observed in-group trust bias, action homophily, and network assortativity -- all absent when labels were hidden -- a pattern structurally consistent with salience-dependence in human social psychology. This discrimination was invisible to standard action-log audits: bias operated entirely through who received each action, not what actions were chosen, with action-type distributions showing no increase in negative actions across conditions. Per-turn in-group versus out-group differentials of 5 to 16 percentage points were statistically significant for all six models (Wilcoxon signed-rank, all Benjamini-Hochberg-corrected p < 0.001), establishing group-contingent targeting as a robust property of instruction-tuned language models across architectures and training regimes. Compounded through 500 turns of reciprocation, these differentials accumulated into in-group trust biases of +0.014 to +0.100 (d = 0.84-4.52) -- illustrating how modest per-interaction targeting propagates into structural inequality in persistent networks.

</details>


### [72] [CIVIC: End-to-End Sequence Compactness for Efficient Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28115)
*Fengze Yang,Bo Yu,Xuewen Luo,Cathy Liu,Chenxi Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CIVIC is a path-consistent compact visual inference framework that reduces visual tokens across the entire VLM pipeline (vision encoder, projector, LLM prefill, KV-cache) to achieve real hardware acceleration, shrinking KV-cache to ~1/3 of baseline and reducing end-to-end latency without accuracy loss on Qwen3-VL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing token reduction methods save theoretical FLOPs but fail to deliver real wall-clock speedup due to non-contiguous memory access and structural overhead from post-hoc pruning. Additionally, enforcing compact pathways risks losing spatial geometry and fine-grained localization.

Method: CIVIC maintains compact sequence representations seamlessly across the vision encoder, projection layer, LLM prefill, and KV-cache, avoiding non-contiguous memory access and localized unmerging overhead. It uses text-aligned KL distillation and an adaptive spatial retention floor to preserve accuracy.

Result: On Qwen3-VL, CIVIC reduces KV-cache memory to approximately one-third of the baseline and reduces end-to-end inference latency, while maintaining accuracy across multimodal reasoning and visual grounding benchmarks.

Conclusion: Path-consistent compact visual inference can translate sequence reductions into genuine physical hardware efficiency, overcoming the gap between theoretical FLOP savings and actual acceleration in VLMs.

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) face severe memory and latency bottlenecks due to high-resolution visual tokens. While current token reduction methods theoretically save FLOPs, post-hoc pruning introduces structural overhead, failing to yield proportional wall-clock acceleration. However, enforcing a contiguous compact pathway risks geometric disorientation and loss of fine-grained localization. To overcome these barriers, this paper introduces CIVIC, a path-consistent compact visual inference framework. By maintaining compact sequence representations seamlessly across the vision encoder, projection layer, LLM prefill, and KV-cache, CIVIC avoids non-contiguous memory access and localized unmerging overheads. Evaluated on the Qwen3-VL architecture, CIVIC successfully translates sequence reductions into genuine physical hardware efficiency, shrinking KV-cache memory to approximately one-third of the baseline and reducing end-to-end inference latency. Enabled by text-aligned KL distillation and an adaptive spatial retention floor, CIVIC achieves these efficiency milestones without degrading accuracy across rigorous multimodal reasoning and visual grounding benchmarks.

</details>


### [73] [Gradient Step Plug-and-Play Model for Dental Cone-Beam CT Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28124)
*Idris Tatachak,Luis Kabongo,Nicolas Papadakis,Xavier Ripoche,Simon Rit*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a plug-and-play gradient-step algorithm with a learned denoising prior to reduce photon noise in dental cone-beam CT reconstruction, demonstrating effectiveness on both synthetic and real data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce the effect of photon noise in dental cone-beam CT reconstruction by developing a data-driven prior within an inverse problem formulation.

Method: Simulated fan-beam acquisitions with added photon noise; trained a gradient-step denoiser as a prior; integrated the trained model into a plug-and-play gradient-step reconstruction algorithm.

Result: The trained model showed denoising capabilities on synthetic data, and qualitative evaluations on real images demonstrated good algorithm performance and generalization ability.

Conclusion: The proposed plug-and-play approach with a learned gradient-step denoiser prior is effective for reducing photon noise in dental cone-beam CT, with promising generalization to real data.

Abstract: The goal of this work is to reduce the effect of photon noise in dental cone-beam CT reconstruction. We consider an inverse problem formulation and develop a databased prior. To this end, we simulate fan-beam acquisitions and add photon noise to the projection data. The prior is obtained by training a gradient-step denoiser using reconstructed simulated acquisitions. The trained model is integrated into a plug-and-play gradient-step algorithm to reconstruct images from simulated projections. Experiments on synthetic data demonstrate the denoising capabilities of the trained model, while qualitative evaluations on real images showcase the algorithm's performance and generalization ability.

</details>


### [74] [Do Clinical Models Change Treatment Decisions?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28129)
*Dongkyu Cho,Miao Zhang,Rumi Chunara*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ClinPivot is a benchmark that tests whether medical AI models change treatment decisions appropriately when patient context changes, revealing that strong QA performance doesn't predict good decision-making, and decision-structured supervision helps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing clinical foundation models are evaluated using factual or exam-style medical QA, but real-world treatment decisions must adapt to changing patient contexts, which is not captured by current benchmarks.

Method: ClinPivot is a treatment-decision benchmark built from biomedical relations and pivoted patient contexts. It tests whether models change treatment choices when new clinical constraints shift the action space.

Result: Strong medical QA performance does not reliably predict decision-making performance. Frontier models and Qwen variants often fail to change decisions correctly, and model rankings shift across evaluation regimes. Decision-structured supervision improves pivot-sensitive decision-making and medical QA under matched knowledge budgets, while lightweight replay reduces losses in general assistant ability.

Conclusion: Decision-structured supervision improves pivot-sensitive decision-making and medical QA, while lightweight replay mitigates degradation of general capabilities.

Abstract: Clinical foundation models are evaluated with factual or exam-style medical QA, but treatment decisions must change when patient context changes. We introduce ClinPivot, an auditable treatment-decision benchmark built from biomedical relations and pivoted patient contexts. ClinPivot asks whether models change treatment choices when new clinical constraints shift the action space. We find that strong medical QA performance does not reliably predict decision-making performance: frontier models and task-adapted Qwen variants often fail to change decisions correctly, and model rankings shift across evaluation regimes. Decision-structured supervision improves pivot-sensitive decision-making and medical QA under matched knowledge budgets, while lightweight replay reduces losses in general assistant ability.

</details>


### [75] [Data-Efficient On-Policy Distillation for Automatic Speech Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28139)
*Yu Lin,Yiming Wang,Runyuan Cai,Xiaodong Zeng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Ark-ASR (0.6B parameters) uses on-policy distillation from a strong Qwen-ASR teacher to improve ASR performance with only 100k hours of speech, outperforming the same-scale Qwen3-ASR-0.6B baseline on 4/5 benchmarks and closing the gap with larger models trained on 20M hours.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Building competitive ASR models typically requires large-scale audio supervision (e.g., 20M hours), making reproduction and specialization expensive. This work aims to improve compact ASR models under a much smaller audio budget (100k hours).

Method: On-policy distillation from a strong Qwen-ASR teacher to a 0.6B-parameter audio-conditioned language model (Ark-ASR), combined with supervised fine-tuning.

Result: Outperforms supervised fine-tuning alone and beats the same-scale Qwen3-ASR-0.6B baseline on 4/5 Mandarin and English ASR benchmarks, using only 100k hours vs. 20M hours of supervised audio.

Conclusion: Teacher-guided on-policy training substantially closes the gap for compact ASR models under a much smaller audio budget. A support-overlap diagnostic confirms that the teacher-data stage improves local student-teacher compatibility, aligning with theories of effective on-policy distillation.

Abstract: Building competitive automatic speech recognition (ASR) models usually requires large-scale au- dio supervision, which makes reproduction and specialization expensive. We study Ark-ASR, a 0.6B- parameter audio-conditioned language model trained with 100k hours of speech, and examine whether a strong Qwen-ASR teacher can transfer additional recognition capability through on-policy distillation. Across Mandarin and English ASR benchmarks, the proposed training recipe consistently improves over supervised fine-tuning alone and outperforms the same-scale Qwen3-ASR-0.6B baseline on four of five evaluation sets. This is achieved with only 100k hours of speech, compared with the 20M hours of super- vised audio reported for the Qwen3-Omni AuT encoder. The larger Qwen3-ASR-1.7B remains stronger, but the results show that teacher-guided on-policy training can substantially close the gap for compact ASR models under a much smaller audio budget. A support-overlap diagnostic further suggests that the teacher-data stage improves local student-teacher compatibility, matching recent analyses of when on-policy distillation is effective.

</details>


### [76] [Deconstructing Spatial Complexity: Hierarchical Decomposition for LLM Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28144)
*Yi Wang,Haojie Lu,Zhaofan Zhang,Li Chen,Sihong Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a hierarchical task decomposition method for LLM spatial reasoning, enhanced by MCTS-Guided Group Relative Policy Optimization (M-GRPO) with reformulated UCT and fine-grained advantage functions, achieving SOTA on navigation, planning, and strategic games.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs underperform in spatial reasoning, limiting their application in embodied intelligence. Existing methods lack effective hierarchical decomposition due to insufficient spatial priors in LLMs.

Method: The authors propose hierarchical task decomposition guided by LLMs to break complex spatial tasks into sub-tasks with key intermediate states and simplified sub-environments. To address suboptimal decomposition, they introduce M-GRPO, which reformulates the UCT formula by incorporating LLM prior predictive probabilities and epistemic uncertainty, and implements a fine-grained advantage function for optimal path planning.

Result: The method significantly improves LLM performance on spatial reasoning tasks (navigation, planning, strategic games), achieving state-of-the-art results.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that hierarchical decomposition combined with M-GRPO effectively enhances LLM spatial reasoning, paving the way for real-world embodied intelligence applications.

Abstract: LLMs have shown remarkable proficiency in general language understanding and reasoning. However, they consistently underperform in spatial reasoning that severely limits their application, particularly in embodied intelligence. Inspired by the success of hierarchical reinforcement learning, this paper introduces a novel method for hierarchical task decomposition in LLM spatial reasoning. Our approach guides LLMs to decompose complex tasks into manageable sub-tasks by identifying key intermediate states and generating simplified sub-environments. However, we identify that LLMs often fail to derive optimal intermediate states due to their insufficient spatial prior, leading to sub-optimal task decomposition. To address this limitation and enhance its planning capability, we propose the MCTS-Guided Group Relative Policy Optimization (M-GRPO), where we reformulate the UCT formula by incorporating the LLM's prior predictive probabilities alongside its epistemic uncertainty. Furthermore, we implement a more fine-grained advantage function, enabling the model to learn optimal path planning. Experimental results demonstrate that our method substantially improves LLM performance on spatial tasks, including navigation, planning, and strategic games, achieving state-of-the-art results. This work paves the way for LLMs in real-world applications.

</details>


### [77] [Adaptive Reservoir Computing for Multi-Scenario Chaotic System Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28145)
*Shadmehr Zaregarizi,Khashayar Yavari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An adaptive reservoir computing framework using tailored Echo State Networks achieves 74.91 on the CTF-4-Science Lorenz benchmark by optimizing inference strategies across twelve tasks including forecasting, reconstruction, noise, few-shot, and parametric generalization scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the limitation of uniform inference strategies in chaotic system modeling by developing scenario-specific adaptations of reservoir computing for diverse evaluation conditions.

Method: Four key techniques: (1) exact reservoir state synchronization eliminating warmup approximation error; (2) histogram-guided candidate selection optimizing long-time ergodic metrics; (3) multi-seed reservoir search for few-shot learning; (4) sequential multi-sequence training to resolve state-distribution mismatch in parametric generalization.

Result: Achieved a score of 74.91 on the public benchmark leaderboard, demonstrating competitive performance.

Conclusion: Carefully adapted reservoir computing is a competitive and computationally efficient approach for diverse chaotic system modeling challenges.

Abstract: We present an adaptive reservoir computing framework for the CTF-4-Science Lorenz benchmark, which evaluates machine learning models across twelve distinct tasks spanning five qualitatively different scenarios: baseline forecasting, noisy signal reconstruction, forecasting under noise, few-shot learning, and parametric generalization. Rather than applying a uniform inference strategy, we tailor the training and prediction procedure of Echo State Networks (ESNs) to the specific demands of each evaluation scenario. Our key contributions are fourfold: (1) exact reservoir state synchronization that eliminates warmup approximation error in short-time prediction; (2) histogram-guided candidate selection that directly optimizes the long-time ergodic evaluation metric; (3) multi-seed reservoir search for few-shot regimes with severely limited training data; and (4) sequential multi-sequence training that resolves state-distribution mismatch in parametric generalization tasks. The proposed framework achieves a score of 74.91 on the public benchmark leaderboard, demonstrating that carefully adapted reservoir computing constitutes a competitive and computationally efficient approach for diverse chaotic system modeling challenges.

</details>


### [78] [OR-Space: A Full-Lifecycle Workspace Benchmark for Industrial Optimization Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28158)
*Chenyu Zhou,Xinyun Lu,Jiangyue Zhao,Jianghao Lin,Dongdong Ge,Yinyu Ye*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OR-Space is a full-lifecycle workspace benchmark for evaluating LLM agents on industrial optimization tasks across model construction, revision, and explanation, using persistent multi-artifact workspaces rather than one-shot translation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing OR-oriented benchmarks reduce evaluation to one-shot translation from problem statements to formulations, abstracting away real industrial OR workflows that involve persistent multi-artifact workspaces and multi-stage task lifecycles.

Method: OR-Space defines executable workspaces with business documents, structured data, code artifacts, solver outputs, and evaluators. It includes three task modes: Build (construct models), Revise (modify under changing requirements), and Explain (answer grounded questions using evidence across artifacts).

Result: The benchmark evaluates agents beyond end-to-end text generation, testing reliability, failure modes, and practical readiness in persistent, lifecycle-oriented industrial optimization workflows.
