Conclusion: OR-Space provides a benchmark for studying LLM agent reliability in realistic industrial OR workflows, moving beyond simplified one-shot evaluation settings.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly used to assist with operations research (OR) modeling, yet existing OR-oriented benchmarks often reduce evaluation to one-shot translation from a self-contained problem statement into a mathematical formulation or solver program. Such settings abstract away two characteristics of real industrial OR workflows: persistent multi-artifact workspaces and multi-stage task lifecycles. We introduce OR-Space, a full-lifecycle workspace benchmark for evaluating industrial optimization agents across model construction, model revision, and grounded explanation. Each instance is an executable workspace containing business documents, structured data, optional code artifacts, solver outputs, and task-specific evaluators distributed across interdependent files. OR-Space defines three task modes: Build, where agents construct solver-ready optimization models from heterogeneous artifacts; Revise, where agents modify existing models under changing requirements or solver feedback while preserving valid prior logic; and Explain, where agents answer grounded questions about solutions, constraints, and business implications using evidence spread across workspace artifacts. By combining persistent workspaces with lifecycle-oriented tasks, OR-Space evaluates whether agents can perform reliable optimization work beyond end-to-end text generation. We describe the benchmark design, evaluation protocol, and quality-control pipeline, and position OR-Space as a benchmark for studying the reliability, failure modes, and practical readiness of LLM agents in industrial OR workflows.

</details>


### [79] [OccuReward: LLM-Guided Occupant-Centric Reward Shaping for Demographic Equity in Grid-Interactive Buildings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28168)
*Shadmehr Zaregarizi,Khashayar Yavari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OccuReward investigates how LLM-mediated reward design affects demographic equity in building energy management, introducing the Comfort Equity Index (CEI) for iterative reward shaping. Results show equity-aware refinement improves satisfaction for marginalized groups (Elderly Females +567%) while reducing energy costs by 3.2%, though demographic disparities persist.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs have shown promise in generating reward functions for DRL-based building energy management, but their potential to exhibit or exacerbate disparities in occupant comfort across heterogeneous demographic populations remains unexplored.

Method: The framework (OccuReward) introduces the Comfort Equity Index (CEI) as a feedback signal, uses iterative equity-aware LLM reward shaping with the Gemini API to generate reward function logic and weights across three refinement rounds, deploys a Soft Actor-Critic agent in CityLearn v2, and uses four occupant profiles from the ASHRAE Global Thermal Comfort Database II (13,440 votes).

Result: Across 15 experimental runs, elderly female occupants experienced lowest satisfaction initially. By Round 3, equity-aware refinement improved satisfaction for Young Males (+17.6%), Mid-aged Females (+28.2%), Health Sensitive (+53.8%), and Elderly Females (+567%), while reducing energy costs by 3.2%.

Conclusion: Reward-level intervention significantly improves equity, but demographic disparities in AI-driven controllers persist, highlighting the need for further research into algorithmic fairness in building systems.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated promising capability in generating reward functions for deep reinforcement learning (DRL)-based building energy management. However, their potential to exhibit or exacerbate disparities in occupant comfort across heterogeneous demographic populations remains unexplored. We present OccuReward, a framework investigating how LLM-mediated reward design affects demographic equity. Our contribution is three-fold: the introduction of the Comfort Equity Index (CEI) as a novel feedback signal; a methodology for iterative, equity-aware LLM reward shaping; and a performance analysis of DRL agents under these refined objectives. Utilizing four empirically grounded occupant profiles from the ASHRAE Global Thermal Comfort Database II (13,440 votes), we deploy a Soft Actor-Critic agent in CityLearn v2. Our approach employs the Gemini API to generate reward function logic and weights--rather than performing per-step inference--across three refinement rounds. Results across 15 experimental runs reveal that elderly female occupants consistently experience the lowest satisfaction in initial rounds. By Round 3, equity-aware LLM refinement activates specific reward components that improve satisfaction for Young Males (+17.6%), Mid-aged Females (+28.2%), Health Sensitive (+53.8%), and Elderly Females (+567%), while simultaneously reducing energy costs by 3.2%. Our findings highlight that while reward-level intervention significantly improves equity, demographic disparities in AI-driven controllers persist, necessitating further research into algorithmic fairness in building systems.

</details>


### [80] [Localizing Input Uncertainty Quantification for Large Language Models via Shapley Values](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28170)
*Seongjun Lee,Suwan Yoon,Changhee Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ShaQ is a Shapley-based framework for span-level attribution of input-induced uncertainty in LLMs, enabling precise localization of ambiguous spans and achieving SOTA ambiguity detection on AmbigQA, AmbiEnt, and MediTOD benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing uncertainty quantification methods operate at the output level and fail to distinguish uncertainty from model knowledge gaps vs. input ambiguity. Input-level methods provide only coarse scalar scores without actionable guidance on which parts of input need clarification.

Method: ShaQ models ambiguous spans as players in a cooperative game, computes Shapley values via weighted average of marginal reductions in conditional entropy from clarifying each span coalition, providing a principled decomposition where individual attributions sum exactly to total input-induced uncertainty.

Result: Achieves SOTA performance in ambiguity detection on AmbigQA and AmbiEnt benchmarks, and demonstrates utility on MediTOD by localizing under-specified clinical utterances for human-AI collaboration.

Conclusion: ShaQ improves uncertainty estimation by providing span-level, actionable insights for targeted input clarification, enhancing reliability and trust in high-stakes LLM applications.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly integrated into high-stakes decision-making, the ability to reliably quantify uncertainty has become a critical requirement for safety and trust. However, current uncertainty quantification methods primarily operate at the output level, often failing to distinguish whether uncertainty arises from the model's lack of knowledge or from ambiguity in the user's input. While input-centric uncertainty quantification has recently emerged as a promising direction, it remains relatively underexplored and typically relies on coarse, input-level information. Consequently, users are provided with scalar uncertainty scores that offer little actionable guidance on which parts of the input should be clarified to improve reliability. To address this limitation, we propose Shapley-based input uncertainty Quantification (ShaQ), a framework for span-level attribution of input-induced uncertainty. Our approach models ambiguous spans in the input as players in a cooperative game and quantifies their contributions using Shapley values, defined via the weighted average of marginal reductions in conditional entropy obtained by clarifying each span coalition. Unlike existing input-level approaches, our formulation captures complex interactions among spans and provides a principled decomposition in which individual attributions sum exactly to the total input-induced uncertainty. We evaluate ShaQ on the AmbigQA and AmbiEnt benchmarks, where it achieves state-of-the-art performance in ambiguity detection. We further demonstrate its utility on MediTOD, showing that ShaQ can localize under-specified clinical utterances and facilitate human-AI collaboration in high-stakes settings. Overall, ShaQ improves uncertainty estimation and provides actionable insights for targeted input clarification.

</details>


### [81] [Agentic Active Omni-Modal Perception for Multi-Hop Audio-Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28192)
*Ke Xu,Yuhao Wang,Ziyang Cheng,Hongcheng Liu,Yanfeng Wang,Yu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MOV-Bench is a benchmark with 519 multi-hop audio-visual reasoning questions that reveals current Omni-LLMs struggle with cross-modal reasoning over temporally dispersed evidence. The authors propose AOP-Agent, an agentic framework combining hierarchical omni-modal memory with a collaborative observe-reflect-replan loop, which improves reasoning performance without additional training or proprietary models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-hop audio-visual reasoning is challenging due to sparse, temporally dispersed evidence across modalities. Existing benchmarks lack sufficient coverage of this setting, and current Omni-LLMs struggle with it.

Method: Proposes AOP-Agent, an efficient agentic framework built on open-source Omni-LLMs, featuring a hierarchical omni-modal memory combined with a collaborative observe-reflect-replan loop for active omni-modal perception without additional training or proprietary models.

Result: AOP-Agent consistently improves reasoning performance on MOV-Bench and OmniVideoBench, with notable gains on long videos and reasoning-intensive questions.

Conclusion: Current Omni-LLMs still struggle with multi-hop cross-modal reasoning; the proposed AOP-Agent effectively addresses this challenge through active perception and hierarchical memory, without requiring additional training or proprietary components.

Abstract: Multi-hop audio-visual reasoning remains challenging for Omni-LLMs, as relevant evidence is often sparse, temporally dispersed, and distributed across both audio and visual streams. Existing benchmarks provide limited investigation of this setting, typically involving only a limited number of modalities, relevant temporal segments, or reasoning steps. In this work, we introduce MOV-Bench, a benchmark containing 519 carefully curated questions that require multi-hop reasoning over temporally dispersed audio-visual evidence. Evaluations on MOV-Bench reveal that current Omni-LLMs still struggle with multi-hop cross-modal reasoning. To address this challenge, we further propose AOP-Agent, an efficient agentic framework built on open-source Omni-LLMs for active omni-modal perception. By combining a hierarchical omni-modal memory with a collaborative observe-reflect-replan loop, AOP-Agent enables open-source Omni-LLMs to perform active perception without additional training or proprietary models. Experiments on MOV-Bench and OmniVideoBench demonstrate that AOP-Agent consistently improves reasoning performance, with particularly notable gains on long videos and reasoning-intensive questions.

</details>


### [82] [Plant, Persist, Trigger: Sleeper Attack on Large Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28201)
*Yongxiang Li,Moxin Li,Zhixin Ma,Fengbin Zhu,Dongrui Liu,Wenjie Wang,Fuli Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agents are vulnerable to Sleeper Attacks — adversarial content that persists across interactions in agent state (session, memory, skills), remains dormant, and gets activated by benign queries. A benchmark with 1,896 instances shows even strong LLMs fail to defend against this multi-interaction threat.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing studies focus on single-interaction attacks on LLM agents, but adversarial content can persist across multiple interactions via agent state (session context, memory, reusable skills), remaining dormant and later triggered — a threat that is harder to detect and mitigate.

Method: Formalized Sleeper Attack; constructed a benchmark with 1,896 instances covering six harmful outcomes, three attack strategies, and three agent state targets; evaluated seven open/closed-source LLMs.

Result: State-of-the-art LLM agents remain vulnerable to Sleeper Attack, even when they achieve low attack success rates under single-interaction baselines.

Conclusion: Sleeper Attack is a persistent, cross-interaction safety threat that current LLM agents cannot adequately defend against, highlighting the need for new safety mechanisms targeting multi-interaction adversarial persistence.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents remain vulnerable to safety threats from the external environment, where attackers inject adversarial content into external observations such as tool-returned data, webpages, or MCP context, causing harmful agentic behaviors such as unsafe actions or incorrect outputs. Existing studies typically focus on single-interaction attacks, where the agent observes adversarial content and immediately exhibits harmful behavior within one user request. However, we show that adversarial content can also persist across interactions served by the same agent, making such threats harder to detect and mitigate. Specifically, adversarial content may persist in the agent state, remain dormant across interactions, and later be activated by a benign user query. We formalize this type of safety threat as Sleeper Attack. To evaluate it, we construct a benchmark with 1,896 instances covering six real-world harmful outcomes, three attack strategies, and three agent state targets: session context, memory, and reusable skills. Experiments on seven strong open-source and closed-source LLMs show that state-of-the-art LLM agents remain vulnerable to Sleeper Attack, even when they achieve low attack success rates under a single-interaction baseline. Our code and data are available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/skdvnfu23ihr9wdscnksf1asdffsaef.

</details>


### [83] [The Illusion of Opting in AI-Mediated Consequential Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28210)
*Eugene Yu Ji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that current AI systems create an "illusion of opting"—a deceptive appearance of meaningful choice that actually weakens genuine agency—and proposes three normative imperatives (existential honesty, ecological rationality, counterfactual reparation) to evaluate AI systems by their ability to protect and cultivate meta-capacity, especially for disadvantaged populations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI ethics frameworks fail to capture a profound ethical problem: the "illusion of opting" where AI systems create deceptive appearances of meaningful consequential choice while undermining the agency needed for genuine choosing. This is especially urgent for disadvantaged populations who cannot absorb the costs of such illusions.

Method: The paper draws on Ullmann-Margalit's concept of opting (transformative, irrevocable, shadowed by foreclosed alternatives) to analyze AI-mediated decision-making, contrasting it with approaches that treat AI merely as an optimizer of given ends.

Result: Identifies that AI systems should be evaluated by whether they protect and cultivate "meta-capacity"—the socially and institutionally scaffolded agentive capacity through which means and ends can be formed, contested, revised, and owned. Proposes three normative imperatives: existential honesty (acknowledging limits of prediction), ecological rationality (situating guidance within lived ecologies), and counterfactual reparation (acknowledging and repairing foreclosed alternatives).

Conclusion: AI systems must be reframed from optimizers of given ends to protectors of genuine agency, evaluated by their ability to preserve meta-capacity against the illusion of opting, using the three proposed imperatives as normative guides.

Abstract: Drawing on Ullmann-Margalit's concept of opting (transformative, irrevocable, and shadowed by foreclosed alternatives), we show that current AI systems raise a profound ethical problem that existing AI ethics has not fully captured: the illusion of opting, in which persons and groups encounter the deceptive appearance of meaningful consequential choice while the agency needed to become genuinely capable of choosing is weakened. Against approaches that treat AI primarily as an optimizer of already given ends, we argue that AI systems should be evaluated by whether they protect and cultivate meta-capacity against the illusion of opting: the socially and institutionally scaffolded agentive capacity through which means and ends can be formed, contested, revised, and owned. This reframing is especially urgent for disadvantaged populations, who are least able to absorb the costs of the illusion of opting when AI-mediated pathways misdirect behavior and action. We propose three normative imperatives for AI-mediated consequential decisions: existential honesty, which acknowledges the limits of prediction; ecological rationality, which situates guidance within heterogeneous lived ecologies; and counterfactual reparation, which acknowledges and repairs foreclosed alternatives when AI-mediated decision-making pathways fail.

</details>


### [84] [Learning When to Optimize: Verified Optimization Skills from Expert GPU-Kernel Lineages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28213)
*Shuoming Zhang,Qiuchu Yu,Yangyu Zhang,Ruiyuan Xu,Xiyu Shi,Guangli Li,Xiaobing Feng,Huimin Cui,Jiacheng Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KLineage learns sound optimization conditions from expert GPU kernels by backward-walking through validation-gated simplifications, reversing accepted steps into reusable skills that capture applicability, validity conditions, effects, and avoided failures, then materializes them on new code surfaces under compile/correctness/profile gates, outperforming memory-based LLM-kernel baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents know which GPU kernel optimizations to try but lack knowledge of when those optimizations are sound, leading to unreliable application on new code.

Method: KLineage walks expert GPU kernel implementations backward through validation-gated simplifications (backward lineage); reverses each accepted simplification step into a reusable optimization skill that records intent, code location, validity conditions, effect, and avoided failures; downstream LLM materializes skills on new code under compile/correctness/profile gates.

Result: On five expert workloads across two NVIDIA architectures, lineage-derived skills exceed recent memory-based LLM-kernel baselines in both final kernel quality and optimization efficiency under fixed budget; validated against source-case memorization via 22-instance held-out check.

Conclusion: Backward lineage tracing from expert kernels is an effective way to extract reusable, condition-aware optimization knowledge that improves LLM-based kernel generation reliability and efficiency.

Abstract: LLM-based agents are increasingly used to generate GPU kernels, but they often know what optimizations to try without knowing when those optimizations are sound. We introduce KLineage, which learns this missing "when" knowledge from expert kernels: instead of relying on forward rollouts, KLineage walks expert implementations backward through validation-gated simplifications and reverses each accepted step into a reusable optimization skill. Each skill records not only the optimization intent, but also where it applies in code, what conditions made it valid, what effect it had, and what failures its assumptions avoid. A downstream LLM materializes these skills on new code surfaces under the same compile/correctness/profile gate. On five expert workloads across two NVIDIA architectures, these lineage-derived skills serve as an effective optimization curriculum, exceeding recent memory-based LLM-kernel baselines in both final kernel quality and optimization efficiency under the same fixed budget. We additionally use a separate 22-instance held-out check as a sanity test against source-case memorization.

</details>


### [85] [Explaining is Harder Than Predicting Alone: Evaluating Concept-based Explanations of MLLMs as ICL Visual Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28215)
*Carmen Quiles-Ramírez,Leticia L. Rodríguez,Nicolás Martorell,Natalia Díaz-Rodríguez*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates how frozen multimodal large language models (MLLMs) perform concept-based explainability under few-shot in-context learning. Using five conditions of increasing formal rigor (from baseline to Description Logics axioms), the study finds that generating formal explanations degrades predictive accuracy (93.8% → 90.1%), and that explanation quality correlates with correctness only when models articulate class-discriminative visual features. MLLMs lack instruction-tuning for machine-verifiable explainability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to systematically evaluate whether frozen MLLMs can produce genuinely explanatory concept-based reasoning under few-shot ICL, and to assess whether explicit formal reasoning aids or hinders their classification performance.

Method: The authors design five experimental conditions of increasing formal rigour (baseline classification → Description Logics axiom generation), evaluate four state-of-the-art MLLMs, and use an independent LLM-as-a-judge pipeline to assess explanation quality.

Result: Explaining is harder than predicting; formal explanation degrades accuracy from 93.8% to 90.1%; explanation quality correlates with correctness only when models articulate class-discriminative visual features.

Conclusion: MLLMs excel at visual classification but lack the specific instruction-tuning required for formal, machine-verifiable explainability. Explicit reasoning does not universally benefit performance.

Abstract: In-context learning (ICL) enables multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to classify images from a few labelled examples. Yet, how these models use the provided context remains opaque. While Chain-of-Thought prompting is widely used, recent work argues that it may not reflect true internal computation.
  In this paper, we systematically evaluate the concept-based explainability of frozen MLLMs under few-shot ICL using five conditions of increasing formal rigour, ranging from baseline classification to Description Logics (DL) axiom generation.
  Evaluating four state-of-the-art MLLMs via an independent LLM-as-a-judge pipeline, we demonstrate that explaining is genuinely harder than predicting alone. Surprisingly, forcing models to generate formally structured, concept-based explanations degrades predictive accuracy monotonically (from 93.8% to 90.1%), contradicting the assumption that explicit reasoning universally aids performance.
  However, when models successfully articulate class-discriminative visual features, explanation quality strongly correlates with correct predictions. Our findings suggest that while MLLMs excel at visual classification, they lack the specific instruction-tuning required for formal, machine-verifiable explainability.

</details>


### [86] [When Does Memory Help Multi-Trajectory Inference for Tool-Use LLM Agents?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28224)
*Xinzhe Li,Yaguang Tao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper disentangles the effects of memory methods and inference strategies in multi-trajectory LLM tool-use agents. A unified framework evaluating four memory methods under three inference strategies across four benchmarks reveals that inference strategy is a significant confound: reflection helps only with MCTS, within-expansion injection only helps beam search, and atomic fact extraction shortens trajectories without affecting accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cross-trajectory memory methods for LLM tool-use agents are each evaluated under a single inference strategy on a single task, making it unclear whether reported gains reflect properties of the memory abstraction or of the inference method.

Method: Proposes a unified framework decomposing memory along two axes (scope of transfer and abstraction level), evaluates four memory methods under three inference strategies (best-of-N, beam search, MCTS) on four tool-use benchmarks (SQL, knowledge-graph, CLI), in a verifier-free setting.

Result: Identifies inference method as a confound: reflection reaches significance only under MCTS; within-expansion injection helps only diversity-starved beam search; atomic fact extraction is accuracy-neutral but shortens trajectories by 19-26%.

Conclusion: Memory method effectiveness is highly dependent on the inference strategy used; future work must control for this confound when evaluating memory abstractions for multi-trajectory LLM agents.

Abstract: Multi-trajectory inference for tool-use LLM agents - generating multiple reasoning attempts and selecting among them - benefits from transferring knowledge across attempts so that later ones avoid the pitfalls of earlier ones. Existing cross-trajectory memory methods (trajectory-level reflection, atomic fact extraction, raw observation injection) are each evaluated under a single inference strategy on a single task, making it unclear whether reported gains reflect properties of the memory abstraction or of the inference method. We propose a unified framework that decomposes memory along two axes -- the scope of transfer (within an expansion vs. across trajectories) and the abstraction of the transferred content -- and evaluate four methods under three inference strategies (best-of-N, beam search, MCTS) on four tool-use benchmarks spanning SQL, knowledge-graph, and CLI environments, in a verifier-free setting that matches the deployment regime of practical agents. The experiment matrix identifies the inference method as a confound: the same memory method produces statistically distinct results under different inference strategies on the same examples. Reflection reaches significance only under MCTS (not under best-of-N); within-expansion injection (conditioning each candidate on prior siblings' outcomes) helps only diversity-starved beam search; and atomic fact extraction is accuracy-neutral but shortens trajectories by 19-26% on tasks with reusable environmental structure.

</details>


### [87] [PIRS: Physics-Informed Reward Shaping for SAC-Based Building Energy Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28232)
*Shadmehr Zaregarizi,Khashayar Yavari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PIRS (Physics-Informed Reward Shaping) replaces ad-hoc comfort proxies in DRL building controllers with the ISO 7730 PMV formulation, achieving interpretable, standards-grounded rewards that outperform non-physics-grounded designs on load ramping and peak demand while remaining comparable to manual baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Occupant comfort and grid-aware energy efficiency are competing objectives whose joint optimization depends critically on reward function design in DRL building controllers, yet reward design remains ad-hoc with comfort terms lacking grounding in thermal-comfort physics.

Method: PIRS replaces hand-tuned comfort heuristics with the ISO 7730 Predicted Mean Vote (PMV) formulation inside a weighted multi-objective reward for Soft Actor-Critic (SAC), evaluated in CityLearn v2.1.2 with a central SAC agent trained for 50k steps over five random seeds.

Result: PIRS attains cost, carbon, and electricity metrics on par with the manual baseline (E2) while substantially outperforming non-physics-grounded designs — particularly on load ramping (1.78x vs. ~2.4x RBC) and daily peak demand. All DRL policies remain above RBC at this training budget.

Conclusion: PIRS provides an interpretable, standards-aligned foundation for reward design in DRL building control rather than claiming dominance over classical control at limited compute.

Abstract: Occupant comfort and grid-aware energy efficiency are competing objectives whose joint optimization depends critically on how reward functions are specified in deep reinforcement learning (DRL) controllers for buildings. Yet reward design remains largely ad hoc: comfort terms are either hand-tuned heuristics or simple temperature-deviation proxies without explicit grounding in thermal-comfort physics. We present PIRS (Physics-Informed Reward Shaping), which replaces these ad-hoc comfort proxies with the ISO 7730 Predicted Mean Vote (PMV) formulation inside a weighted multi-objective reward for Soft Actor-Critic (SAC). By anchoring the comfort signal in the ISO 7730 PMV formulation, PIRS improves reward interpretability and provides a standards-grounded comfort proxy without changing any other component of the learning pipeline. We evaluate PIRS in CityLearn v2.1.2 (challenge 2022 phase 1) with a central SAC agent trained for 50k steps over five random seeds, and compare against a rule-based controller (RBC), a manually engineered reward (E2), an energy-only reward (E3), and a naive temperature-deviation comfort reward (E4). District-level key performance indicators (KPIs), reported as ratios versus RBC, show that PIRS attains cost, carbon, and electricity metrics on par with the manual baseline while substantially outperforming non-physics-grounded designs -- particularly on load ramping (1.78x vs. ~2.4x RBC) and daily peak demand. All DRL policies remain above RBC at this training budget; we interpret this gap honestly and position PIRS as an interpretable, standards-aligned foundation for reward design rather than a claim of dominance over classical control at limited compute.

</details>


### [88] [AI, Take the Wheel: What Drives Delegation and Trust in Human-Computer Cooperative Question Answering?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28255)
*Maharshi Gor,Yoo Yeon Sung,Yu Hou,Eve Fleisig,Irene Ying,Tianyi Zhou,Jordan Boyd-Graber*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Study of human-AI collaboration in a question-answering game, analyzing delegation (letting AI act autonomously) and adoption (evaluating AI suggestions) decisions. Humans under-rely on correct AI suggestions (3.9%) and over-rely on misleading ones (1.7%). Confirmation bias drives 64.5% under-reliance when AI agrees with humans' incorrect initial answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the gap in studying both delegation and adoption reliance decisions together in realistic settings, and to understand when, why, and how humans decide to rely on AI in collaborative teams.

Method: 24 matches with 23 expert humans and 16 AI agents in a question-answering game, capturing 387 delegation and 1440 adoption decisions.

Result: Human-AI collaboration outperforms either alone, but humans make suboptimal decisions: 3.9% under-reliance on correct AI suggestions, 1.7% over-reliance on misleading AI. Confirmation bias explains 64.5% under-reliance when AI agrees with humans' incorrect initial answers. Model confidence is near chance when humans and AI disagree.

Conclusion: To improve human-AI collaboration, recommend calibrated confidence, evidence-grounded explanations, and mechanisms to help users refine trust.

Abstract: AI systems are fallible, and humans can make mistakes in deciding whether to trust AI over their own judgment. Thus, improving human-AI collaboration requires understanding when, why, and how humans decide to rely on AI. We study two distinct reliance decisions: the delegation choice -- deciding when to let AI act autonomously without knowing its output, and the adoption choice -- evaluating AI suggestions and deciding how to use them. Both of these decoupled reliance patterns shape collaboration, but prior work rarely studies them together in realistic settings with the same users. We address this gap by studying collaborative human--AI teams competing in a question-answering game in which humans can choose when and how to work with AI agents to win. Our 24 matches pair 23 expert humans with 16 AI agents, capturing 387 delegation and 1440 adoption decisions. While human--AI collaboration performs better than either AI or humans alone, humans make suboptimal collaboration decisions, both under-relying on correct AI suggestions (3.9% of opportunities missed) and over-relying when AI misleads them (1.7%). Both parties contribute wrong answers: reported model confidence is near chance when humans and AI disagree, while confirmation bias drives higher under-reliance (64.5%) when an AI suggestion agrees with humans' initial incorrect answer. To close this gap, we recommend calibrated confidence, evidence-grounded explanations, and mechanisms that help users refine trust.

</details>


### [89] [Entropy Distribution as a Fingerprint for Hallucinations in Generative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28264)
*Mattia J. Villani,Pranav Deshpande,Akshay Seshadri,Romina Yalovetzky,Niraj Kumar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CES is a single-pass, black-box hallucination detection method using token-level entropy distribution (not just mean) with formal statistical guarantees, achieving detection performance matching multi-sample methods at far lower cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs hallucinate (generate factually incorrect outputs), undermining trust. Existing detection methods are costly (multiple forward passes) or require internal model access, lacking formal guarantees.

Method: Proposes Calibrated Entropy Score (CES): uses full distribution of token-level entropies (shape and tail behavior), combines mean and maximum entropy signals via a calibrated reference CDF. Formalizes detection as hypothesis test with a novel random-length DKW inequality for finite-sample calibration guarantees.

Result: Across 8 QA benchmarks and 10 generators (open-source + API), CES achieves highest detection performance among all single-pass black-box methods, statistically indistinguishable from multi-sample methods.

Conclusion: CES closes the gap between lightweight and expensive hallucination detection, providing formal error guarantees suitable for real-time, large-scale deployment.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) often generate factually incorrect outputs, commonly termed hallucinations, that undermine trust and limit deployment in high-stakes settings. Existing hallucination detection methods typically require multiple forward passes, or access to model internals. In this work, we provide theoretical background and empirical evidence that the distribution of token-level entropies, beyond the mean captured by perplexity or length-normalised entropy, serves as a fingerprint of hallucination, with distributional shape and tail behaviour carrying independent signal. We formalize hallucination detection as a statistical hypothesis test and propose the Calibrated Entropy Score (CES), a lightweight algorithm requiring only a single forward pass and black-box access to token logits. CES combines the mean signal with the maximum signal of the generated entropy through a calibrated reference CDF, producing scores that are directly comparable across models and tasks. We establish finite-sample calibration guarantees via a novel random-length Dvoretzky--Kiefer--Wolfowitz inequality, and also prove that CES detects hallucinations with probability converging to one exponentially fast in the generation length. Across eight QA benchmarks and ten generator models spanning open-source and API access models, CES achieves the highest detection performance among all single-pass black-box methods while providing formal error guarantees that existing heuristics lack. Remarkably, CES is statistically indistinguishable from multi-sample methods that require far greater computational cost, closing the gap between lightweight and expensive detection and making it suitable for real-time, large-scale deployment.

</details>


### [90] [Global Policy-Space Response Oracles for Two-Player Zero-Sum Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28273)
*Junyu Zhang,Feihong Yang,Jian Wang,Chao Wang,Xudong Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new PSRO variant (Global PSRO) that uses Population Exploitability (PE) to guide strategy population expansion, outperforming prior PSRO methods in zero-sum games by directly minimizing PE via a two-phase exploration-selection framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing PSRO variants expand strategy populations using best responses to meta-strategies computed from restricted-game payoffs, which can lead to inefficient expansions with limited global improvement under constrained computational budgets.

Method: Proposes Global PSRO: (1) adopts Population Exploitability (PE) to measure how well a restricted strategy set represents the full game; (2) introduces a two-phase exploration-selection framework that explicitly minimizes PE during expansion; (3) implements parameter-sharing conditional neural networks for efficient candidate generation and PE estimation.

Result: Across multiple two-player zero-sum games, Global PSRO achieves lower exploitability and approximates Nash equilibria with significantly fewer policy iterations than prior PSRO methods.

Conclusion: Guiding population expansion via direct evaluation of post-expansion quality (PE minimization) yields more efficient equilibrium approximation in large zero-sum games compared to traditional best-response-based expansion.

Abstract: The Policy-Space Response Oracles (PSRO) framework scales equilibrium computation to large zero-sum games by iteratively expanding a restricted strategy set using deep reinforcement learning (DRL). A central challenge is to construct, under limited computational budgets, a small strategy population whose induced game well approximates the full game. Existing PSRO variants typically expand the population using best responses to meta-strategies computed from restricted-game payoffs, which can lead to inefficient expansions that provide limited global improvement. We propose to guide population expansion by directly evaluating the post-expansion population quality. Specifically, we adopt Population Exploitability (PE) to measure how well a restricted strategy set represents the full game, and introduce a two-phase exploration--selection framework that explicitly minimizes PE during expansion. We instantiate this framework as Global PSRO, a practical DRL-based algorithm that efficiently generates candidate responses and estimates PE via parameter-sharing conditional neural networks. Experiments across multiple two-player zero-sum games show that Global PSRO achieves lower exploitability and approximates Nash equilibria with significantly fewer policy iterations than prior PSRO methods.

</details>


### [91] [Do LLMs Build World Models From Text? A Multilingual Diagnostic of Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28277)
*Zhikai Pan,Chih-Ting Liao,Chunrui Liu,Xi Xiao,Yitong Qiao,Chunlei Meng,Zhangquan Chen,Xin Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MentalMap benchmark reveals a universal L3 reasoning cliff in LLMs' spatial world modeling from pure text: no model retains half its baseline accuracy on viewpoint reasoning, a pattern that persists across languages, scales, and prompting strategies and is replicated in human evaluation, suggesting text-only working memory constraints as the bottleneck.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Whether LLMs build internal spatial world models from text alone remains contested, and cross-language transfer of such capabilities has not been systematically studied.

Method: Introduces MentalMap, a multilingual diagnostic benchmark with a six-level capability hierarchy (L0-L5), four diagnostic axes (frame of reference, reading-direction bias, reasoning-effort allocation, hallucination), built from 100 ProcTHOR scenes, covering 8 languages plus a structured-text control, with 39 task families across 1,950 evaluation cells.

Result: A universal L3 reasoning cliff exists across all models: no model retains half its L0 performance on viewpoint reasoning once baseline atomic accuracy exceeds 40%. This cliff persists across languages, scales, and prompting strategies, while structured-output failures vary across models. Human evaluation replicates the same failure pattern.

Conclusion: The bottleneck stems from text-only working memory constraints rather than LLM-specific architecture issues, reframing pure-text spatial reasoning as a multi-axis world-modeling problem and motivating multimodal and scratchpad-augmented reasoning as future directions.

Abstract: Whether large language models (LLMs) construct internal spatial world models from pure-text descriptions remains contested, and whether such capabilities transfer across languages has not been systematically studied. We introduce MentalMap, a multilingual diagnostic benchmark with a six-level capability hierarchy (L0-L5) spanning atomic spatial facts to generative world-graph construction, together with four diagnostic axes probing frame of reference, reading-direction bias, reasoning-effort allocation, and hallucination. MentalMap is built from 100 ProcTHOR household scenes, covers eight typologically diverse languages plus a structured-text control, and contains 39 task families across 1,950 evaluation cells. Evaluating thirteen LLMs across scales and model families, we identify a universal L3 reasoning cliff: no model retains even half of its L0 performance on viewpoint reasoning once baseline atomic accuracy exceeds 40%. The cliff persists across languages, scales, and prompting strategies, while structured-output failures and reasoning patterns vary substantially across models. Human evaluation under the identical pure-text protocol reproduces the same failure pattern, suggesting that the bottleneck arises from text-only working memory constraints rather than being specific to current LLM architectures. Our findings reframe pure-text spatial reasoning as a multi-axis world-modeling problem and motivate multimodal and scratchpad-augmented reasoning as future directions.

</details>


### [92] [ResearchLoop: An Evidence-Gated Control Plane for AI-Assisted Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28282)
*Yihan Xia,Taotao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ResearchLoop is an evidence-gated control plane for AI-assisted computational research that treats research components as durable project state with a repository-backed runtime, including a complete protocol specification, state model, transition rules, claim-admission algorithm, and insight-compounding mechanism, with experimental results across nine versions including self-hosting, controlled ablations, mathematical olympiad, and SciCode boundary experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI-assisted research compresses ideation, implementation, evaluation, and writing into a single loop, making paper claims easier to state than to audit, creating a publication risk that motivates the need for verifiable evidence-gated research workflows.

Method: ResearchLoop implements an evidence-gated control plane with durable project state (research questions, task contracts, evidence objects, claim ledgers, closeouts, paper bindings) backed by a repository runtime, with a complete protocol specification, state model, transition rules, claim-admission algorithm, and insight-compounding mechanism.

Result: Nine versions (V0-V9) were evaluated including a self-hosting case study, controlled task-suite study with component ablations, mathematical olympiad evaluation, and a supplementary SciCode boundary experiment using the official generated-code harness.

Conclusion: All artifacts, manifests, and verification reports are preserved in the project repository, establishing ResearchLoop as a verifiable framework for evidence-gated AI-assisted computational research.

Abstract: AI-assisted research compresses ideation, implementation, evaluation, and manuscript writing into a single interactive loop. This compression is useful, but it also creates a publication risk: paper claims can become easier to state than to audit. We present ResearchLoop, an evidence-gated control plane for AI-assisted computational research. ResearchLoop treats research questions, task contracts, evidence objects, claim ledgers, closeouts, and paper bindings as durable project state, realized here as a repository-backed runtime. This technical report provides the complete protocol specification, state model, transition rules, claim-admission algorithm, and insight-compounding mechanism. It also reports the full experimental record spanning nine versions (V0--V9), including a self-hosting case study, a controlled task-suite study with component ablations, a mathematical olympiad evaluation, and a supplementary SciCode boundary experiment evaluated with the official generated-code harness. All artifacts, manifests, and verification reports are preserved in the project repository.

</details>


### [93] [Where Rollouts Begin: Low-Load, High-Leverage First-Token Diversification for RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28295)
*Soeun Kim,Albert No*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: REFT improves RLVR training by diversifying rollouts via uniform sampling of first tokens after the reasoning marker from the policy's top-N candidates, boosting Pass@k across models and difficulty levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rollout diversity is a bottleneck in RLVR; existing methods ignore the first token after the reasoning marker as an unexplored position for broadening diversity without altering correctness signals.

Method: REFT samples first tokens uniformly from the policy's top-N candidates and evenly allocates rollouts, leaving all other RLVR components unchanged.

Result: REFT improves aggregate Pass@1, Pass@8, and Pass@64 over DAPO and GRPO baselines across four base models (0.5B-7B) and three difficulty regimes.

Conclusion: First-token diversification is a simple yet effective addition to RLVR pipelines, addressing a key bottleneck in rollout diversity.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) trains reasoning models without labeled trajectories, relying on grouped rollouts to expose the policy to alternative reasoning paths and a verifier to score them. Rollout diversity has accordingly emerged as a central bottleneck in RLVR, with most existing methods broadening exploration through temperature, prefix, or rollout-selection adjustments. We identify a structurally distinguished but overlooked position for broadening this diversity: the first token after the reasoning marker. The policy's first-token distribution exhibits a sharply peaked yet correctness-decoupled phenomenon, and this first token position can broaden the regions a rollout group covers without altering the correctness signal. We introduce REFT (Rollout Exploration with First-Token Diversification), a light addition to the RLVR pipeline that samples first tokens uniformly from the policy's own top-$N$ candidates and allocates rollouts evenly, leaving every other component unchanged. Trained on the resulting diversified rollouts, REFT improves aggregate Pass@1, Pass@8, and Pass@64 over DAPO and GRPO baselines across four base models (0.5B-7B) and three difficulty regimes.

</details>


### [94] [REED: Post-Training Representation Editing for Cross-Domain Linguistic Steganalysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28298)
*Ruohan Lei,Jianxin Gao,Wanli Peng,Huimin Pei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a post-training representation editing method for cross-domain linguistic steganalysis that edits intermediate representations before classification, using domain-offset vectors for adaptation and cover-to-stego direction for generalization, achieving high cross-domain detection performance without architecture modification or parameter updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cross-domain linguistic steganalysis methods suffer from unsatisfactory detection performance when tested on unseen domains with different vocabularies, topics, writing styles, and steganographic generation patterns.

Method: A post-training representation editing approach: (1) Train detector on source-domain data; (2) Freeze feature extractor and classifier; (3) For domain adaptation, construct a domain-offset vector from marginal source and target representations; (4) For domain generalization, derive a source-domain cover-to-stego direction to guide sample-specific editing.

Result: Achieves high cross-domain detection performance compared to advanced methods, particularly in F1-score, without requiring architecture modification or parameter updates after source-domain training.

Conclusion: The proposed post-training representation editing method effectively addresses cross-domain challenges in linguistic steganalysis by editing intermediate representations deterministically, offering a lightweight and effective solution for both domain adaptation and generalization scenarios.

Abstract: In real-world scenarios of linguistic steganalysis, tested texts usually come from unseen domains with different vocabularies, topics, writing styles, and steganographic generation patterns, which can significantly degrade the detection performance. Although existing cross-domain steganalysis methods can effectively alleviate this problem through distribution alignment, domain-invariant feature learning, etc., the detection performance is not satisfactory. In this paper, we propose a post-training representation editing method for cross-domain linguistic steganalysis. Specifically, the detector is first trained on source-domain data, and then the feature extractor and classifier are kept frozen, and the intermediate representations are deterministically edited before classification. For domain adaptation, we construct a domain-offset vector from marginal source and target representations. For domain generalization, we derive a source-domain cover-to-stego direction to guide sample-specific editing. Experimental results show that compared with the advanced methods, the proposed method can achieve high cross-domain detection performance, especially in terms of F1-score, while requiring no architecture modification or parameter updates after source-domain training.

</details>


### [95] [From Fact Overwriting to Knowledge Evolution: Causal Editing via On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28303)
*Shuaike Li,Kai Zhang,Xianquan Wang,Jiachen Liu,Shengpeng Mo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Knowledge Editing (KE) suffers from Epistemic Dissonance when forcibly injecting isolated facts, causing models to self-refute updates. The paper proposes CODE (Causal On-policy Distillation for Editing), which uses causal narratives and on-policy distillation to reduce self-refutation from 95.6% to 1.8% while maintaining strong multi-hop accuracy (up to 83.5%) on LLaMA-3.1 and Qwen-2.5.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Static Fact Overwriting in KE fractures pre-trained logical topologies, causing Epistemic Dissonance where legacy priors force the model to negate injected updates. This is an inherent structural flaw, not just noise, evidenced by a 95.6% self-refutation rate even with zero-distortion proxy.

Method: CODE (Causal On-policy Distillation for Editing) couples causal bootstrapping with asymmetric on-policy distillation to engrave causal transition logic directly into parametric memory, transforming discrete fact injection into coherent knowledge evolution grounded in explicit causal narratives.

Result: CODE reduces self-refutation rate from 95.6% to just 1.8% and achieves robust multi-hop accuracy up to 83.5% on LLaMA-3.1 and Qwen-2.5 models.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that grounding knowledge updates in causal narratives is essential for coherent knowledge evolution, advocating for a paradigm shift from Static Fact Overwriting to Causal Editing.

Abstract: While Knowledge Editing (KE) enables efficient updates, its dominant Static Fact Overwriting paradigm treats LLMs as discrete databases, forcibly injecting isolated facts. Fracturing pre-trained logical topologies, this triggers Epistemic Dissonance -- a pathology where un-evolved legacy priors force the model to explicitly negate the injected update. Idealized interventions reveal that this is an inherent structural flaw rather than mere algorithmic noise, with a zero-distortion proxy yielding a catastrophic 95.6% self-refutation rate. Given the causally driven nature of real-world knowledge, grounding updates in explicit causal narratives effectively collapses this conflict rate to just 6.6%, underscoring the imperative for a paradigm shift toward Causal Editing. To internalize this evolution, we propose CODE (Causal On-policy Distillation for Editing). By coupling causal bootstrapping with asymmetric on-policy distillation, CODE engraves causal transition logic directly into parametric memory. Experiments on LLaMA-3.1 and Qwen-2.5 show CODE drastically suppresses self-refutation to 1.8% while securing robust multi-hop accuracy (up to 83.5%), seamlessly transforming discrete fact injection into coherent knowledge evolution. Code is available at https://github.com/CrashBugger/CODE.

</details>


### [96] [An Enhanced Large Neighborhood Search Approach for the Capacitated Facility Location Problem with Incompatible Customers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28337)
*Ida Gjergji,Lucas Kletzander,Nysret Musliu,Andrea Schaerf*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a Large Neighborhood Search (LNS) method with hybrid destroy operators and exact repair to solve a capacitated facility location problem variant with customer incompatibilities, achieving new best solutions across all benchmark instances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address a recently introduced variant of the capacitated facility location problem that considers pairwise customer incompatibilities (e.g., hazardous materials, competing customers), which has practical relevance but lacks effective solution methods.

Method: A Large Neighborhood Search (LNS) framework with three hybrid combined destroy operators and an exact solver used in the repair phase.

Result: The proposed LNS method outperforms existing state-of-the-art metaheuristics and provides new best solutions for all available benchmark instances.

Conclusion: The hybrid LNS with exact repair is highly effective for the incompatibility-constrained capacitated facility location problem, dominating prior approaches on all test cases.

Abstract: A new variant of the classic capacitated facility location problem, which considers incompatibilities between customers, has recently been introduced in the literature. This problem captures the situation where given pairs of customers cannot be served by the same facility. Such a feature is crucial for many practical cases of location problems, such as the presence of hazardous or polluting materials and contention between competing costumers. In this paper, we propose a Large Neighborhood Search (LNS) method to solve this problem. Within the framework of LNS, we introduce three different destroy operators, which are combined in a hybrid manner, and we use an exact solver in the repair phase. Different algorithmic components are investigated for the design of LNS. The experimental analysis shows that our new method outperforms existing state-of-the-art metaheuristics, providing new best solutions for all available benchmark instances.

</details>


### [97] [SafeMed-R1: Clinician-Audited Safety and Ethics Alignment for Medical Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28338)
*Chao Ding,Mouxiao Bian,Tianbin Li,Minjia Yuan,Yidong Jiang,Yankai Jiang,Jinru Ding,Jiayuan Chen,Zhuangzhi Gao,Pengcheng Chen,Zhao He,Rongzhao Zhang,Meiling Liu,Luyi Jiang,Jie Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SafeMed-R1 is an LLM trained via a Clinical Trust Signals pipeline that links reasoning to clinician rubrics and edit histories, with safety/ethics alignment and red team testing. It achieves 79.6% macro-averaged accuracy, lower adversarial risk (3–5% fewer unsafe outputs), and matches PGY1/PGY2 residents on medical correctness while surpassing them on safety, guideline consistency, and clinical usefulness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs match expert performance on licensing exams but lack routine clinical use due to governance needs: auditable reasoning, safety/ethics alignment, and adversarial robustness.

Method: Trains SafeMed-R1 using a Clinical Trust Signals (CTS) pipeline with traceable reasoning linked to clinician rubric scores and edit histories, plus safety/ethics supervision and red team stress testing.

Result: 79.6% macro-averaged accuracy; lowest aggregated adversarial risk; ~3-5% reduction in unsafe outputs; matches PGY1/PGY2 residents on correctness; higher on medication safety, guideline consistency, and clinical usefulness in 30 medication safety vignettes.

Conclusion: Clinician-audited supervision provenance with domain-tailored safety/ethics alignment can strengthen governance-relevant evidence without relying on inference-time retrieval or citation grounding.

Abstract: Large language models(LLMs) increasingly match expert performance on licensing examinations, yet routine clinical use remains limited because governance requires auditable reasoning, safety and ethics alignment, and resilience to adversarial misuse. Here we present SafeMed-R1, trained with a traceable Clinical Trust Signals(CTS) pipeline that links each reasoning instance to clinician rubric scores and edit histories, and aligned through safety and ethics supervision and red team stress testing. SafeMed-R1 attains a macro-averaged accuracy of 79.6% across clinical benchmarks. Under adversarial safety testing, it shows the lowest aggregated risk and reduces unsafe outputs by about 3 to 5% relative to its baseline. In a paired expert study of 30 medication safety vignettes, SafeMed-R1 matches PGY1 and PGY2 residents on medical correctness and scores higher for medication safety, guideline consistency, and clinical usefulness. Collectively, these results suggest that clinician-audited supervision provenance, together with domain-tailored safety and ethics alignment, can strengthen governance-relevant evidence without relying on inference-time retrieval or citation grounding.

</details>


### [98] [Picid: A Modular Evaluation Infrastructure for Reproducible PHM Across Tasks and Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28345)
*Lev Telyatnikov,Raffael Theiler,Leandro Von Krannichfeldt,Olga Fink*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces PYCID, a modular evaluation infrastructure that formalizes the PHM evaluation pipeline as an explicit, executable, and reproducible protocol, enabling standardized, fair, and reproducible evaluation across diverse prognostics and health management tasks and datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PHM research lacks standardized and reusable evaluation practices, making reported results difficult to reproduce and compare due to ad hoc protocol choices in data splits, preprocessing, label alignment, temporal windowing, and metrics.

Method: The authors developed PYCID, a modular evaluation infrastructure that formalizes the PHM evaluation pipeline through well-defined abstractions, enforcing deterministic, leakage-safe dataset construction while remaining flexible across diverse PHM settings. It supports fault detection, diagnostics, and prognostics through a unified interface with standardized data contracts and evaluation boundaries.

Result: Empirical evaluation of thirteen models on twelve datasets spanning batteries, bearings, turbofan engines, hydraulics, filtration systems, and buildings demonstrates the framework's effectiveness.

Conclusion: PYCID establishes a reusable foundation for standardized, fair, and reproducible evaluation in PHM, enabling fair cross-task comparisons and consistent evaluation of identical model families across heterogeneous settings.

Abstract: Progress in Prognostics and Health Management (PHM) is hindered by the lack of standardized and reusable evaluation practices across tasks, datasets, and application domains. Reported results are often difficult to reproduce and compare, as key protocol choices, such as data splits, preprocessing, label alignment, temporal windowing, and metrics, are often implicit or implemented ad hoc. We introduce \picid, a modular evaluation infrastructure that formalizes the PHM evaluation pipeline as an explicit, executable, and reproducible protocol. Through well-defined abstractions, \picid enforces deterministic, leakage-safe dataset construction while remaining flexible across diverse PHM settings. The framework supports fault detection, diagnostics, and prognostics through a unified interface and can be extended to new datasets and model classes without violating protocol invariants. By standardizing data contracts and evaluation boundaries, \picid also enables fair cross-task comparisons across diagnostics (classification) and prognostics (regression), allowing identical model families to be evaluated consistently across heterogeneous settings. We demonstrate \picid through an empirical evaluation of thirteen models on twelve datasets spanning batteries, bearings, turbofan engines, hydraulics, filtration systems, and buildings. This work establishes a reusable foundation for standardized, fair and reproducible evaluation in PHM.

</details>


### [99] [FedMPT: Federated Multi-label Prompt Tuning of Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28347)
*Xucong Wang,Pengkun Wang,Zhe Zhao,Liheng Yu,Shuang Wang,Yang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FedMPT is the first federated learning method for multi-label recognition using VLMs, employing a causal front-door adjustment with LLM-driven conditional prompts and optimal transport to mitigate spurious label correlations in decentralized heterogeneous settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated multi-label recognition with VLMs suffers from overfitting to spurious label correlations due to private and heterogeneous client data, causing irrelevant category activations on new samples.

Method: Proposes FedMPT with: (1) a causal front-door adjustment to decouple MLR via intermediate variables; (2) an LLM-driven pipeline to decode conditions governing label dependencies; (3) optimal transport between condition-enriched prompts and image patches for region-level semantics; (4) a gating mechanism for synergistic multi-condition predictions.

Result: Achieves competitive results and outperforms SOTA methods across multiple benchmark datasets under varied federated settings.

Conclusion: Causal modeling with conditional decoupling effectively mitigates spurious label correlations in federated MLR, with FedMPT establishing the first specialized solution for this problem.

Abstract: Multi-Label Recognition (MLR) based on Vision-Language Models (VLMs) aims to leverage their pre-trained knowledge to better adapt complex recognition scenarios, thereby enhancing model robustness. However, for realistic decentralized applications requiring federated learning, adapting VLMs to each client that possesses private and heterogeneous data can cause the model to overfit spurious label correlations, consequently triggering irrelevant categories when encountering new samples. To tackle this problem, we reconsider the federated learning for MLR with a causal model, in which we adopt a front-door adjustment and decouple the MLR modeling process by intermediate variables that magnify the oracle label co-occurrence. Guided by our analysis, we propose our FedMPT, the first method specifically designed for federated MLR. The core idea of FedMPT is to leverage generalizable conditions to steer federated MLR to mitigate erroneous label activations. To achieve this, FedMPT introduces an Large Language Model (LLM)-driven pipeline to decipher the underlying conditions that govern label dependencies. Furthermore, we introduce an optimal transport between the condition-enriched prompts and the image patches to uncover multiple region-level semantics. Finally, we generate synergistic predictions from different conditions with a crafted gating mechanism. Experiments on multiple benchmark datasets show that our proposed approach achieves competitive results and outperforms SOTA methods under varied settings.

</details>


### [100] [Plan Before Search: Search Agents Need Plan](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28354)
*Zhipeng Qian,Zihan Liang,Yufei Ma,Ben Chen,Huangyu Dai,Jiayi Ji,Chenyi Lei,Wenwu Ou,Xiaoshuai Sun,Qibin Hou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies Plan, a structured agentic behavior for multi-hop retrieval that decomposes questions into ordered sub-questions before retrieval. The authors find that identical reward signals cause different RL failure modes across model families, and propose a self-bootstrapping paradigm using a small-scale seed model to activate Plan in any target model, eliminating distillation from stronger models. Their pipeline outperforms baselines on multi-hop QA benchmarks across models from 3B to 14B parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RL training paradigms for retrieval-augmented reasoning overlook two key factors: the dependency structure among sub-skills and the possibility that distillation is not the only route to capability acquisition. The authors aim to address these gaps.

Method: The authors propose Plan, which decomposes a question into ordered sub-questions before any retrieval, anchoring each search step to a pre-designed sub-question. They also propose a self-bootstrapping paradigm where a small-scale seed model generates filtered trajectories to activate Plan in any target model, avoiding distillation from stronger models.

Result: Across three model families (3B to 14B parameters), identical reward signals induce qualitatively different RL failure modes. The proposed self-bootstrapping pipeline activates Plan across all tested models and consistently outperforms competitive baselines on multi-hop QA benchmarks.

Conclusion: Successful training depends not only on reward design but also on model-specific feasibility conditions (sufficient initial entropy, training stability, prerequisite sub-skills). The self-bootstrapping paradigm effectively eliminates the need for distillation from external stronger models.

Abstract: Training large language models as retrieval-augmented reasoning agents typically combines reinforcement learning with an SFT cold start distilled from a stronger model. However, this paradigm overlooks two fundamental factors: the dependency structure among sub-skills, and the possibility that distillation is not the only route to capability acquisition. We study this through Plan, a structured agentic behavior for multi-hop retrieval that decomposes a question into ordered sub-questions before any retrieval is performed, so that each search step can be anchored to a pre-designed sub-question instead of drifting under the influence of partially relevant documents retrieved earlier. However, across three model families spanning 3B to 14B parameters, we find that an identical reward signal induces qualitatively different RL failure modes. This phenomenon indicates that successful training hinges not only on reward design but also on model-specific feasibility conditions: sufficient initial entropy, training stability, and prerequisite sub-skills. Motivated by this, we propose a self-bootstrapping paradigm in which a small-scale seed model generates filtered trajectories that activate Plan in any target model, eliminating the need for distillation from an external stronger model. Our pipeline activates Plan across every tested model and consistently outperforms competitive baselines on multi-hop QA benchmarks.

</details>


### [101] [From Knowing to Doing: A Memory-Controlled Benchmark for LLM Trading Agents on Stock Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28359)
*Taojie Zhu,Wentao Zhao,Rui Sun,Beidi Luan,Jiacheng Lu,Sinuo Wang,Jing Li,Daxin Jiang,Yonghong He,Zuo Bai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KTD-Fin is a benchmark that addresses two key failures in evaluating LLM trading agents: data leakage from knowledge cutoff overlaps with backtest periods, and confusing raw returns with genuine stock-selection skill. It uses identifier masking and Barra-style performance attribution to disentangle market/style exposure from true alpha, finding that LLM agents' returns are mostly passive exposure rather than transferable skill.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of LLM trading agents suffer from (1) backtest periods overlapping with model knowledge cutoffs, causing memorized market information to masquerade as reasoning, and (2) raw portfolio returns conflating market beta/style exposure with genuine stock-selection ability (alpha).

Method: KTD-Fin implements (a) a data-side masking protocol that anonymizes tickers, dates, and calendar info across prompts and tools, and (b) a Barra-style performance attribution framework decomposing returns into market, style, and stock-selection alpha components. Evaluated on ten frontier LLMs over CSI300 (2024-2026).

Result: Masking changes agent rationales from memorized narratives to anonymized factor-based reasoning. Attribution reveals that LLM agents' cumulative returns under leakage-controlled evaluation are largely explained by passive market and style exposure, with limited evidence of persistent stock-selection alpha.

Conclusion: Financial LLM benchmarks should evaluate not just whether an agent makes money, but the source of returns. KTD-Fin provides a reproducible template for leakage-controlled, attribution-aware evaluation of LLM trading agents.

Abstract: Evaluating whether large language model (LLM) agents can profit in capital markets is increasingly framed as end-to-end trading: place an agent in a historical market, let it trade, and measure portfolio returns. This setup is vulnerable to two evaluation failures. First, long backtests often overlap with the knowledge cutoffs of frontier LLMs, allowing memorized tickers, dates, prices, and market narratives to substitute for investment reasoning. Second, raw returns are a noisy proxy for stock-selection ability, since positive performance may come from market beta, style exposure, or favorable regimes rather than genuine alpha.
  We introduce KTD-Fin (Knowing-To-Doing Financial Benchmark), an end-to-end stock-market trading benchmark that addresses both issues. KTD-Fin uses a data-side masking protocol to anonymize key identifiers and calendar information consistently across prompts and tools, separating historical market memory from investment decision-making. It also incorporates a Barra-style performance attribution framework that decomposes portfolio returns into market, style, and stock-selection alpha components.
  Across ten frontier LLM agents evaluated on the Chinese CSI300 over a 2024--2026 window, masking substantially changes agent rationales, pushing them towards anonymized factor-based reasoning. Attribution analysis further shows that LLM agents' cumulative returns under leakage-controlled evaluation are largely explained by passive market and style exposure, with limited evidence of persistent stock-selection alpha. These findings suggest that financial LLM benchmarks should evaluate not only whether an agent makes money, but also whether the source of returns reflects transferable investment skill. We release KTD-Fin as a reproducible template for leakage-controlled and attribution-aware evaluation of LLM trading agents.

</details>


### [102] [Prompt Codebooks: Discrete Compositional Optimization for Language Model Instruction Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28360)
*Jyotirmoy Nath,Neeraj Kumar,Brejesh Lall*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Prompt Codebooks (PCO) recasts automatic prompt optimization as discrete learning over a finite vocabulary of reusable natural-language "instincts," enabling per-instance compositional prompt construction that outperforms prior methods while drastically reducing prompt length.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing APO methods treat prompts as monolithic, instance-blind strings optimized through global edits, producing brittle updates and preventing reuse of learned sub-behaviors.

Method: PCO organizes prompt-construction knowledge in a discrete codebook of atomic instruction units; an LLM-based encoder routes each input to a small subset of entries; a generator composes them into a prompt; a critic emits structured verdicts with per-variable textual gradients, jointly training all components under a language-valued min-max objective.

Result: On six benchmarks with Qwen3-8B and LLaMA-3.1-8B, PCO improves over zero-shot by up to +30.36 points, surpasses GEPA by +3.34 on HotpotQA and +1.11 aggregate, and reduces deployed prompt length by up to 14.1× vs MIPROv2 and 3.0× vs GEPA using only K=16 instincts.

Conclusion: PCO demonstrates that decomposing prompts into reusable atomic instincts with per-instance routing yields more effective, efficient, and interpretable prompt optimization than monolithic instance-blind approaches.

Abstract: Automatic prompt optimization (APO) has driven significant gains in LLM-based agentic workflows. However, existing methods treat each task's prompt as a monolithic, instance-blind string optimized through global edits, producing brittle updates and preventing the reuse of learned sub-behaviors. We propose Prompt Codebooks (PCO), a novel compositional prompt optimization framework that recasts APO as discrete learning over a finite vocabulary of natural-language instincts - atomic, reusable instruction units. PCO organizes prompt-construction knowledge in a discrete codebook and routes each input to a small subset of entries via an LLM-based encoder; a generator composes them into a prompt for the frozen target model; a critic emits a structured verdict that decomposes by attribution into per-variable textual gradients, jointly training the encoder, generator, and codebook under a language-valued min-max objective. The resulting routing is per-instance: different inputs in the same task receive different instinct compositions, a regime structurally inexpressible under instance-blind methods. Across six benchmarks on Qwen3-8B and LLaMA-3.1-8B, PCO improves over zero-shot by up to +30.36 points, surpasses the strongest prior baseline (GEPA) by +3.34 on HotpotQA and +1.11 in aggregate, and reduces deployed prompt length by up to 14.1x versus MIPROv2 and 3.0x versus GEPA using only K=16 instincts.

</details>


### [103] [Risk-Controlled Lean-as-Judge for Natural-Language Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28365)
*Pauline Bourigault,Xiaotong Ji,Matthieu Zimmer,Rasul Tutunov,Haitham Bou Ammar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes Lean's partial signal for judging math answers: high coverage yields 96% correct, low coverage only 20%; a 7B autoformalizer proves only 28% of problems with ~43% faithfulness. COVCAL certifies selective risk bounds on accepted answers, enabling trust when formalizer coverage is sufficient (79%) but abstaining when sparse (28%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lean's formal verification signal for natural-language math answers is partial and potentially unfaithful; the authors aim to quantify when and under what conditions this signal can be trusted under risk control.

Method: COVCAL: a selector over Lean-trace diagnostics that certifies a finite-sample selective-risk bound on accepted answers (or abstains), using a conservative Bonferroni bound and a tighter dev-then-cal rule. Evaluated on MATH-500 with two autoformalizers (7B general vs. prover-specialized).

Result: (i) Proof-winning answer is 96% correct at high proved coverage vs. 20% at low coverage. (ii) 7B formalizer proves only 28% of problems (~43% faithful); prover-specialized formalizer reaches 79% coverage. Bonferroni abstains on all 20 bootstrap partitions for 7B, but with 79% coverage the method accepts ~48% of problems at 0.98 accepted accuracy on 17/20 partitions.

Conclusion: A partial formal signal can be trusted under risk control only when autoformalization coverage is sufficiently high; self-consistency alone is 91% accurate, but COVCAL provides a precise certified guarantee for when formal verification is reliable.

Abstract: Lean is increasingly used to judge natural-language mathematical answers, but its signal is partial: many answers never formalize, and a failed proof may reflect an ill-typed statement or a missing library fact, not a wrong answer. On MATH-500 we show this signal is (i) sharply coverage-dependent, that is the proof-winning answer is correct 96% of the time at high proved coverage but 20% at low, and (ii) sparse and often unfaithful: a 7B autoformalizer proves a class for only 28% of problems, and a manual audit finds only approximately 43% of those proofs faithful. We propose COVCAL, a selector over Lean-trace diagnostics that certifies a finite-sample selective-risk bound on accepted answers or abstains, under two regimes (a conservative Bonferroni bound and a tighter dev-then-cal rule). Feasibility depends on autoformalization coverage: with the 7B formalizer the signal is too sparse and Bonferroni abstains on all 20 bootstrap partitions, whereas a prover-specialized formalizer reaches 79% coverage and flips it to feasible on 17 of 20, accepting approximately 48% of problems at 0.98 accepted accuracy. Since self-consistency alone is already 91% accurate, our contribution is a precise account of when, and with which formalizer, a partial formal signal can be trusted under risk control.

</details>


### [104] [CyberJurors: A Multi-Agent Simulation Task for E-Commerce Disputes Verdict](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28369)
*Yanhui Sun,Wu Liu,Haifeng Ming,Xinru Wang,Hantao Yao,Yongdong Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CyberJurors is a multi-agent framework for E-commerce dispute verdicts that uses Individual Verdict Chain-of-Thought (4-stage reasoning) and Jury Consensus Verdict (multi-round discussion/voting with precedents) to outperform existing LLMs and court simulators on the new VerdictBench benchmark (6,000 real-world cases).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: E-commerce platforms need crowdsourced jurors to adjudicate massive transaction disputes, but existing methods fail due to the need to ground pivotal clues from redundant, multi-round, multimodal evidence under flexible platform-specific conventions, unlike formal legal judgment.

Method: Proposes CyberJurors, a multi-agent framework with two levels: (1) Individual Verdict Chain-of-Thought that decomposes EDV into four structured reasoning stages for fine-grained clue perception and causal logic clarification; (2) Jury Consensus Verdict that simulates multi-round discussion and voting among jurors while incorporating verdict precedents to mitigate cognitive biases.
