Result: CyberJurors outperforms state-of-the-art LLMs, MLLMs, and court simulators on VerdictBench, achieving stronger alignment with real-world jury voting patterns.

Conclusion: The proposed multi-agent framework effectively addresses the unique challenges of E-commerce dispute verdicts by combining structured individual reasoning with collective deliberation mechanisms.

Abstract: E-commerce platforms have begun recruiting crowdsourced jurors to adjudicate massive volumes of transaction disputes. Unlike formal legal judgment, E-commerce dispute verdicts require grounding pivotal clues from redundant, multi-round, multimodal evidence and making decisions under flexible platform-specific conventions. These characteristics render existing methods insufficient for this scenario. To bridge this gap, we introduce a pioneering task, E-commerce Dispute Verdicts (EDV), and present VerdictBench, a multimodal benchmark comprising 6,000 real-world cases designed to reflect crowdsourced jury decisions. Building upon this, we propose CyberJurors, a multi-agent framework to clarify the dispute logic and regulate the verdict process. At the individual level, Individual Verdict Chain-of-Thought decomposes the EDV task into four structured reasoning stages, enabling fine-grained clue perception and clarifying causal logic between pivotal clues and the dispute focus. At the collective level, Jury Consensus Verdict simulates multi-round discussion and voting among jurors, while incorporating verdict precedents to mitigate cognitive biases toward either disputant. Experiments on VerdictBench show that CyberJurors outperforms state-of-the-art LLMs, MLLMs, and court simulators, while achieving stronger alignment with real-world jury voting patterns. Code and dataset are available at https://github.com/YanhuiS/CyberJurors and https://huggingface.co/datasets/piggi/VerdictBench.

</details>


### [105] [From paper to benchmark: agentic, framework-based reproduction of under-specified methods in machine health intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28371)
*Raffael Theiler,Ludovico Comito,David Leko,Leandro Von Krannichfeldt,Lev Telyatnikov,Olga Fink*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces an agentic, framework-based approach for reproducing PHM papers by using a slot-binding interface that maps paper components into a shared benchmark framework, enabling consistent and comparable implementations across 16 evaluated papers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reproducing under-specified methods in PHM is difficult due to restricted access to industrial datasets, incomplete reporting of preprocessing/evaluation protocols, and implicit design choices; existing paper-to-code systems generate isolated artifacts that are not directly comparable.

Method: The approach uses an agent that translates papers into a shared PHM benchmark framework via a slot-binding interface, which maps equations and protocol descriptions into structured components (task definitions, dataset adapters, windowing, targets, models, evaluators) while explicitly recording unresolved assumptions.

Result: Evaluated on 16 PHM papers, the framework-enhanced agentic reproduction outperforms framework-free agents in reproduction success, model-based code evaluation, framework binding of assumptions, and cross-paper benchmark comparability under standardized protocols.

Conclusion: Coupling agentic generation with a shared framework transforms paper reproduction from isolated code synthesis into executable, assumption-aware, and systematically comparable benchmark implementations.

Abstract: Industrial Prognostics and Health Management (PHM) provides a representative case study for a broader challenge in applied machine learning: translating published papers into executable, benchmark-ready implementations. Reproducing under-specified methods in PHM is particularly difficult due to restricted access to industrial datasets, incomplete reporting of preprocessing and evaluation protocols, and implicit design choices (e.g., windowing, target construction, data splits) that critically affect performance. Existing paper-to-code systems generate implementations for individual papers, but these artifacts are often not directly comparable due to inconsistencies in assumptions and evaluation settings. We introduce \emph{agentic, framework-based PHM paper reproduction}, where an agent translates a paper into a shared PHM benchmark framework via a \emph{slot-binding interface}. This interface maps equations and protocol descriptions into structured components (task definitions, dataset adapters, windowing, targets, models, and evaluators), while explicitly recording unresolved assumptions. The resulting implementations are validated against standardized task contracts and evaluation hooks, enabling consistent and comparable benchmarking. We evaluate this approach on 16 PHM papers, comparing framework-enhanced, skill-based and prompt-based agentic reproduction against a recent framework-free paper-reproduction agent. We assess reproduction success, model-based code evaluation, framework binding of paper assumptions, and cross-paper benchmark comparability under standardized protocols. Our results show that coupling agentic generation with a shared framework transforms paper reproduction from isolated code synthesis into executable, assumption-aware, and systematically comparable benchmark implementations.

</details>


### [106] [Mechanistically Interpreting the Role of Sample Difficulty in RLVR for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28388)
*Yue Cheng,Jiajun Zhang,Xiaohui Gao,Weiwei Xing,Zheng Wang,Zhanxing Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates the role of sample difficulty in Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Reward (RLVR) for LLMs, finding that easy and medium-difficulty problems yield the best reasoning improvements, while overly hard problems can degrade performance. Using T-SAE analysis, they reveal difficulty-dependent internal feature dynamics and propose adaptive strategies for hard samples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the mechanistic role of sample difficulty in RLVR, which empirically improves LLM reasoning but whose underlying dynamics are poorly understood.

Method: Conducted difficulty-wise and one-sample analysis of RLVR; used Temporal Sparse Autoencoders (T-SAE) to analyze internal feature dynamics across different difficulty levels; proposed difficulty-adaptive strategies (backward-reasoning reformulation and T-SAE-guided training signals).

Result: Easy and medium-difficulty problems produce strongest/most stable reasoning improvements; hard problems yield weak signals, cause degenerate behaviors (answer repetition, skipped computation), and degrade existing capabilities. T-SAE reveals easy problems reinforce direct-answer and basic-computation features while suppressing deliberative reasoning; hard problems only become useful with successful trajectories; medium problems provide balanced signals.

Conclusion: Sample difficulty is a key factor governing optimization dynamics and representation evolution in RLVR.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Reward (RLVR) is empirically shown to notably enhance the reasoning performance of large language models (LLMs), particularly in mathematics and programming. However, the mechanistic role of Sample Difficulty in RLVR remains poorly understood. In this paper, we investigate RLVR through the lens of difficulty-wise and one-sample analysis. We find that sample difficulty has a non-monotonic effect on RLVR: easy and medium-difficulty problems yield the strongest and most stable reasoning improvements, whereas overly hard problems often provide weak learning signals, induce degenerate behaviors such as answer repetition or skipping necessary computation, and can ultimately degrade the model's pre-existing capabilities. Beyond the obverse of response, we further analyze the model's internal feature dynamics using Temporal Sparse Autoencoders (T-SAE). Easy problems mainly reinforce direct-answer and basic-computation features while suppressing deliberative-reasoning features; hard problems activate reasoning-related features but become useful only when successful trajectories are sampled; medium-difficulty problems provide a more balanced signal, strengthening both computation and multi-step reasoning features. Motivated by these findings, we propose difficulty-adaptive strategies for hard-sample utilization, using backward-reasoning reformulation and T-SAE-guided training signals to improve reward density and credit assignment during RLVR. Overall, our results identify sample difficulty as a key factor governing both the optimization dynamics and representation evolution of RLVR.

</details>


### [107] [HRBench: Benchmarking and Understanding Thinking-Mode Switch Strategies in Hybrid-Reasoning LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28398)
*Yansong Ning,Mianpeng Liu,Jingwen Ye,Weidong Zhang,Hao Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HRBench is a unified evaluation framework for studying thinking-mode switching in hybrid-reasoning LLMs, organizing 12 controlled settings across three switching strategies and four training regimes, evaluated on 6 LLMs and 5 benchmarks, revealing distinct trade-off regions for different strategies and how training, model scale, and task domain affect optimal choices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for adaptive thinking-mode selection in hybrid-reasoning LLMs are evaluated under inconsistent models, datasets, and implementation assumptions, making fair comparison difficult. There is a need for a unified benchmark to systematically compare different switching strategies under controlled conditions.

Method: HRBench organizes the design space along two axes: (1) three switching strategy families—prompt-based selection, external routing, and speculative execution; and (2) four training regimes—training-free, SFT, offline RL, and online RL—yielding 12 controlled settings. It reimplements 12+ prior methods and evaluates across 6 LLMs (Qwen3.5-2B to Kimi-K2.5-1.1T) and 5 reasoning benchmarks in math, science, and code.

Result: Different switching strategies occupy distinct effectiveness-efficiency trade-off regions: prompt-based methods offer favorable token-accuracy trade-offs, routing methods provide more stable cost reduction, and speculative methods improve accuracy at higher token cost. Training affects strategies differently, and the preferred strategy varies with model scale and task domain.

Conclusion: HRBench provides reference implementations and a unified evaluation platform to support more controlled research on efficient reasoning in hybrid-reasoning LLMs, with open-sourced data, code, and repository.

Abstract: Hybrid-reasoning large language models (LLMs) expose explicit controls over reasoning effort, allowing users or systems to trade off answer quality against inference cost. However, existing methods for adaptive thinking-mode selection are typically evaluated under different models, datasets, and implementation assumptions, making it difficult to compare their practical behavior. We introduce HRBench, a unified evaluation framework for studying thinking-mode switching in hybrid-reasoning LLMs. HRBench organizes the design space along two axes: three switching strategy families, prompt-based selection, external routing, and speculative execution, and four training regimes, training-free, SFT, offline and online RL, yielding 12 controlled evaluation settings. We evaluate these settings across 6 LLMs, from Qwen3.5-2B to Kimi-K2.5-1.1T, and 5 reasoning benchmarks covering mathematics, science, and code, while reimplementing 12+ representative prior methods within the same pipeline. Our analysis characterizes how different switching strategies occupy distinct effectiveness-efficiency trade-off regions: prompt-based methods often provide favorable token-accuracy trade-offs, routing methods offer more stable cost reduction, and speculative methods tend to improve accuracy at higher token cost. We further find that training affects strategies differently, and that the preferred strategy varies with model scale and task domain. HRBench provides reference implementations and a unified evaluation platform to support more controlled research on efficient reasoning in hybrid-reasoning LLMs. Our data, code and repository are available at https://github.com/usail-hkust/HRBench.

</details>


### [108] [Measuring Progress Toward AGI: A Cognitive Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28405)
*Ryan Burnell,Yumeya Yamamori,Orhan Firat,Kate Olszewska,Steph Hughes-Fitt,Oran Kelly,Isaac R. Galatzer-Levy,Meredith Ringel Morris,Allan Dafoe,Alison M. Snyder,Noah D. Goodman,Matthew Botvinick,Shane Legg*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a framework for measuring AGI progress by decomposing general intelligence into 10 cognitive faculties based on psychology/neuroscience/cognitive science, and evaluating systems via a cognitive profile from targeted held-out tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is no clear framework for measuring AGI progress, fueling subjective claims, hindering progress tracking, and risking irresponsible governance.

Method: The authors introduce a Cognitive Taxonomy deconstructing general intelligence into 10 key cognitive faculties, then propose an evaluation protocol using a suite of held-out cognitive tasks to generate a 'cognitive profile' of a system's strengths and weaknesses.

Result: A practical roadmap and initial step toward rigorous, empirical evaluation of AGI, producing a cognitive profile for understanding system capabilities relative to human cognition.

Conclusion: This framework serves as a starting point for more rigorous, empirical AGI evaluation, drawing from established cognitive science to enable objective progress measurement.

Abstract: Despite widespread discussion of AGI, there is no clear framework for measuring progress toward it. This ambiguity fuels subjective claims, makes it difficult to track progress, and risks hindering responsible governance. As a starting point to address this gap, we present a framework for understanding system capabilities in relation to human cognitive abilities. Drawing from decades of research in psychology, neuroscience, and cognitive science, we introduce a Cognitive Taxonomy that deconstructs general intelligence into 10 key cognitive faculties. We then propose a rigorous evaluation protocol in which a system's performance is measured across a suite of targeted, held-out cognitive tasks, generating a 'cognitive profile' that can be used to understand a system's strengths and weaknesses. We hope this framework will provide a practical roadmap and an initial step toward more rigorous, empirical evaluation of AGI.

</details>


### [109] [Efficient Post-training of LLMs for Code Generation With Offline Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28409)
*Mingze Wu,Abhinav Anand,Shweta Verma,Mira Mezini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper explores offline reinforcement learning as an alternative to online RL for training code-generating LLMs, showing it's effective especially for small models and hard problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online RL for code generation is resource-intensive due to LLM inference and output verification. The authors aim to find a more efficient training approach using offline RL that leverages existing code datasets.

Method: The authors apply offline reinforcement learning to code-generating LLMs by utilizing existing code datasets, rather than generating new samples through online inference and verification.

Result: Offline RL is an effective training strategy for improving LLM performance in code generation. It is especially beneficial for small LLMs and challenging coding problems.

Conclusion: Offline RL can serve as a viable and efficient alternative to online RL for post-training code-generating LLMs, reducing computational overhead while still improving model performance.

Abstract: Post-training using online reinforcement learning (RL) is an important training step for LLMs, including code-generating models. However, online RL for code generation involves LLM inference and verification of the generated output, which can take considerable time and resources. In this paper, we explore the application of offline RL to code-generating models by leveraging existing code datasets. Our experiments demonstrate that offline RL is an effective training strategy for improving LLM performance. We show that offline RL can be especially beneficial for small LLMs and challenging coding problems.

</details>


### [110] [DenoiseRL: Bootstrapping Reasoning Models to Recover from Noisy Prefixes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28421)
*Caijun Xu,Changyi Xiao,Zhongyuan Peng,Yixin Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DenoiseRL is a reinforcement learning framework that improves large language model reasoning by learning directly from incorrect reasoning traces (failures from weak models) using recovery-oriented optimization, eliminating the need for stronger teacher models or curated datasets, and achieving superior performance on math and general reasoning benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL methods for LLM reasoning still depend on stronger teacher models or heavily curated difficult datasets, limiting scalable capability improvement. The goal is to reduce reliance on external supervision and expensive data curation.

Method: DenoiseRL substitutes external supervision with recovery-oriented optimization over failures from weak models. It converts incorrect reasoning traces into improvement opportunities, generating richer and more diverse learning signals to improve exploration efficiency from imperfect model behavior.

Result: DenoiseRL consistently outperforms strong on-policy RL baselines across competitive mathematical and general reasoning benchmarks, and promotes stronger self-corrective behavior as training difficulty increases.

Conclusion: Learning from incorrect reasoning traces via recovery-oriented optimization provides an effective and scalable alternative pathway for improving reasoning in LLMs, reducing the need for expensive data curation or stronger teacher models.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning has become a central paradigm for advancing reasoning in large language models, yet most existing methods still depend on stronger teacher models or heavily curated difficult datasets, limiting scalable capability improvement. In this paper, we introduce DenoiseRL, a reinforcement learning framework that substitutes external supervision with recovery-oriented optimization over failures from weak models. Instead of relying on stronger supervision or carefully engineered data, DenoiseRL learns directly from incorrect reasoning traces by converting them into opportunities for improvement, making training more scalable and less dependent on external resources. This yields a richer and more diverse learning signal, improving exploration efficiency from imperfect model behavior. As a result, DenoiseRL improves reasoning performance and overall training efficiency while reducing the need for expensive data curation or stronger teacher models. Empirically, DenoiseRL consistently outperforms strong on-policy RL baselines across competitive mathematical and general reasoning benchmarks and promotes stronger self-corrective behavior as training difficulty increases, highlighting an effective and scalable alternative pathway for improving reasoning in large language models.

</details>


### [111] [GONDOR to the Rescue: Satisficing Planning with Low Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28454)
*Yonatan Vernik,Alexander Tuisov,Alexander Shleyfman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GONDOR is a memory-efficient extension of GBFS that compresses the search tree periodically using anchor states and reconstructs paths via re-searching, improving coverage under strict memory constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard GBFS struggles under tight memory constraints (e.g., edge devices) because it stores the full search tree. The paper aims to enable heuristic search to continue effectively under strict memory limits.

Method: GONDOR periodically compresses the search tree into a sparse set of anchor (outpost) states, discarding intermediate nodes. Upon reaching the goal, it reconstructs the full path by re-searching between consecutive anchor states. Multiple outpost selection policies are studied, and Bloom filters are explored for compact duplicate detection in the closed list.

Result: Experiments across numeric planning domains and heuristic configurations show GONDOR consistently improves coverage under low memory budgets compared to standard GBFS.

Conclusion: GONDOR is an effective memory-efficient variant of GBFS for resource-constrained settings, with open-source implementation released for further research.

Abstract: Greedy Best-First Search (GBFS) is the dominant approach for solving search problems where the goal can be estimated with a heuristic, such as planning, route finding, navigation, and pathfinding. This is especially true when the memory is tightly constrained, such as planning on edge devices. To alleviate that, we present GONDOR (Greedy Online Navigation with Dynamic Outpost-based Re-search), a memory-efficient extension of GBFS that allows search to continue under strict memory limits by periodically compressing the search tree while retaining a sparse set of anchor states, then upon reaching the goal reconstructs the path by re-searching between the sparse states. We analyze the algorithm and discuss several variants defined by different outpost selection policies. In addition, we explore using Bloom filters for compact duplicate detection in the closed list. Experiments across numeric planning domains and heuristic configurations show that GONDOR consistently improves coverage under low memory budgets compared to standard GBFS. We release the implementation of GONDOR and the Bloom-filter variant to facilitate further research on memory-efficient heuristic search.

</details>


### [112] [Diffusion Large Language Models for Visual Speech Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28456)
*Jeong Hun Yeo,Chae Won Kim,Hyeongseop Rha,Yong Man Ro*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DLLM-VSR is a novel VSR framework using Diffusion Large Language Models with iterative masked denoising and flexible-order decoding, achieving state-of-the-art 19.5% WER on LRS3.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VSR systems use left-to-right autoregressive decoding that forces premature decisions on visually ambiguous tokens before sufficient context is available.

Method: (1) Formulates transcription as iterative masked denoising with flexible-order decoding using DLLM; (2) Confidence-based unmasking commits high-confidence positions early and uses them as bidirectional context; (3) Two-stage masked-denoising training strategy separating visual-to-text alignment from length modeling; (4) Length-guided candidate decoding using video duration for transcript-length hypotheses, decoding under multiple hypotheses, and reranking based on length plausibility and confidence.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art WER of 19.5% on LRS3 using only its labeled training data.

Conclusion: The proposed DLLM-based VSR with iterative masked denoising and length-guided decoding outperforms autoregressive approaches, and reducing target-length uncertainty is key for further improvement.

Abstract: Existing Visual Speech Recognition (VSR) systems commonly rely on left-to-right autoregressive decoding, which can force premature decisions on visually ambiguous tokens before sufficient context is available. We propose DLLM-VSR, to the best of our knowledge, the first Diffusion Large Language Model (DLLM)-based VSR framework, formulating transcription as iterative masked denoising with flexible-order decoding. With confidence-based unmasking, DLLM-VSR commits high-confidence positions early and uses the committed tokens as bidirectional context to refine ambiguous ones. To adapt DLLMs to VSR, we introduce a two-stage masked-denoising training strategy that separates visual-to-text content alignment from length modeling. We further observe a performance gap with oracle-length decoding, which assumes access to the true transcript length, indicating that reducing target-length uncertainty can improve DLLM-based VSR. To reduce this gap, we develop length-guided candidate decoding, which uses video duration to construct plausible transcript-length hypotheses, decodes under multiple hypotheses, and reranks candidates using length plausibility and decoding confidence. The proposed method achieves a state-of-the-art WER of 19.5\% on LRS3 using only its labeled training data.

</details>


### [113] [From Learning Resources to Competencies: LLM-Based Tagging with Evidence and Graph Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28483)
*Ngoc Luyen Le,Marie-Hélène Abel,Bertrand Laforge*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An end-to-end pipeline (LLM+BM25+Graph) aligns LMS learning resources with a structured competency framework by segmenting content, retrieving candidate competencies, using an LLM as a constrained evidence-producing tagger, and refining predictions via the competency graph, achieving superior performance (fragment-level micro-F1 0.57, macro-F1 0.50; resource-level macro-F1 0.51; MRR 0.82) over baselines while providing traceable evidence for auditing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual tagging of learning resources to competency frameworks is labor-intensive, and fully automatic methods lack transparency, motivating the need for an efficient, interpretable alignment approach.

Method: The pipeline segments LMS resources (instructional content and assessments) into pedagogical fragments, retrieves candidate competencies from structured profiles using BM25 with graph-based context enrichment, employs an LLM as a constrained evidence-producing tagger to select the best competencies and extract supporting evidence spans, and refines predictions using the competency graph structure, aggregating at the resource level.

Result: The LBG pipeline achieves fragment-level micro-F1 of 0.57 and macro-F1 of 0.50, resource-level macro-F1 of 0.51, and MRR of 0.82, outperforming zero-shot/few-shot LLM variants, retrieval/similarity baselines, and supervised classifiers.

Conclusion: The proposed pipeline provides an effective and transparent solution for competency-resource alignment, producing mechanically traceable evidence spans that support human auditing and educational analysis.

Abstract: Linking learning resources to a structured competency framework is key to enabling competency-based search and curriculum analytics in Learning Management Systems (LMS). However, manual tagging is labor-intensive, and fully automatic methods often lack transparency. In this paper, we present an end-to-end alignment pipeline that uses a large language model (LLM) as a constrained, evidence-producing tagger. LMS resources -both instructional content and assessments -are first segmented into meaningful pedagogical fragments. For each fragment, a small set of candidate competencies is retrieved from structured competency profiles enriched with graph-based context. The LLM then selects the most relevant competencies from this set and provides supporting evidence spans from the fragment text. These predictions are refined using the structure of the competency graph and aggregated at the resource level. We evaluate our approach on a dataset built from the Computer Science department's competency referential at the Université de Technologie de Compiègne (UTC), covering 22 competencies across multiple course materials. Our LLM+BM25+Graph (LBG) pipeline achieves strong results, with a micro-F1 of 0.57 and macro-F1 of 0.50 at the fragment level, 0.51 macro-F1 at the resource level, and an MRR of 0.82outperforming zero-shot and few-shot LLM variants, retrieval/similarity baselines, and supervised classifiers -while also producing more mechanically traceable evidence spans to support human auditing and educational analysis.

</details>


### [114] [ProvMind: Provenance-grounded reasoning for materials synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28487)
*Yiming Zhang,Ryo Tamura,Koji Tsuda*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MatProcBench is a provenance-grounded benchmark for materials process optimization, evaluating seven process-reasoning tasks. ProvMind, a process-memory reasoning framework, achieves 52.84% accuracy on the challenging dual-OOD split, outperforming existing baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing computational formulations flatten synthesis procedures into text or ordered steps, lacking the ability to reason over routes, conditions, tools, and causal dependencies in materials process optimization.

Method: MatProcBench uses literature-mined MatPROV graphs for seven process-reasoning tasks with same-split and shift-aware evaluation (including dual-OOD split combining temporal and material-class shift). ProvMind retrieves analogous training processes, converts them into provenance-aware option-level compatibility scores, and uses language model constrained decision making.

Result: ProvMind achieves 52.84% accuracy on the dual-OOD split, outperforming prompting, retrieval-augmented, and supervised fine-tuning baselines.

Conclusion: The provenance-grounded benchmark and process-memory reasoning approach advance materials process optimization by enabling structured reasoning over causal dependencies and process variables under distribution shift.

Abstract: Materials process optimization requires reasoning over routes, conditions, tools and causal dependencies, yet most computational formulations flatten synthesis procedures into text or ordered steps. We introduce MatProcBench, a provenance-grounded benchmark constructed from literature-mined MatPROV graphs, to evaluate seven process-reasoning tasks spanning route continuity, step-level variable inference and global causal consistency under both same-split and shift-aware evaluation, including a strict dual-OOD split that combines temporal and material-class shift. We further introduce ProvMind, a process-memory reasoning framework that retrieves analogous training processes, converts them into provenance-aware option-level compatibility scores, and uses a language model for constrained final decision making. ProvMind achieves 52.84\% accuracy on the dual-OOD split, outperforming prompting, retrieval-augmented and supervised fine-tuning baselines.

</details>


### [115] [Benchmarking AI for low-resource contexts: Thinking beyond leaderboards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28508)
*Aakash Pant,Kavya Shah,Apoorv Agnihotri,Sneha Nikam,Prasaanth Balraj,Nakul Jain*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper identifies critical gaps between AI evaluation practices and real-world low-resource deployment conditions, arguing that deployed systems (not isolated models) should be assessed with context-sensitive metrics, and proposing a shared reporting framework with standardized artifacts like benchmark cards and deployment profiles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI evaluation practices fail to capture how systems perform in low-resource environments, where operational constraints (noisy inputs, code-switching, intermittent connectivity, low-end hardware, domain shift) shape usability as much as model quality. The paper aims to bridge the gap between laboratory benchmarks and real-world deployment conditions.

Method: Structured analysis of existing benchmark families across speech, chat/RAG, and vision systems to identify gaps between lab evaluations and low-resource deployment conditions.

Result: Identification of critical gaps between evaluation practices and deployment realities; finding that the meaningful unit of assessment is the deployed system (not isolated model); recognition that different application classes need distinct evaluation profiles rather than single aggregate scores.

Conclusion: Proposes a shared reporting framework that preserves comparability while being sensitive to deployment context, and emphasizes concise actionable reporting artifacts (one-page benchmark cards, deployment profiles, failure handling documentation, human oversight mechanisms) for policymakers, donors, and implementers.

Abstract: Existing AI evaluation practices often fail to capture how systems actually perform in low-resource environments, where operational constraints shape usability as much as model quality. Through a structured analysis of existing benchmark families across speech, chat/RAG, and vision systems, we identify critical gaps between laboratory evaluation practices and real-world deployment conditions in low-resource environments. We argue that the meaningful unit of assessment is the deployed system rather than an isolated model and that effective evaluation frameworks must integrate task performance with deployment conditions such as noisy inputs, code-switching, intermittent connectivity, low-end hardware, and domain shift. At the same time, benchmarks should recognize that different application classes require distinct evaluation profiles rather than a single aggregate score that obscures operational differences. To support practical decision-making, we propose a shared reporting framework that preserves comparability across systems and application types while remaining sensitive to deployment context. Finally, we emphasize the need for concise and actionable reporting artifacts for policymakers, donors, and implementers, including standardized one-page benchmark cards, deployment profiles, and explicit documentation of failure handling procedures and human oversight mechanisms.

</details>


### [116] [GS-FUSE: Granger-Supervised Gated Fusion and Multi-Granularity Alignment for Event-Driven Financial Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28520)
*Yang Zhang,En Chun,Ziyun Mao,Yulu Wu,Jun Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GS-Fuse is a multimodal event-based forecasting framework that uses a Granger-supervised gated fusion module to selectively incorporate event text only when it provides predictive value beyond historical prices, along with a multi-granularity alignment mechanism, achieving state-of-the-art results on real-world financial datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal time-series models fuse text and prices symmetrically without an explicit mechanism to determine when event text is truly predictive, failing to exploit the directional event-to-price structure and the heterogeneous roles of textual and price signals.

Method: GS-Fuse employs (i) a Granger-supervised, causal-aware gated fusion module that opens toward event text only when it provides incremental predictive value beyond historical prices, and (ii) a multi-granularity alignment mechanism that aligns both high-level event representations and fine-grained textual cues with future market trajectories. It is designed as a plug-and-play adapter on top of off-the-shelf LLMs and time-series foundation models.

Result: Extensive experiments on real-world financial datasets show GS-Fuse consistently outperforms state-of-the-art time-series and multimodal baselines across multiple assets and forecasting horizons.

Conclusion: The proposed causal-aware gating and multi-granularity alignment enable more effective multimodal event-based forecasting, and the framework's flexible adapter design allows instantiation across diverse backbones and market settings.

Abstract: Accurately forecasting the impact of salient financial events on markets is critical for investors and policymakers. However, existing multimodal time-series models typically fuse text and prices symmetrically, without an explicit way to decide when event text is truly predictive, and thus struggle to exploit the directional event-to-price structure and the heterogeneous roles of textual and price signals. In this work, we propose GS-Fuse, a multimodal event-based forecasting framework that employs (i) a Granger-supervised, causal-aware gated fusion module, which learns to open toward event text only when it provides incremental predictive value beyond historical prices, and (ii) a multi-granularity alignment mechanism that jointly aligns high-level event representations and fine-grained textual cues with future market trajectories. Built as a flexible, plug-and-play adapter on top of off-the-shelf large language models and time-series foundation models, GS-Fuse can be instantiated across diverse backbones and market settings. Extensive experiments on real-world financial datasets show that GS-Fuse consistently outperforms state-of-the-art time-series and multimodal baselines across multiple assets and forecasting horizons.

</details>


### [117] [Let Relations Speak: An End-to-End LLM-GNN Soft Prompt Framework for Fraud Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28524)
*Zhixing Zuo,Huilin He,Jiasheng Wu,Dawei Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LGSPF proposes a soft prompt framework to bridge LLMs and GNNs for fraud detection, eliminating reliance on text attributes and improving multi-relational semantic alignment, achieving state-of-the-art results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based graph methods fail in fraud detection due to heavy reliance on rich text attributes, hard prompt-induced feature distortion, and inability to capture multi-relational semantic complexity.

Method: LGSPF uses soft prompts to bridge graph structure and semantic space without text, introduces a parallel GNN encoder to convert multi-relational topologies into graph tokens, and performs end-to-end optimization for deep semantic alignment between LLM and GNN.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance across diverse fraud detection benchmarks and enhances semantic interpretability of fraud behaviors.

Conclusion: The soft prompt framework effectively eliminates text dependency while improving multi-relational fraud understanding, demonstrating both performance gains and better interpretability.

Abstract: In recent years, Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown great capability in processing graph tasks such as fraud detection. However, most existing methods rely heavily on rich text attributes, which poses difficulties for this domain due to the lack of textual data. Although some pioneering methods attempt to overcome it, their textualization of graph structures via hard prompts easily leads to feature distortion. Additionally, fraud detection often exhibits multi-relational complexity, where current methods struggle to capture this deep semantic information. To address these challenges, we propose LLM-GNN Soft Prompt Framework (LGSPF). Specifically, LGSPF bridges the graph structure and semantic space using soft prompt to eliminate reliance on text. We further introduce a parallel Graph Neural Network (GNN) encoder to translate multi-relational topologies into graph tokens for fine-grained LLM fraud comprehension. Through end-to-end optimization, LGSPF enhances deep semantic alignment between LLM and GNN. Experiments across diverse fraud detection benchmarks demonstrate our method achieves state-of-the-art performance. Moreover, we further validate the contribution of LGSPF on enhancing the semantic interpretability of fraud behaviors.

</details>


### [118] [Entropy-aware Masking for Masked Language Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28526)
*Gokul Srinivasagan,Kai Hartung,Munir Georges*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes an entropy-based token masking strategy for masked language model pretraining, where tokens are selected based on the model's predictive uncertainty rather than random selection, achieving ~5% GLUE improvement and further gains when combined with knowledge distillation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Random token masking in masked language modeling may not provide the most effective learning signals, motivating the need for a more informed masking strategy that targets informative and uncertain tokens.

Method: The authors propose (1) an entropy-based masking strategy that uses the model's own predictive entropy to identify which tokens to mask, and (2) a self-masking approach that avoids reliance on an external reference model, further combined with knowledge distillation.

Result: The proposed method achieves a 5% average improvement in GLUE scores over the random masking baseline, and combining entropy masking with knowledge distillation yields the best overall results.

Conclusion: Entropy-based masking is an effective alternative to random masking for encoder-based language model pretraining, and it can be further enhanced via knowledge distillation.

Abstract: Masked language modeling has become a standard pretraining objective for training encoder-based language models. In this approach, certain tokens in the input are masked, and the model learns to predict them using the surrounding context. This process enables the model to capture both syntactic and semantic properties of language. Conventionally, the tokens selected for masking are chosen at random, which may not always yield the most effective learning signals. In this work, we examine a token masking strategy based on entropy distribution. We use the model's entropy over token predictions to identify which tokens should be masked. This method aims to target tokens that are more informative and uncertain to improve the training efficacy. We also propose a novel self-masking approach that enhances training efficiency without relying on an external reference model. Experimental results demonstrate that our method achieves an average performance improvement of 5% in GLUE scores compared to the baseline. Further, we experiment with combining knowledge distillation with entropy masking, resulting in the best overall results.

</details>


### [119] [Do Agents Know What They Can't Do? Evaluating Feasibility Awareness in Tool-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28532)
*Liang Cheng,Mingsheng Cai,Jiuming Jiang,Luo Mai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FeasiGen is an automatic pipeline that identifies critical tools from successful agent executions and masks them to construct infeasible tasks. Evaluations show agents have weak infeasibility detection (false continue rate up to 73.9%), while multi-agent systems help reduce erroneous execution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tool-using agents often waste computational resources executing infeasible tasks when required capabilities are unavailable. Detecting infeasibility early can reduce unnecessary costs, but currently agents lack such awareness.

Method: FeasiGen extracts tool-calling traces from successful agent executions, identifies critical tools consistently shared across diverse strategies, and masks these tools to convert solvable tasks into infeasible ones. Human verification confirms >94% annotation accuracy.

Result: Across nine models, infeasibility detection is substantially weak with false continue rates up to 73.9%. Multi-agent architectures significantly reduce erroneous execution under infeasible conditions.

Conclusion: Current agents lack robustness in detecting infeasible tasks; FeasiGen provides a scalable way to generate benchmarks for this capability, and multi-agent design is a promising direction for improvement.

Abstract: Tool-using agents often incur substantial computational cost due to long reasoning chains and iterative tool usage. In practical scenarios, many tasks become infeasible under constrained tool environments, where the capabilities required for successful task completion are unavailable. Detecting infeasible tasks and stopping execution early can significantly reduce unnecessary execution cost. In this work, we propose FeasiGen, an automatic pipeline for constructing infeasible agent tasks by identifying the critical tools required for successful task completion. Our approach extracts tool-calling traces from successful executions across multiple agent systems, identifies critical tools consistently shared across diverse execution strategies, and masks these tools to automatically transform solvable tasks into infeasible ones. Human verification confirms that the infeasibility annotations for our constructed tasks achieve over 94% accuracy. We further introduce feasibility-aware evaluation metrics for measuring whether agents can recognize infeasible tasks and stop execution appropriately. Extensive evaluations across nine models reveal substantially weak infeasibility detection ability, with false continue rate reaching up to 73.9%. We further observe that multi-agent architectures significantly reduce erroneous execution under infeasible conditions.

</details>


### [120] [Cultural Binding Heads in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28543)
*Avrile Floro,Luca Benedetto*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs lack cultural difference awareness despite having relevant knowledge. Using mechanistic interpretability on the N4 benchmark, we identified causal attention heads for cultural binding across eight models. Knocking out identity-to-item connections reduces binding by 9-23%. These heads originate from pre-training. Steering at generation (α=2-3) improves differentiation accuracy by 1-3 pp without harming neutral reasoning. Models know 3-5x more than they use, indicating a routing bottleneck.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs treat cultural groups equally even when context calls for differentiation—a lack of difference awareness. The authors aim to understand the mechanistic basis of cultural binding (associating cultural items with appropriate identities) and determine whether the bottleneck is missing knowledge or poor routing.

Method: Used mechanistic interpretability and a factorial design on the N4 cultural appropriation benchmark from Wang et al. (2025). Identified 2-3 mid-layer attention heads per model causally contributing to cultural binding across eight models (four architectures, base & instruct). Performed knockout of identity-to-item edges, α-scaling dose-response analysis, steering at generation, and a knowledge probing task.

Result: Knockout lowers binding strength by 9-23%. Heads transfer from instruct to base models, suggesting pre-training origin. α-scaling shows graded dose-response; steering at α=2-3 improves cultural differentiation accuracy by 1-3 pp with minimal effect on neutral reasoning. Knowledge probing reveals models know 3-5 times more than they act upon.

Conclusion: The bottleneck for cultural differentiation is routing (not knowledge). Cultural binding is established during pre-training, and targeted intervention can improve differentiation without degrading general reasoning.

Abstract: LLMs often default to equal treatment across cultural groups, even though context warrants differentiation: this is a lack of difference awareness. Using mechanistic interpretability and a factorial design on the N4 cultural appropriation benchmark from Wang et al. (2025), we identify 2-3 mid-layer attention heads per model that contribute causally to cultural binding across eight models (four architectures, base and instruct). Cultural binding is the process of associating cultural items with the appropriate identity. Knockout of the identity-to-item edges on these heads lowers the binding strength by 9-23%. The identified heads transfer from instruct to base models, suggesting that cultural binding is created at pre-training. An $α$-scaling shows a graded dose-response and moderate amplification steering at generation ($α= 2-3$) increases cultural differentiation accuracy by 1-3 pp while leaving neutral reasoning mostly intact. A knowledge probing task shows that models know 3-5 times more than they act upon it, indicating that the bottleneck lies in routing and not knowledge.

</details>


### [121] [Refusal Before Decoding: Detecting and Exploiting Refusal Signals in Intermediate LLM Activations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28553)
*Matteo Gioele Collu,Riccardo Conte,Alberto Giaretta,Denis Kleyko,Mauro Conti,Matteo Zavatteri,Roberto Confalonieri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Refusal behavior in LLMs can be predicted from intermediate activations using linear probes, and this signal can be exploited via Mechanistic AutoDAN to achieve competitive attack success rates while reducing search time by up to 72%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether refusal behavior is encoded in intermediate LLM activations before decoding, and whether this signal can be leveraged for efficient jailbreak attacks.

Method: Trained linear probes on residual stream activations at each transformer block to decode refusal; introduced Mechanistic AutoDAN, which uses probe-based scoring with partial forward passes inside a genetic prompt search loop to replace full-model fitness evaluation.

Result: Mechanistic AutoDAN achieves competitive attack success rates vs. vanilla AutoDAN while reducing per-iteration search time by up to 72%; probe-guided prompts match or exceed cross-model transfer; probe guidance usefulness increases with model scale.

Conclusion: Refusal is encoded as a structured and actionable signal in intermediate LLM activations, not just observable at the output level.

Abstract: In this paper, we investigate whether refusal behavior can be predicted from LLM intermediate activations before decoding using linear probes trained on residual stream activations at each transformer block. We find that refusal is linearly decodable well before the final layer, indicating that safety-relevant behavior is represented in intermediate activations before output generation. To test whether this signal is actionable, we introduce Mechanistic AutoDAN, a probe-guided variant of AutoDAN that replaces full-model fitness evaluation with partial forward passes and probe-based scoring inside a genetic prompt search loop. Across the evaluated models, our method achieves attack success rates competitive with vanilla AutoDAN while reducing per-iteration search time by up to 72%, and probe-guided prompts match or exceed AutoDAN's cross-model transfer in several configurations. We further find that the usefulness of probe guidance increases with model scale. Our results show that refusal is not only observable at the output level, but is encoded as a structured and actionable signal in intermediate LLM activations.

</details>


### [122] [A Matter of TASTE: Improving Coverage and Difficulty of Agent Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28556)
*Tomer Keren,Nitay Calderon,Asaf Yehudai,Yotam Perlitz,Michal Shmueli-Scheuer,Roi Reichert*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TASTE (Task Synthesis from Tool Sequence Evolution) is an automatic method that reverses traditional benchmark construction by first generating diverse tool sequences via an Adaptive Contrastive n-gram model, then clustering and instantiating them into challenging benchmark tasks. Using TASTE, the authors construct τ^c-Bench, which exposes severe performance drops in models that nearly saturate existing benchmarks (e.g., Gemini-3-Flash drops from 0.82-0.94 to 0.28-0.61), while more than doubling unique tool combinations tested.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agent benchmarks (e.g., τ^2-Bench) are becoming saturated, while constructing new benchmarks manually is complex, costly, and captures only narrow tool-use patterns. The standard natural-language-to-tool-sequence approach limits diversity and coverage of agent tool-use behaviors.

Method: TASTE uses an Adaptive Contrastive n-gram model trained on LLM-judged validity signals to sample valid tool sequences with broad coverage. Representative sequences are selected via clustering, instantiated into complete benchmark tasks, and refined through iterative difficulty evolution.

Result: τ^c-Bench, constructed via TASTE across three domains, shows severe performance drops: Gemini-3-Flash falls from 0.82-0.94 to 0.28-0.61. Generated tasks more than double unique tool combinations agents must execute compared to τ^2-Bench.

Conclusion: High scores on existing benchmarks often reflect saturation rather than robust task-solving ability. TASTE enables continuous, scalable, automated generation of difficult, high-coverage benchmarks for evaluating future agents.

Abstract: As agent capabilities advance, existing benchmarks, such as $τ^2$-Bench, are becoming increasingly saturated. Yet constructing new benchmark tasks remains complex, costly, and labor-intensive. Moreover, the standard approach, in which scenarios are first written in natural language and then mapped to tool sequences, captures only a narrow subset of the tool-use patterns agents exercise. In this paper, we address these problems by reversing the task construction process. We propose TASTE: Task Synthesis from Tool Sequence Evolution, an automatic method that generates challenging tasks with broader tool-use coverage. TASTE utilizes an Adaptive Contrastive $n$-gram model trained on LLM-judged validity signals. This enables sampling valid tool sequences that cover a vast range of tool combinations. TASTE then selects representative sequences from the pool via clustering, instantiates them into complete benchmark tasks, and refines them through iterative difficulty evolution. Using TASTE, we construct $τ^c$-Bench, a challenging extension of the three domains of $τ^2$-Bench. We evaluate $11$ agent/user LLM pairs and find that models nearly saturating $τ^2$-Bench suffer severe performance drops on our tasks (e.g., Gemini-3-Flash falls from $0.82\!-\!0.94$ to $0.28\!-\!0.61$). Beyond increasing difficulty, our generated tasks more than double the number of unique tool combinations agents must execute. Our results suggest high scores on existing benchmarks often reflect saturation rather than robust task-solving ability. By automating the generation of difficult, high-coverage benchmarks, TASTE enables continuous, scalable evaluation of future agents.

</details>


### [123] [Tree of Thoughts as a Classical Heuristic Search Problem: Formal Foundations and Design Patterns](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28566)
*Guni Sharon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper synthesizes the fragmented Tree-of-Thoughts (ToT) research landscape by proposing a unified taxonomy based on classical heuristic search terminology, mapping LLM reasoning components (state, successor generation, heuristic evaluation) to search paradigms, identifying design patterns for different tasks, and highlighting open challenges at the intersection of heuristic search and LLM reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Tree-of-Thoughts research is fragmented across NLP and Automated Planning communities with inconsistent terminology and ad-hoc implementations, hindering systematic progress and cross-pollination of ideas.

Method: The authors synthesize the ToT landscape through a unified taxonomy grounded in classical heuristic search terminology. They map LLM-based reasoning components to search concepts: state representation (thought granularity), successor generation (prompting operators), and heuristic evaluation (self-assessment). They analyze existing work within this framework to identify design patterns.

Result: Two emergent design patterns are identified: (1) Best-First Search for shallow, deterministic tasks, and (2) lookahead-heavy strategies (DFS, MCTS) for deep multi-step reasoning. The taxonomy reveals systematic relationships between task characteristics and suitable search paradigms.

Conclusion: The paper identifies open algorithmic challenges at the intersection of heuristic search and LLM reasoning and issues a call to action for the heuristic search community to engage with this emerging domain.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable reasoning capabilities, yet their standard generation process -- auto-regressive token prediction -- is inherently myopic and prone to cascading errors. To address this, the Tree-of-Thoughts (ToT) framework creates a search space over intermediate reasoning steps, allowing search models to explore, look ahead, and backtrack. However, current ToT research remains fragmented across Natural Language Processing and Automated Planning communities, often using inconsistent terminology and ad-hoc implementations. Consequently, we synthesize the ToT landscape through a unified taxonomy based on classical heuristic search terminology. We map LLM-based reasoning to classical search components: state representation (granularity of thoughts), successor generation (prompting operators), and heuristic evaluation (self-assessment of progress). We analyze existing work within the context of our taxonomy and identify emerging design patterns: systematic search (Best-First Search) for shallow, deterministic tasks and lookahead-heavy strategies (DFS, MCTS) for deep multi-step reasoning. We conclude by identifying open algorithmic challenges at the intersection of heuristic search and LLM reasoning, and call on the heuristic search community to engage with this emerging domain.

</details>


### [124] [A Conflict-Aware Penalty and Statistical Loss Framework for Balancing Modalities and Enhancing Stability in Multimodal Sentiment Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28575)
*Jianheng Dai,Jiazhang Liang,Sijie Mai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Conflict-aware Penalty (CP) and Statistical Loss (SL) to mitigate text modality dominance in multimodal sentiment analysis, achieving SOTA on CMU-MOSI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pre-trained text encoders dominate optimization in MSA, suppressing weaker acoustic/visual modalities and causing gradient norm conflicts that destabilize training.

Method: (1) Conflict-aware Penalty (CP) detects and penalizes gradient norm conflicts at each training step; (2) Statistical Loss (SL) aligns predicted distribution statistics with empirical input statistics; (3) unified framework with adaptive modality encoding, gated cross-modal fusion, and unimodal auxiliary heads.

Result: State-of-the-art performance on CMU-MOSI; ablation studies confirm effectiveness of each component.

Conclusion: CP and SL together enable synergistic multimodal training by preventing dominant modality gradients from interfering with the SL objective, effectively addressing modality imbalance.

Abstract: Multimodal Sentiment Analysis (MSA) fuses text, acoustic, and visual streams to infer sentiment. Because pre-trained text encoders are far more expressive than their acoustic and visual counterparts, the text modality tends to dominate optimization, suppressing weaker modalities and inducing gradient norm conflicts that destabilize training. To address this, we propose a Conflict-aware Penalty (CP) that detects and penalizes gradient norm conflicts at each training step, and a Statistical Loss (SL) that aligns predicted distribution statistics with empirical input statistics. Crucially, CP prevents dominant modality gradients from interfering with the SL objective, enabling synergistic training within a unified framework incorporating adaptive modality encoding, gated cross-modal fusion, and unimodal auxiliary heads. Experiments on CMU-MOSI demonstrate state-of-the-art performance, with ablation studies confirming the effectiveness of each component.

</details>


### [125] [Continual Model Routing in Evolving Model Hubs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28577)
*Jack Bell,Giacomo Carfì,Gerlando Gramaglia,Vincenzo Lomonaco*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper formalizes Continual Model Routing (CMR) for large-scale AI model hubs, introduces CMRBench (a benchmark with 2,000+ models), and proposes CARvE—a contrastive embedding method using checkpoint anchoring and structured replay—which outperforms baselines in routing accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid growth of AI model hubs poses two challenges: scaling model selection across thousands of experts and continually updating routing mechanisms as new models/tasks are added.

Method: CARvE: a contrastive embedding approach that uses checkpoint-based anchoring and structured replay for efficient continual model routing.

Result: CARvE significantly outperforms zero-shot retrieval, fine-tuning, and adapter-merging baselines in model-level, family-level, and domain-level accuracy.

Conclusion: Formalized CMR as a new problem, built CMRBench for evaluation, and demonstrated that CARvE effectively addresses both scalability and continual update challenges in model routing.

Abstract: AI model hubs provide access to a rapidly growing collection of powerful pre-trained models, enabling off-the-shelf mixture-of-experts systems with different routing strategies. However, this rapid growth poses two fundamental challenges: scaling model selection across thousands of experts and continually updating routing mechanisms as new models and tasks are introduced. In this paper, we formalise this setting as Continual Model Routing (CMR) and propose CMRBench, a new large-scale benchmark simulating realistic hub expansion and including over 2,000 candidate models. Finally, we introduce CARvE, a contrastive embedding approach for efficient continual model routing via checkpoint-based anchoring and structured replay. Extensive empirical results and ablations show that CARvE significantly outperforms zero-shot retrieval, fine-tuning, and adapter-merging baselines in model, family, and domain-level accuracy.

</details>


### [126] [MUSE: Benchmarking Manufacturable, Functional, and Assemblable Text-to-CAD Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28579)
*Xiaoyu Dong,Zhi Li,Xiao-Ming Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MUSE is a Text-to-CAD benchmark focused on complex B-Rep assemblies, evaluating generated models through a three-stage protocol (code check, geometric check, design-intent alignment) using rubric-based VLM judges to assess functionality, manufacturability, and assemblability beyond traditional shape matching.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Text-to-CAD benchmarks focus on single-part models and use geometric similarity metrics that fail to capture functionality, manufacturability, and assemblability—critical for industrial product design.

Method: MUSE pairs design instances with structured Design Specifications and evaluates via a three-stage protocol: (1) code check, (2) geometric check, and (3) design-intent alignment using design-specific rubrics assessed by a rubric-based VLM judge validated through human annotation.

Result: Experiments reveal a failure cascade from executable code to valid geometry to engineering-ready design; even the strongest models achieve limited success on fine-grained engineering criteria.

Conclusion: MUSE provides a realistic benchmark and evaluation framework advancing Text-to-CAD from geometric generation toward true engineering design.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have recently advanced text-driven 3D generation, yet Text-to-CAD remains far from supporting industrial product design. Existing benchmarks focus primarily on generating single-part CAD models and evaluate them using geometric similarity metrics that fail to capture functionality, manufacturability, and assemblability. To address this gap, we introduce MUSE, a Text-to-CAD benchmark focused on complex, editable boundary representation (B-Rep) assemblies. MUSE pairs practical design instances with structured Design Specifications and evaluates generated models through a three-stage protocol: code check, geometric check, and design-intent alignment. The final stage uses design-specific rubrics to assess functionality, manufacturability, and assemblability, moving beyond shape matching toward practical design quality. To enable scalable evaluation, we use a rubric-based visual language model (VLM) judge and validate its reliability through human annotation. Experiments on closed-source and open-source LLMs reveal a clear failure cascade from executable code to valid geometry and finally to engineering-ready design, with even the strongest models achieving limited success on fine-grained engineering criteria. Together, MUSE provides a realistic benchmark and evaluation framework for advancing Text-to-CAD from geometric generation toward true engineering design. Our project website, including the leaderboard, dataset, and code, is available at https://dong7313.github.io/muse-benchmark/.

</details>


### [127] [Satisfiability Solving with LLMs: A Matched-Pair Evaluation of Reasoning Capability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28602)
*Leizhen Zhang,Shuhan Chen,Sheng Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs evaluated on SAT and its reductions show that conventional metrics are misleading due to over-prediction of satisfiable formulas. The authors propose a paired-formula protocol with ADR (Accurate Differentiation Rate) for more faithful, representation-robust assessment, finding that SAT serves as a conservative probe for LLM reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used for tasks reducible to Boolean satisfiability (SAT), yet their actual reasoning ability on SAT problems remains unclear. Standard metrics (accuracy, precision, recall, F1) can be misleading because models often over-predict satisfiable formulas, fail to reproduce the classical easy-hard-easy phase-transition signature, and degrade sharply with problem scale.

Method: Systematic study of LLMs on 2-SAT and 3-SAT, plus two canonical reductions (Vertex Cover and discrete 3D packing). Introduces a paired-formula protocol using minimally different satisfiable/unsatisfiable instances and a new metric, Accurate Differentiation Rate (ADR). Tests cross-representation consistency by converting CNF to graph/packing instances.

Result: Conventional metrics are misleading; many models over-predict satisfiable formulas, fail to reproduce the easy-hard-easy signature around the 3-SAT threshold, and degrade sharply with more variables. ADR separates reasoning-oriented models from heuristic ones and correlates with witness validity. Cross-representation consistency shows >80% agreement for most models.

Conclusion: SAT is a conservative probe for LLM reasoning. Paired evaluation with ADR provides a more faithful and representation-robust assessment than conventional metrics.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for tasks that implicitly reduce to Boolean satisfiability (SAT), yet their reasoning ability on SAT remains unclear. We present a systematic study of LLMs on 2-SAT and 3-SAT, together with two canonical reductions, Vertex Cover and discrete 3D packing, to probe representation-invariant reasoning. We first evaluate models using conventional metrics, including accuracy, precision, recall, and F1, as well as the SAT phase-transition setting. We find that these metrics can be misleading: many models obtain high scores by over-predicting satisfiable formulas, fail to reproduce the classical easy-hard-easy signature around the 3-SAT threshold, and degrade sharply as the number of variables grows.
  To address this problem, we introduce a paired-formula protocol based on minimally different satisfiable and unsatisfiable instances, together with Accurate Differentiation Rate (ADR), which requires both members of each pair to be classified correctly. ADR separates reasoning-oriented models from heuristic ones and correlates with witness validity. Beyond CNF, we test cross-representation consistency by converting CNF to Vertex Cover and 3-SAT to discrete 3D packing. Model decisions on CNF and on the corresponding graph or packing instances agree for most models on more than 80 percent of instances, suggesting stable decision rules across representations. Overall, our results show that SAT is a conservative probe for LLM reasoning, and that paired evaluation with ADR provides a more faithful and representation-robust assessment than conventional metrics.

</details>


### [128] [Adaptive Multimodal Agents-Based Framework for Automatic Workflow Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28607)
*Susanna Cifani,Mario Luca Bernardi,Marta Cimitile*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multimodal multi-agent framework that constructs topological knowledge bases from execution logs and uses Adaptive RAG with collaborative verification for automatic GUI workflow navigation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agents treat task sequences as discrete, linear episodes, failing to capture underlying transition topology, which limits effectiveness in novel or non-stationary scenarios.

Method: Two-phase pipeline: (1) Offline discovery phase adaptively builds a topological knowledge base from fragmented execution logs; (2) Inference phase uses Adaptive RAG over the pre-established graph with a closed-loop collaborative verification protocol for dynamic self-correction and navigation.

Result: Demonstrated in real-world context to maintain high reliability and semantic awareness even with limited training data.

Conclusion: Graph-based topological knowledge with adaptive retrieval and multi-agent collaboration enables superior task decomposition and adaptive navigation performance over fragmented linear approaches.

Abstract: Modern information systems require autonomous agents capable of navigating complex workflows, yet current methodologies often struggle with the transition from structured metadata parsing to general environmental perception. While the integration of MLLMs has enabled agents to interact directly with GUIs, existing approaches typically treat task sequences as discrete, linear episodes. This fragmentation prevents agents from capturing the underlying transition topology, limiting their effectiveness in novel or non-stationary scenarios. To address this, we propose a novel multimodal multi-agent framework that achieves automatic workflow execution through a distinct two-phase pipeline. First, during an offline discovery phase, the architecture adaptively constructs a topological knowledge base from fragmented execution logs. During inference, agents leverage Adaptive Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) over this fixed, pre-established graph, coupled with a closed-loop collaborative verification protocol to dynamically self-correct and navigate. This graph-based approach facilitates superior task decomposition and adaptive navigation performance. We validate our framework in a real-world context, demonstrating its ability to maintain high reliability and semantic awareness even with limited training data.

</details>


### [129] [LACUNA: Safe Agents as Recursive Program Holes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28617)
*Yaoyu Zhao,Yichen Xu,Oliver Bračevac,Cao Nguyen Pham,Frank Zhengqing Wu,Martin Odersky*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LACUNA is a programming model for LLM agents that closes the gap between the agent runtime and model-written code by having each agent action be a typed call that the LLM fills with code at execution time, with type-checking ensuring safety, consistency, and bounded tool/data access before execution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agents suffer from a split between the runtime (which controls the loop, context, and control flow) and the model-written code (which has little influence over the runtime). Making model-written code shape the runtime would increase expressiveness but also magnify safety risks (prompt injections, wrong tool calls, partial failures, inconsistent state), necessitating a safer integrated approach.

Method: LACUNA introduces a programming model where each agent action is a typed call agent[T](task) that the LLM fills with code at execution time. The code is type-checked against the surrounding program before execution, and actions are accepted or rejected as a whole—rejected actions leave the environment untouched, with compiler diagnostics driving retries. The type system bounds tool/data usage and data flow. This primitive can express ReAct loops, sub-agents, skills, parallel decomposition, and multi-model planning as ordinary control flow.

Result: On BrowseComp-Plus, 8.6% of generations are rejected before execution with 0.7 retries per query on average, achieving 27.1% accuracy. On τ²-bench, LACUNA solves 76.0% of 392 tasks across four domains with a capable model, performing on par with the baseline agent.

Conclusion: LACUNA effectively closes the runtime-code split in LLM agents while preserving safety through compile-time type-checking, whole-action acceptance/rejection, and bounded data/tool flow, achieving competitive performance with improved safety guarantees.

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly act by writing code, yet a split persists between the runtime that drives the agent and the code the model writes. The runtime owns the loop, context, and control flow, and the model has little say over any of them. Letting model-written code shape the runtime itself would make agents more expressive, but it would also sharpen safety problems. A model can be diverted by a prompt injection, call the wrong tool, or fail partway and leave an inconsistent state, and each such failure reaches further when the code shapes the runtime than when it expresses a single action. We present LACUNA, a programming model for agents that closes this split while preserving safety. Each agent action is a typed call $\texttt{agent[T](task)}$ that the LLM fills with code when execution reaches it, and the code is type-checked against the surrounding program before it runs. Because each action is accepted or rejected as a whole, a rejected one leaves the environment untouched, and its compiler diagnostics drive a retry. The same check also bounds which tools and data an action may use and how they flow. Our primitive expresses ReAct loops, sub-agents, skills, parallel decomposition, and multi-model planning as ordinary control flow. We evaluate LACUNA on a collection of test cases, BrowseComp-Plus, and $τ^2$-bench. On BrowseComp-Plus, $8.6\%$ of generations are rejected before execution, with 0.7 retries per query on average, and the agent reaches $27.1\%$ accuracy. On $τ^2$-bench, LACUNA solves $76.0\%$ of $392$ tasks across four domains with a capable model, on par with the baseline agent.

</details>


### [130] [Bandwidth-Efficient and Privacy-Preserving Edge-Cloud Many-to-Many Speech Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28642)
*Yexing Du,Kaiyuan Liu,Youcheng Pan,Bo Yang,Ming Liu,Bing Qin,Yang Xiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ESRT is a privacy-preserving, bandwidth-efficient edge-cloud collaborative framework for many-to-many speech-to-text translation using multimodal LLMs. It uses split inference (lightweight on-device encoder, cloud LLM) to prevent voiceprint leakage and reduce bandwidth by 10×, combined with multi-task curriculum learning for cross-lingual consistency. ESRT-4B and ESRT-12B achieve SOTA on FLEURS across 45 languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MLLM-based S2TT paradigms face three key challenges: (1) pure on-device models are resource-constrained, (2) centralized cloud systems transmit raw voice data, causing privacy risks (voiceprint leakage) and bandwidth bottlenecks, and (3) most models are English-centric, hindering many-to-many translation scaling.

Method: (1) Edge-cloud split inference architecture: a lightweight speech encoder and adapter remain on-device, transmitting only highly compressed intermediate features to the cloud LLM, preventing voiceprint leakage and reducing bandwidth by 10×. (2) Multi-task weighted curriculum learning with data balancing to ensure robust cross-lingual consistency and overcome English-centric bottlenecks.

Result: ESRT-4B and ESRT-12B achieve state-of-the-art many-to-many S2TT performance on the FLEURS dataset across 45 languages (45×44 translation directions).

Conclusion: The proposed ESRT framework effectively addresses privacy and bandwidth concerns in cloud-based S2TT while enabling scalable many-to-many translation. Code and models are released for reproducible, privacy-aware MLLM S2TT research.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have demonstrated significant potential for speech-to-text translation (S2TT). However, existing deployment paradigms face critical challenges: pure on-device models suffer from resource constraints, while centralized cloud systems incur severe privacy risks and bandwidth bottlenecks by transmitting raw voice data. Furthermore, most models exhibit English-centric biases, restricting many-to-many translation scaling. In this paper, we propose Edge-cloud Speech Recognition and Translation (ESRT), a privacy-preserving and bandwidth-efficient collaborative edge-cloud MLLM framework. Specifically, we design an edge-cloud split inference architecture that retains a lightweight speech encoder and adapter on the device, transmitting only highly compressed intermediate features to the cloud. This fundamentally prevents voiceprint leakage and reduces bandwidth requirements by up to 10$\times$. To overcome English-centric bottlenecks, we introduce a multi-task weighted curriculum learning strategy with data balancing to ensure robust cross-lingual consistency. Extensive experiments on the FLEURS dataset demonstrate that our models, ESRT-4B and ESRT-12B, achieve state-of-the-art many-to-many S2TT performance across 45 languages ($45 \times 44$ directions). Code and models are released to facilitate reproducible, privacy-aware MLLM S2TT research. The code and models are released at https://github.com/yxduir/esrt.

</details>


### [131] [The Ethics of LLM Sandbox and Persona Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28647)
*Tim Gebbie,Stewart Gebbie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM guardrails can create "reality gaps" that shift epistemic risk to users, constituting "reality laundering" which becomes unethical, especially in high-exposure advice contexts. The paper argues for top-down causal task-level requirements over bottom-up moral correction, distinguishing refusing harm from refusing reality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To expose how LLM guardrails and persona dynamics can produce a "reality gap" that is unethical because it knowingly shifts epistemic risk to uninformed users (reality laundering), potentially causing harm when deployed at scale.

Method: Conceptual analysis drawing analogies from financial regulation failures (Basel, B-BBEE, Societe Generale, London Whale) to demonstrate how formal safety systems become legible, gameable, and performative while real risk migrates elsewhere, then applying this pattern to LLM moral compliance.

Result: Guardrails that naively prevent direct harm often suppress truthful perception and launder uncomfortable mechanisms into acceptable abstractions. The distinction between refusing harm vs. refusing reality is established, showing that "ethical AI" becomes substantively unethical when it substitutes institutional reassurance for contact with reality.
