Experiments across diverse environments show that SID consistently outperforms baseline methods in terms of planning effectiveness and constraint satisfaction, and scales to scenarios with 108 robots and 160 obstacles.

</details>


### [156] [AURA: Asymptotically Optimal Uncertainty-Robust Replanning Algorithm for Kinodynamic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27699)
*Seyedali Golestaneh,Zhuoyun Zhong,Donghyung Lee,Constantinos Chamzas*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a unified meta-planner framework that combines asymptotically-optimal kinodynamic motion planning with online replanning and control optimization to improve both trajectory quality and tracking accuracy under motion uncertainty, validated in simulation and real-world experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sampling-based motion planners are typically used offline and suffer from tracking inaccuracies under motion uncertainty, requiring a framework that enables online refinement and improved execution robustness.

Method: The framework includes (1) a main execution thread, (2) a continuous replanning method that explores the state space and refines the trajectory during execution, and (3) an optimization process that adjusts future control inputs to minimize tracking error.

Result: The approach consistently improves trajectory quality, tracking accuracy, and overall performance compared to baseline methods in both simulation and real-world tests across multiple systems.

Conclusion: The proposed meta-planner enables asymptotically optimal planning to be leveraged online while improving execution accuracy under uncertainty, offering a practical solution for kinodynamic motion planning.

Abstract: Sampling-based motion planners offer a practical and scalable approach to kinodynamic motion planning, notably for high-dimensional, underactuated, or non-holonomic systems. However, these planners are typically used offline, requiring execution to begin only after the trajectory has been computed. In addition, the planned trajectory may not be accurately tracked in the presence of motion uncertainty, leading to deviations from the nominal solution. In this work, these limitations were addressed within a unified framework, \method, an asymptotically-optimal meta-planner framework that improves both path quality and tracking performance during execution. In addition to the main execution thread, this framework comprises a replanning method that continuously explores the state space and refines the trajectory during execution, and an optimization process that refines future control inputs to reduce tracking error. Together, these components enable \method to leverage asymptotically optimal planning online while improving execution accuracy under uncertainty. The proposed approach is evaluated in both simulation and real-world environments across multiple systems, demonstrating consistent improvements in trajectory quality, tracking accuracy, and overall performance compared with baseline methods.

</details>


### [157] [HumanoidMimicGen: Data Generation for Loco-Manipulation via Whole-Body Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27724)
*Kevin Lin,Ajay Mandlekar,Caelan Reed Garrett,Nikita Chernyadev,Yu Fang,Runyu Ding,Yuqi Xie,Justin Tran,Linxi Fan,Yuke Zhu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HumanoidMimicGen is a method for automatically generating humanoid legged loco-manipulation data from a handful of source demonstrations, enabling imitation learning across diverse scenes and outperforming real-world-only training by 20%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imitation learning for humanoid robots requires a large number of demonstrations that are time-intensive and difficult to collect via teleoperation, and existing data-generation algorithms are ineffective on humanoids due to their high-dimensional composite action spaces involving arms, legs, and torsos.

Method: HumanoidMimicGen adapts contact-rich whole-body skills from a handful of source demonstrations to new states, generalizing across object pose changes, and interleaves single- and dual-arm skills with whole-body locomotion and manipulation planning to generate stable, collision-free data across diverse scenes and layouts.

Result: Whole-body visuomotor policies co-trained with HumanoidMimicGen-generated data outperform those trained only on real-world data by 20% on a new simulated loco-manipulation benchmark containing nine diverse tasks.

Conclusion: HumanoidMimicGen enables automatic large-scale dataset generation for imitation learning on humanoids and allows systematic study of how data generation and policy learning decisions impact model performance.

Abstract: Imitation learning is a promising approach for training humanoid robots to both walk and manipulate, but it requires a large number of demonstrations, which are time-intensive and difficult to collect via teleoperation. Existing data-generation algorithms can automatically synthesize demonstrations for manipulators, but they are ineffective on humanoids because their high-dimensional composite action spaces involve arms, legs, and torsos. We present HumanoidMimicGen, a method for generating humanoid legged loco-manipulation data. Our method adapts contact-rich whole-body skills from a handful of source demonstrations to new states, generalizing across changes in object pose. By interleaving these single- and dual-arm skills with whole-body locomotion and manipulation planning, the method generates stable, collision-free data across diverse scenes and layouts. To evaluate our approach, we introduce a new simulated loco-manipulation benchmark containing nine diverse tasks that test humanoid loco-manipulation capabilities. There, we demonstrate that HumanoidMimicGen automatically generates large datasets for imitation learning and enables a systematic study of how data generation and policy learning decisions impact model performance. We show that whole-body visuomotor policies co-trained with data generated by HumanoidMimicGen outperform those trained only on real-world data by 20%.

</details>


### [158] [Colosseum V2: Benchmarking Generalization for Vision Language Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27759)
*Jeremy Morgan,Prajwal Vijay,Hyeonho Oh,Jincen Song,Ashvin Arora,Alina Du,Gaurav Sukhatme,Jesse Thomason,Ishika Singh*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Colosseum V2 is a large-scale simulation benchmark for evaluating VLA model generalization in robotic manipulation, featuring 28 tasks across 13 categories and two robot morphologies, demonstrating strong sim-to-real correlation and revealing limitations in current methods like ACT and Pi0.5.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite VLA models showing promising zero-shot perception and language capabilities, their task performance degrades under distribution shifts, revealing gaps in translating high-level understanding into robust behavior. The authors aim to systematically study this gap.

Method: The authors introduce Colosseum V2, a GPU-parallelized simulation benchmark built on ManiSkill, comprising 28 tasks across 13 categories and two robot morphologies. They evaluate state-of-the-art methods including ACT and Pi0.5 under both in-domain and out-of-domain conditions.

Result: State-of-the-art methods (ACT, Pi0.5) reveal limitations in both base performance and generalization under distribution shifts. Strong correlations between simulation and real-world metrics support ecological validity.

Conclusion: Colosseum V2 enables reproducible, fair comparisons and reduced evaluation overhead through standardized tasks, metrics, and evaluation protocols, accelerating progress toward general-purpose robot policies.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models demonstrate promising generalization in robotic manipulation, driven by advances in large-scale vision and language pre-training. This progress can be misleading. Despite the zero-shot perception and language capabilities of VLAs, their overall task performance often degrades under distribution shifts, revealing gaps in how these systems translate high-level understanding into robust behavior. To systematically study this gap, we introduce Colosseum V2, a large-scale simulation benchmark for evaluating VLA generalization in robot learning across diverse conditions. The benchmark comprises 28 tasks spanning 13 task categories and two robot morphologies, covering a wide range of manipulation primitives and long-horizon behaviors. Built on the ManiSkill simulator, Colosseum V2 enables fast, GPU-parallelized evaluation and supports both in-domain and out-of-domain testing at scale. We evaluate state-of-the-art methods, including Action Chunking Transformers (ACT) and Pi0.5, and reveal limitations in both base performance and generalization. We demonstrate strong correlations between simulation and real-world metrics that support the ecological validity of the benchmark. By standardizing tasks, metrics, and evaluation protocols within a unified benchmark, Colosseum V2 enables reproducible and fair comparisons, reduced evaluation overhead, and accelerated progress toward general-purpose robot policies.

</details>


### [159] [Turning Video Models into Generalist Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27817)
*Sizhe Lester Li,Evan Kim,Xingjian Bai,Tong Zhao,Tao Pang,Max Simchowitz,Vincent Sitzmann*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VERA is a robot policy that decouples video planning (using a pretrained video generative model) from action prediction (using a trained inverse dynamics model). By keeping the video planner embodiment-agnostic and designing an IDM based on the robot Jacobian, VERA achieves strong zero-shot, cross-embodiment performance across simulated and real-world tasks, including Panda arm manipulation and Allegro-hand dexterous manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing robot foundation models finetune video models with action-labeled data, coupling video prediction and action generation. This work tests the limits of an alternative decoupled approach: using a frozen, embodiment-agnostic video planner paired with a separately trained, embodiment-specific inverse dynamics model (IDM). This avoids costly re-training, enables easy swapping of video models, and leverages abundant self-play data for IDM training.

Method: VERA combines an action-free video world model (pretrained, frozen) with an inverse dynamics model (IDM) that maps video frames to actions. The IDM is designed using the robot embodiment Jacobian for data efficiency and scalability to high-dimensional action spaces. The system operates in a closed-loop video-to-action manner: the video model generates future frames conditioned on current observations and a goal, and the IDM translates frame differences into executable actions.

Result: VERA achieves strong performance on simulated and real-world benchmarks, including zero-shot Panda arm manipulation and 16-DoF Allegro-hand dexterous cube re-orientation. The same video planner works across multiple embodiments when paired with different embodiment-specific IDMs, demonstrating cross-embodiment generalization.

Conclusion: Decoupled video planning (frozen, embodiment-agnostic video model) plus faithful video-to-action translation (via a separately trained IDM) is a viable alternative route toward zero-shot, cross-embodiment, and generalizable robot control.

Abstract: Video generative models have emerged as a promising robotics backbone, capable of generating videos that depict the completion of complex tasks across embodiments and environments. Recent work proposes robot foundation models that jointly predict future observations and actions by finetuning video models with action-labeled data. In this paper, we test the limits of an alternative approach: leave the video planner as-is while training an embodiment-specific inverse dynamics model (IDM). This decoupling offers several natural benefits: the video planner remains embodiment-agnostic, different video models can be interchanged easily without re-training the IDM, and the IDM can be independently trained with readily available self-play data. We present a closed-loop, video-to-action policy that combines an action-free video world model with a carefully-designed IDM based on the robot embodiment Jacobian. We demonstrate that our IDM design is both data-efficient and scalable to high-dimensional action spaces. Our policy, which we coin the Video-to-Embodied Robot Action Model (VERA), achieves strong performance across simulated and real-world benchmarks, including zero-shot Panda arm manipulation and 16-DoF Allegro-hand dexterous cube re-orientation. The same video planner can be used across multiple embodiments by pairing it with different embodiment-specific IDMs. Our results show that decoupled video planning plus faithful video-to-action translation is a viable alternative route towards zero-shot, cross-embodiment, and generalizable robot control. More results are available on our project website: https://vera.csail.mit.edu.

</details>


### [160] [Tabero: Learning Gentle Manipulation with Closed-Loop Force Feedback from Vision, Touch, and Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27886)
*Qiwei Wu,Rui Zhang,Xin Xiang,Tao Li,Weihua Zhang,Junjie Lai,Renjing Xu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Tabero introduces a benchmark and model suite (Tabero-VTLA) for gentle, language-conditioned robotic manipulation. It repurposes open-source trajectories to generate vision-tactile-language data, uses a decoupled force-position command interface with a hybrid controller, and achieves over 70% reduction in grip force under gentle instructions while maintaining task success.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VLA models struggle to exploit tactile feedback for gentle manipulation due to scarce aligned vision-tactile-language data and lack of effective closed-loop force feedback mechanisms.

Method: Tabero benchmark uses a data-efficient pipeline to repurpose open-source robot manipulation trajectories for vision-tactile-language tasks. Tabero-VTLA proposes a decoupled force-position command interface executed by a fixed hybrid controller for real-time force-aware manipulation.

Result: The model maintains high task success while reducing average grip force by over 70% under gentle instructions, demonstrating force modulation based on multimodal experience.

Conclusion: The decoupled force-position architecture enables effective closed-loop force feedback for gentle robotic manipulation, addressing key limitations of current VLA models.

Abstract: Tactile sensing is essential for robots to achieve human-like gentle manipulation. However, existing Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models struggle to exploit tactile feedback for gentle manipulation due to scarce aligned vision-tactile-language data and the lack of effective closed-loop force feedback mechanisms. To address these challenges, we introduce Tabero, a benchmark and model suite for gentle, language-conditioned robotic manipulation that demands fine-grained contact force perception. First, the Tabero benchmark addresses the scarcity of tactile data by presenting a data-efficient pipeline that repurposes open-source robot manipulation trajectories to generate diverse vision-tactile-language tasks, and establishes a multidimensional evaluation protocol that measures task success alongside physical interaction quality. Second, we propose Tabero-VTLA, an architecture with a decoupled force-position command interface; the resulting force-position commands are executed by a fixed hybrid controller to enable real-time, force-aware manipulation. Evaluated on Tabero, our model maintains high task success while reducing average grip force by over 70\% under gentle instructions, demonstrating its ability to modulate interaction forces based on multimodal experience. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/NathanWu7/Tabero.

</details>


### [161] [S-Cheetah: A Novel Quadrupedal Robot with a 3-DOF Active Spine Learning Agile Locomotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27909)
*Zimu Li,Weibang Bai*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: S-Cheetah is a quadrupedal robot with a 3-DOF bio-inspired serial active spine that achieves biomimetic spatial tri-axial rotation. Using a specialized reinforcement learning framework with acceleration curriculum learning and tailored rewards, it reaches 6.9 m/s gallop speed, 7.2 rad/s turning rate, and exhibits feline-inspired aerial self-righting, demonstrating that the 3-DOF spine enhances locomotive agility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing quadrupedal robots simplify spinal design by reducing degrees of freedom, failing to replicate the spatial tri-axial rotation of biological spines. This limits their ability to achieve the agile locomotion seen in animals, creating a need for a multi-DOF biomimetic spine and a control framework that can fully exploit its capabilities.

Method: The authors built S-Cheetah with a 3-DOF serial active spine enabling sagittal flexion/extension, lateral bending, and axial rotation. They developed a reinforcement learning framework incorporating acceleration curriculum learning and custom reward functions (gallop gait reward, spine undulation reward, spine steering reward) to actively encourage spine engagement and maximize locomotion performance.

Result: S-Cheetah achieved a peak speed of 6.9 m/s with rotary G2 gallop gait, an in-place turning rate of 7.2 rad/s, and demonstrated emergent feline-inspired aerial self-righting from arbitrary orientations during free fall, landing stably on all four feet.

Conclusion: The introduction of the 3-DOF bio-inspired spine comprehensively enhances the locomotive agility of quadrupedal robots, validating that multi-axis spinal articulation is key to replicating animal-like dynamic locomotion capabilities.

Abstract: The biological spine of quadrupeds enables sagittal flexion/extension, lateral bending, and axial rotation, playing a crucial role in highly agile and dexterous locomotion. While numerous studies have integrated active spinal joints into quadrupedal robots to enhance agility, most designs simplify control complexity by reducing spinal degrees of freedom (DOF), failing to achieve the spatial tri-axial rotation characteristic of biological spines. Consequently, replicating a multi-DOF biomimetic spine and effectively leveraging it to empower the agile locomotion of quadrupedal robots remains a significant research challenge. In this study, we present S-Cheetah, a quadrupedal robot featuring a 3-DOF bio-inspired serial active spine capable of biomimetic spatial tri-axial rotation. To empower the robot to fully utilize this active spine, we developed a specialized reinforcement learning framework to actively promote the engagement of the introduced spine and maximize the robot's locomotive capabilities by integrating an acceleration curriculum learning strategy with tailored reward functions, such as a gallop gait reward, a spine undulation reward, and a spine steering reward. Experimental results demonstrate that S-Cheetah can achieve a peak speed of 6.9 m/s using the rotary G2 gallop gait and an in-place turning rate of 7.2 rad/s. Besides, the system exhibits an emergent, feline-inspired aerial self-righting capability, allowing it to land stably on four feet from arbitrary orientations during free fall. Finally, through extensive evaluations across diverse locomotion tasks, we prove that the introduction of the proposed 3-DOF spine comprehensively enhances the locomotive agility of quadrupedal robots. Project website: himmy-robotics.github.io/scheetah

</details>


### [162] [A Surveillance Evasion Game with Continuous Sensor Redeployment via Bilevel Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27917)
*Jaehyeok Kim,Kartik A. Pant,Joseph Kinerson,Kylie Sommer-Kohrt,Worawis Sribunma,Li-Yu Lin,James M. Goppert*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper formulates a two-player zero-sum differential game between adversarial UAS and heterogeneous sensor networks, introducing continuous sensor redeployment along building boundaries via smooth approximation, computing attacker's best response via STP-RRT* and nonlinear programming, and converging to Local Nash Equilibrium through alternating bilevel optimization with analytical stationarity conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: UASs exploit spatiotemporal gaps in sensor perimeters to infiltrate restricted airspace undetected, threatening critical infrastructure security. Existing game-theoretic approaches restrict defenders to discrete placement graphs or fixed configurations, lacking flexibility for real-world deployment.

Method: Two-player zero-sum differential game between adversarial UAS and heterogeneous sensor network (directional + omnidirectional). Continuous sensor redeployment along convex building boundaries via log-sum-exp smooth approximation preserving differentiability at polygon vertices. Attacker's best response via two-step approach: STP-RRT* for feasible trajectory initialization, nonlinear programming for detection-minimization refinement. Joint optimization converges to Local Nash Equilibrium via alternating bilevel optimization with analytical first-order stationarity conditions.

Result: The joint optimization converges to a Local Nash Equilibrium (LNE), with analytical first-order stationarity conditions derived for both players, establishing a deployable baseline for heterogeneous sensor placements in CUAS missions.

Conclusion: The proposed continuous sensor redeployment technique with smooth approximation and alternating bilevel optimization provides a theoretically grounded, deployable baseline for heterogeneous sensor placements in counter-UAS missions, overcoming limitations of prior discrete or fixed-configuration approaches.

Abstract: Uncrewed Aerial Systems (UASs) have become a growing threat to the security of critical infrastructure, exploiting spatiotemporal gaps in sensor perimeters to infiltrate restricted airspace undetected. We formulate this interaction as a two-player zero-sum differential game between an adversarial UAS and a heterogeneous sensor network of directional and omnidirectional sensors. Unlike earlier game-theoretic approaches that restrict the defender to discrete placement graphs or fixed configurations, we introduce a continuous sensor redeployment technique in which each sensor slides freely along the convex building boundaries. This is enforced via a log-sum-exp smooth approximation that preserves differentiability at polygon vertices, enabling optimization with gradient-based methods. The attacker's best response is computed via a two-step approach combining STP-RRT* for feasible trajectory initialization and nonlinear programming for detection-minimization refinement. The joint optimization converges to a Local Nash Equilibrium (LNE) via alternating bilevel optimization, with analytical first-order stationarity conditions derived for both players, thereby establishing a deployable baseline for heterogeneous sensor placements in CUAS missions.

</details>


### [163] [Frequency-Guided Action Diffusion via Sub-Frequency Manifold Traversal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27919)
*Junlin Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FGO is a frequency-based method that improves diffusion-based behavior cloning policies by guiding action generation through expanding spectral bands, reducing high-frequency noise from human demonstrations while preserving task-critical details.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human demonstrations contain high-frequency noise (jerks, pauses, jitter) that behavior cloning policies inherit, especially diffusion policies which amplify these artifacts. Existing methods fail to suppress this noise while retaining fine-grained details.

Method: Frequency Guidance Operator (FGO) steers diffusion policy generation by progressively driving noisy samples through intermediate sub-frequency manifolds with expanding spectral bands, enabling implicit spectral maneuvering during denoising.

Result: Validated on 15 robotic manipulation tasks across 5 benchmarks, FGO achieves superior action smoothness and temporal consistency while preserving task-relevant details.

Conclusion: FGO effectively addresses high-frequency noise pathology in diffusion-based behavior cloning by spectral guidance, improving policy quality without sacrificing performance-critical fine details.

Abstract: Learning visuomotor policies via behavior cloning typically involves mimicking expert demonstrations collected by human operators. However, natural human demonstrations inherently contain high-frequency noise, such as intermittent jerks, pauses, and action jitter. Training policies to directly imitate these raw trajectories inevitably causes the model to inherit these suboptimal behaviors. This pathology is particularly pronounced in diffusion-based policies, where iterative denoising steps can inadvertently amplify high-frequency artifacts at the expense of meaningful fine-grained details. To address these limitations, we present a novel frequency-based algorithm that enables implicit spectral maneuvering and smooth action generation. Our method, Frequency Guidance Operator (FGO), steers the generation process of diffusion polices by progressively driving the noisy samples through intermediate sub-frequency manifolds with expanding spectral bands. Validated on 15 robotic manipulation tasks from 5 benchmarks, FGO achieves superior performance in enhancing action smoothness and temporal consistency while preserving the details necessary for successful task execution. Project website: https://henrywjl.github.io/frequency-guidance-operator/

</details>


### [164] [SANTS: A State-Adaptive Scheduler for World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27947)
*Yirui Sun,Guangyu Zhuge,Keliang Liu,Jie Gu,Xinyu Bing,Zhongxue Gan,Chunxu Tian*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SANTS is a lightweight scheduler that adaptively selects a state-dependent stopping point along the video noise trajectory for video-to-action diffusion policies, achieving 94.4% success on RoboTwin 2.0 and 73.1% on real-robot tasks while reducing latency by ~80% compared to full video denoising.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In pixel-space World Action Models (WAMs), full video denoising is not always optimal for action generation; beyond a state-dependent point, further video refinement yields diminishing or even negative returns for action quality, yet existing approaches use a fixed terminal denoising depth.

Method: SANTS is a post-trained scheduler that reads the current video-state representation and noise level, jointly predicts a cumulative stopping hazard and a relative noise-progression ratio, and is optimized via a path-level reward based on downstream action quality while penalizing redundant video-state updates.

Result: 94.4% overall success on RoboTwin 2.0 and 73.1% average success across seven real-robot tasks, with 81.7% and 79.0% latency reduction relative to full denoising, respectively.

Conclusion: Adaptive selection along the video noise trajectory preserves the control benefits of WAM-style future reasoning while eliminating much of the redundant inference cost.

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) improve robot manipulation by using video-based future representations to condition action generation. In pixel-space WAMs, however, the best action condition is not necessarily the fully denoised video. Controlled denoising-depth scans show that video refinement can reduce action error up to a state-dependent point, after which the gain may saturate or even reverse when late predictions become less action-relevant or physically unreliable. This suggests that action generation should use a state-dependent point along the video noise trajectory rather than a fixed terminal denoising depth. We introduce State-Adaptive Noise Trajectory Scheduler (SANTS), a lightweight scheduler for video-to-action diffusion policies. At each video decision point, SANTS reads the current video-state representation and noise level, then jointly predicts a cumulative stopping hazard and a relative noise-progression ratio. SANTS is post-trained with a path-level reward computed after the frozen action branch generates the final action chunk, so the scheduler is optimized for downstream action quality rather than intermediate video fidelity, while redundant video-state updates are explicitly penalized. Experiments show that SANTS reaches \(94.4\%\) overall success on RoboTwin 2.0 and \(73.1\%\) average success across seven real-robot tasks, while reducing latency by \(81.7\%\) and \(79.0\%\) relative to full video denoising, respectively. These results indicate that adaptive selection along the video noise trajectory can preserve the control benefits of WAM-style future reasoning while removing much of its redundant inference cost.

</details>


### [165] [VLM-Based Advanced Rider Assistance System for Motorcycle Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27948)
*Mohamed Elnoor,Francesca Baldini,Ananya Trivedi,Faizan M. Tariq,Jovin D'sa,David Isele,Sangjae Bae,Dinesh Manocha,Yosuke Sakamoto*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes an Advanced Rider Assistance System (ARAS) that uses Vision-Language Models (VLMs) and segmentation-based detection to construct dense risk maps encoding semantic and physical hazards, then employs a sampling-based planner tailored to motorcycle dynamics to recommend safe throttle and steering actions, achieving higher success rates and lower hazard exposure in CARLA simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motorcycles have disproportionately high crash risks compared to cars due to limited protection and heightened sensitivity to surface hazards, yet ARAS remains underdeveloped relative to ADAS.

Method: The system integrates Vision-Language Models (VLMs) for contextual hazard reasoning with segmentation-based detection to construct dense risk maps encoding both semantic characteristics (e.g., pothole severity, puddle slipperiness) and physical attributes (e.g., size, depth). These maps produce per-pixel hazard costs, which are used by a sampling-based planner tailored to motorcycle dynamics to recommend throttle and steering actions minimizing hazard exposure.

Result: In CARLA simulator evaluations, the proposed method achieves higher success rates and lower hazard exposure compared to the baseline method, with qualitative results demonstrating interpretable risk maps and safe trajectory recommendations.

Conclusion: The proposed ARAS effectively enhances motorcycle safety through semantic perception and risk-aware planning, outperforming baseline methods in simulation.

Abstract: Motorcycles face disproportionately high crash risks compared to cars due to limited protection and heightened sensitivity to surface hazards, yet Advanced Rider Assistance Systems (ARAS) remain underdeveloped relative to Advanced Driver Assistance Systems (ADAS). We propose a novel ARAS that enhances motorcycle safety through semantic perception and risk-aware planning. Our approach leverages Vision-Language Models (VLMs) for contextual hazard reasoning and integrates them with segmentation-based detection to construct dense risk maps. These maps encode both semantic characteristics (e.g., pothole severity, puddle slipperiness) and physical attributes (e.g., size, depth), which produce per-pixel hazard costs that capture motorcycle-specific risks. These maps are used by a sampling-based planner tailored to motorcycle dynamics to recommend throttle and steering actions that minimize hazard exposure while advancing toward the destination. We evaluate our system in different scenarios in the CARLA simulator. Compared to the baseline method, our method achieves higher success rates and lower hazard exposure, while qualitative results demonstrate interpretable risk maps and safe trajectory recommendations.

</details>


### [166] [Simultaneous Contact Selection and Planning for Contact-Rich Manipulation with Cascaded Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27972)
*Zhe Zhang,Xingrong Diao,Haoxiang Liang,Han Yang,Bi-Ke Zhu,Dandan Zhang,Jiankun Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SCSP is a cascaded optimization framework for robust contact-rich manipulation that enables simultaneous contact location selection and trajectory planning through a surrogate contact model and discrete-continuous optimization, outperforming existing methods in diversity and robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing contact-implicit methods lack the ability to autonomously reason and generate diverse contact location sequences and manipulation trajectories (active contact location selection), limiting their applicability to relatively simple tasks. This is challenging due to complementarity in contact dynamics and sparse gradients.

Method: A cascaded optimization framework comprising two components: (1) Contact Selection Optimization (CSO) using a surrogate contact model and discrete-continuous optimization for efficient global searching of optimal contact locations; (2) Contact Planning Optimization (CPO) for prior-guided contact planning that evaluates reference contact locations from CSO and generates manipulation trajectories in real time for redundant manipulators.

Result: Extensive simulations and real-world experiments show SCSP produces diverse manipulation behaviors and robust control under inaccurate dynamics and perceptual noise. Generalization is validated on challenging manipulation tasks.

Conclusion: The proposed SCSP framework successfully addresses the challenge of active contact location selection in contact-rich manipulation, enabling a unified solution for contact selection and planning that works robustly in practice.

Abstract: We propose an optimization-based framework for robust contact-rich manipulation. Recent contact-implicit methods enable online hybrid planning across contact modes, allowing closed-loop manipulation for a given target state and contact location sequence of the robot and object. However, most existing approaches lack the ability to autonomously reason and generate diverse contact location sequences and manipulation trajectories, i.e., active contact location selection, which limits their applicability to relatively simple tasks. Active contact location selection is challenging due to complementarity in contact dynamics and the sparse gradients, making the design of a unified framework for contact selection and planning difficult. To address these challenges, we introduce Simultaneous Contact Selection and Planning (SCSP), a cascaded optimization framework comprising Contact Selection Optimization (CSO) and Contact Planning Optimization (CPO). CSO leverages a surrogate contact model and discrete-continuous optimization to efficiently resolve the nonsmoothness and coupling in contact selection, enabling online global searching of optimal contact locations. CPO performs prior-guided contact planning by evaluating the reference contact locations produced by CSO and generating corresponding manipulation trajectories in real time for redundant manipulators. Extensive simulations and real-world experiments demonstrate that SCSP produces diverse manipulation behaviors and robust control under inaccurate dynamics and perceptual noise. We further validate the generalization of the framework on challenging manipulation tasks.
  Project website: \href{https://sites.google.com/view/scsp-robot}{https://sites.google.com/view/scsp-robot}.

</details>


### [167] [How Should We Teach Robots? A Comparison of Kinesthetic, Joystick, and Gesture-Based Teaching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28033)
*Petr Vanc,Jan Kristof Behrens,Václav Hlaváč,Karla Stepanova*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A user study (N=8) comparing kinesthetic guidance, joystick teleoperation, and hand gestures for robot teaching across three manipulation tasks, finding kinesthetic guidance best for orientation-sensitive/contact-rich tasks, joystick best for simple peg picking, and hand gestures surprisingly competitive.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the usability and performance trade-offs of different teaching modalities (kinesthetic, joystick, hand gestures) for instructing robots from demonstrations, as each modality has distinct practical implications.

Method: User study with 8 participants comparing three teaching modalities (kinesthetic guidance, joystick teleoperation, hand gestures) across three manipulation tasks, evaluating replay success rate, modified NASA-TLX workload, and common teaching errors.

Result: Kinesthetic guidance yielded shortest demonstrations, lowest workload, and highest success on orientation-sensitive and contact-rich tasks; joystick teleoperation excelled at simple peg picking; hand gestures performed better than expected, sometimes comparable to kinesthetic guidance.

Conclusion: The optimal teaching modality depends on task characteristics: kinesthetic guidance for complex manipulation requiring orientation/contact precision, joystick for simple pick-and-place, and hand gestures as a viable albeit less reliable alternative.

Abstract: Instructing robots from demonstrations can be done through different teaching modalities, each with different usability and performance trade-offs. This paper compares kinesthetic guidance, joystick teleoperation, and hand gestures in a user study with eight participants. We evaluate replay success, modified NASA-TLX workload, and common teaching errors across three manipulation tasks. Kinesthetic guidance produced the shortest demonstrations, lowest workload, and highest success on the more orientation-sensitive and contact-rich tasks. Joystick teleoperation performed best on simple peg picking. Hand-gesture teaching, although less reliable overall, performed better than expected and in some cases achieved results comparable to kinesthetic guidance.

</details>


### [168] [SAFEVPR: Patch-Based Conformal Verification for Safe Cross-Condition Sequence Visual Place Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28048)
*Ha Sier,Jiaqiang Zhang,Zhuo Zou,Xianjia Yu,Tomi Westerlund*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SAFEVPR is a non-trainable verification-and-calibration pipeline that enables safe sequence-based visual place recognition under cross-condition deployment, using MNN patch-matching scores from DINOv2 features and Mondrian conformal LTT with Bonferroni correction to achieve empirical FDR control (mean 0.014) and high TPR (0.75) across 23 cross-condition setups, outperforming pure discrimination-based approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sequence-based VPR for SLAM and robot relocalization needs to decide whether the top-1 retrieved candidate is safe to accept. Conformal prediction is a natural framework but its exchangeability assumption is violated under cross-condition deployment, motivating a method that provides valid accept/reject decisions despite domain shifts.

Method: SAFEVPR pipeline: (1) replaces cosine similarity with a mutual-nearest-neighbour (MNN) patch-matching score from frozen DINOv2 ViT features; (2) replaces flat Learn-Then-Test calibration with Mondrian conformal LTT, fitting separate Bonferroni-corrected thresholds across score bins.

Result: Across 23 cross-condition setups from Oxford RobotCar, NCLT, and St Lucia datasets with three frozen VPR backbones: empirically valid on 23/23 setups at target FDR α=0.10, mean accepted FDR 0.014, mean TPR 0.75. Pure discrimination approaches (AnyLoc-VLAD, SuperPoint+LightGlue) with comparable AUROC failed more setups under the same calibration.

Conclusion: Raw discrimination alone is insufficient for conformal validity under domain shift; SAFEVPR provides safe abstention on unreliable matches (e.g., textureless repetitive scenery) where other methods fail.

Abstract: Sequence-based visual place recognition (VPR) for SLAM and robot relocalization must decide whether the retrieved top-1 candidate is safe to accept. Conformal prediction is a natural framework for this accept/reject decision, but its finite-sample guarantees rely on exchangeability between calibration and deployment (test) data, which is violated under cross-condition deployment. We introduce SAFEVPR, a non-trainable verification-and-calibration pipeline for safe cross-condition sequence VPR. SAFEVPR replaces the standard backbone cosine similarity with a mutual-nearest-neighbour (MNN) patch-matching score computed from frozen DINOv2 ViT features, and replaces flat Learn-Then-Test calibration with Mondrian conformal LTT, fitting separate Bonferroni-corrected thresholds across score bins. Under exchangeability, these thresholds would provide finite-sample false-discovery-rate (FDR) control; under condition shift, we evaluate empirical validity per deployment. Across 23 cross-condition setups from Oxford RobotCar, NCLT, and St Lucia datasets, using three frozen VPR backbones, SAFEVPR is empirically valid on 23/23 setups at target FDR alpha = 0.10, achieving mean accepted FDR 0.014 and mean true-positive rate (TPR) 0.75. The results show that raw discrimination alone is not sufficient for conformal validity: AnyLoc-VLAD and Super-Point+LightGlue reach comparable area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) but fail more setups under the same calibration. On textureless repetitive scenery, SAFEVPR safely abstains rather than accepting unreliable matches. Code is available at https://github.com/Hasar12139/SafeVPR.

</details>


### [169] [Whose Is This?: Context-Aware Object Ownership Inference with Uncertainty-Guided Questioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28087)
*Saki Hashimoto,Akira Taniguchi,Shoichi Hasegawa,Yoshinobu Hagiwara,Tadahiro Taniguchi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: COIN is a framework that uses LLMs and conformal prediction to infer object ownership from context (user background + usage history), achieving high accuracy (0.988 Subset Accuracy, 0.991 Jaccard) even under temporary sharing scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Service robots need to infer latent object ownership to correctly interpret user instructions (e.g., "bring me my cup"), but existing methods rely on limited cues like recent usage and fail under temporary sharing or ambiguous ownership scenarios.

Method: COIN integrates user background and object usage history via an LLM to estimate ownership scores, then applies conformal prediction to construct a set of plausible owners, selectively generating user queries when predictions are uncertain.

Result: In simulated home environments, COIN achieves Subset Accuracy of 0.988 and Mean Jaccard index of 0.991, consistently outperforming baselines, and maintains high performance under temporary use and shared ownership scenarios.

Conclusion: Combining contextual reasoning (LLM-based) with uncertainty-aware interaction (conformal prediction + selective querying) improves both ownership estimation accuracy and robustness for service robots.

Abstract: Service robots must infer object ownership to correctly interpret instructions such as "bring me my cup." However, ownership is a latent attribute that cannot be directly observed, and existing methods often rely on limited cues such as recent usage, making them unreliable in scenarios such as temporary sharing. We propose a framework for context-aware ownership inference with uncertainty-guided interaction (COIN). The method integrates user background information and object usage history using a large language model (LLM) to estimate ownership scores. To handle uncertainty, we apply conformal prediction to construct a set of plausible owners and selectively generate user queries when the prediction is uncertain. Experiments in a simulated home environment show that the proposed method consistently outperforms baseline approaches, achieving a Subset Accuracy of 0.988 and a Mean Jaccard index of 0.991. The method also maintains high performance in scenarios involving temporary use and shared ownership. The results demonstrate that combining contextual reasoning with uncertainty-aware interaction improves both estimation accuracy and robustness. The project page is available at https://emergentsystemlabstudent.github.io/COIN/.

</details>


### [170] [An Operator-Based Approach to STL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28092)
*Panagiotis Rousseas,Dimos V. Dimarogonas*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes a novel operator-based approach to Signal Temporal Logic (STL) using reachability value functions, enabling handling of complex multi-nested formulae and online control synthesis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing STL verification and control synthesis methods are limited in handling complex and deeply nested formulae, hindering their application in autonomous planning and control.

Method: Develops an operator acting on reachability value functions, establishing a new theoretical framework that directly implements operator-based nesting rules rather than designing STL-based reachability or control barrier functions.

Result: Theoretically derives necessary and sufficient conditions for STL formula satisfaction; demonstrates expressiveness through simulations with complex fragments.

Conclusion: The operator-based approach provides a more expressive theoretical framework for handling multi-nested STL formulae and enables practical online control synthesis.

Abstract: Signal Temporal Logic (STL), has recently seen extensive development, owing to its rich expressivenes for autonomous planning and control. Nevertheless, existing verification and control synthesis methods are limited with respect to the complexity and degree of nesting of the formulae. In this work, we propose a novel approach to STL based on an operator acting on reachability value functions. This constitutes a new theoretical framework for handling complex multi-nested formulae while at the same time providing tools for on-line control synthesis. In contrast to focusing on the design of STL-based reachability (or control barrier) functions, we develop operator-based nesting rules directly. Our method's expressiveness is demonstrated both theoretically, where necessary and sufficient conditions for STL formula satisfaction are extracted, as well as in simulations with complex fragments.

</details>


### [171] [ICAN-Deploy: Identity-Stable Canary Deployment for Safety-Critical Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28097)
*Xue Qin,Simin Luan,John See,Zeyd Boukhers,Cong Yang,Zhijun Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ICAN-Deploy maintains cryptographic identity stability across canary deployments by separating capability names from versions, enabling safety-critical agents to evolve without re-certification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mainstream canary deployment controllers (Argo Rollouts, Spinnaker, Flagger) alter the cryptographic identity during canary windows, which is harmless for stateless microservices but breaks the safety guarantee that "the agent you certified is still the agent you have" for embodied agents, forcing costly re-certification per canary cycle.

Method: ICAN-Deploy is a middleware construction using a state machine that holds an identity hash invariant across the canary window by separating capability names (frozen, hashed) from capability versions (mutable runtime state). Implemented inside a runtime governance layer for LLM-driven robots. Verified via closed-form proof, AST lint, and TLA+ model-checking, plus empirical testing over N=100 canary cycles on a Franka Panda arm in MuJoCo.

Result: Zero identity drift observed across 100 canary cycles; entry latency 95% BCa CI [1.52, 2.01] ms. A feature-flagged strawman that folds versions into the manifest falsified on the same workload.

Conclusion: A system certified once at identity-creation time can ship arbitrary capability evolution under that same certification, within the version-and-name envelope, eliminating the need for re-certification per canary update in safety-critical embodied agents.

Abstract: Canary deployment routes a fraction of traffic to a new software version, monitors metrics, and rolls back on regression. Mainstream controllers (Argo Rollouts, Spinnaker, Flagger) change the deployed system's cryptographic identity during the canary window. The drift is harmless for stateless microservices but breaks the claim that "the agent you certified is still the agent you have" for safety-critical embodied agents, forcing re-certification per canary. We present ICAN-Deploy (Identity-stable CANary Deployment), a middleware construction whose state machine holds the identity hash invariant across the canary window by separating capability names (frozen, hashed) from capability versions (mutable runtime state). We implement ICAN-Deploy inside a runtime governance layer for LLM-driven robots and verify invariance by closed-form proof, AST lint, and TLA+ model-checking, then corroborate over N=100 real canary cycles on a Franka Panda arm in MuJoCo (zero drift; entry latency 95% BCa CI [1.52, 2.01] ms). A feature-flagged strawman that folds versions into the manifest falsifies on the same workload. A system certified once at identity-creation time can then ship arbitrary capability evolution under that same certification, within the version-and-name envelope.

</details>


### [172] [STR Robot: Design of an Autonomous Mobile Robot from Simulation to Reality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28110)
*Vinh Nguyen,Gia-Uy Le,Tien-Dat Nguyen,Tri-Tin Nguyen,Vinh-Hao Nguyen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a simulation-to-real implementation of an autonomous mobile robot, focusing on onboard control, self-localization, and autonomous navigation. The framework is developed and tested in simulation before deployment on a real robot, demonstrating that simulation provides an effective foundation for developing reliable autonomous robotic systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To leverage simulation tools for efficient development and validation of autonomous robotic systems before real-world deployment, reducing the gap between simulation and real-world performance.

Method: The authors developed onboard control, self-localization, and autonomous navigation on an existing mechanical platform, first testing the framework in simulation and then deploying it on a real robot for experimental evaluation.

Result: The proposed approach demonstrated feasibility, with simulation providing an effective foundation for developing reliable autonomous mobile robot systems that transfer successfully to real-world operation.

Conclusion: Simulation-to-real transfer is a viable strategy for developing autonomous mobile robots, enabling efficient development and validation before real-world deployment.

Abstract: With the rapid development of simulation tools, the development and validation of autonomous robotic systems have become more efficient before real-world deployment. This paper presents a simulation-to-real implementation of an autonomous mobile robot based on an existing mechanical platform. Instead of focusing on mechanical design, our work concentrates on the development of the onboard control, self-localization, and autonomous navigation system. The proposed robot is equipped with onboard sensing and computation to estimate its pose and navigate autonomously in the environment. The overall framework is first developed and tested in simulation, and then deployed on the real robot for experimental evaluation. The results demonstrate the feasibility of the proposed approach and show that simulation provides an effective foundation for developing reliable autonomous mobile robot systems. The source code will be released at https://ntdathp.github.io/outdoor-robot-web.

</details>


### [173] [Provably Guaranteed Polytopic Uncertainty Quantification for SLAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28172)
*Guangyang Zeng,Yulong Gao,Yuan Shen,Lingpeng Chen,Haoying Li,Guodong Shi,Junfeng Wu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents provably guaranteed uncertainty quantification algorithms for 3D-3D landmark-based SLAM using polytope representations, with three modules (forward UQ for mapping, backward UQ for pose tracking, and pose compound) that produce certified uncertainty sets with deterministic containment guarantees, enhanced by conformal prediction for practical calibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety-critical robotics requires guaranteed and practical uncertainty quantification in perception, but existing works lack formal containment guarantees, rely on restrictive assumptions, or focus only on pose estimation rather than complete SLAM pipelines.

Method: Three UQ modules using polytopes for uncertainty representation: forward UQ (mapping), backward UQ (pose tracking), and pose compound. Conformal prediction calibrates measurement uncertainty from data with prescribed probability.

Result: Simulations and experiments demonstrate strong theoretical guarantees and practical usability of the proposed algorithms.

Conclusion: The proposed polytope-based UQ algorithms provide deterministic containment guarantees for both poses and landmarks in SLAM, with practical calibration via conformal prediction, making them suitable for safety-critical robotics.

Abstract: In safety-critical robotics applications, guaranteed and practical uncertainty quantification (UQ) in perception is vital. Many existing works either offer no formal containment guarantee, rely on restrictive modeling assumptions, or focus only on pose estimation rather than a complete SLAM pipeline. This paper presents provably guaranteed UQ algorithms for 3D-3D landmark-based SLAM. The algorithms consist of three basic UQ modules: forward UQ for mapping, backward UQ for pose tracking, and pose compound. Each module produces a certified uncertainty set; when the input uncertainty bounds are deterministic, the output sets inherit deterministic guarantees, i.e., they provably contain the true poses and landmarks. Specifically, we use polytopes to represent uncertainty sets, enabling tractable computations and a unified treatment of pose uncertainty. To enhance algorithms' practical usability, we incorporate conformal prediction to calibrate measurement uncertainty from data with prescribed probability. Simulations and experiments demonstrate that the proposed algorithms provide both strong theoretical guarantees and practical usability. The code is open-sourced at https://github.com/LIAS-CUHKSZ/Polytopic-SLAM-Uncertainty-Quantification.

</details>


### [174] [Visualizing Latent Phase Structures in Locomotion Policies: A Multi-Environment Study with Temporal Feature Extension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28186)
*Daisuke Yasui,Toshitaka Matuki,Hiroshi Sato*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a framework to uncover latent motion phase structures (e.g., stance/swing phases) from DRL locomotion policies by clustering augmented features (observations, actions, next states, next actions) with a novel cluster-count determination method that suppresses self-transitions. Applied to Ant-v5, HalfCheetah-v5, and Walker2D-v5, it identifies clearer and more regular phase transitions than existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although DRL achieves high performance on locomotion control tasks, visualizing the internal motion structures learned by the policy network remains challenging. Biomechanics research shows locomotion is realized through repeating motion phases (e.g., stance/swing), motivating the need to uncover these latent phase structures from DRL policies.

Method: The method extends clustering features from state observations alone to augmented features including actions, next states, and next actions. It also introduces a technique to determine the optimal number of clusters by suppressing self-transitions (i.e., incentivizing transitions between different phases rather than staying in the same phase).

Result: Applied to Ant-v5, HalfCheetah-v5, and Walker2D-v5, the proposed method successfully identified phase structures with clearer and more regular transition rules compared to existing methods.

Conclusion: The proposed framework effectively uncovers interpretable motion phase structures from DRL locomotion policies, offering better insight into how these policies structure movement over time through regular phase transitions.

Abstract: Deep reinforcement learning (DRL) has been shown to achieve high performance on locomotion control tasks in MuJoCo benchmarks such as HalfCheetah, Ant, and Walker2D. However, visualizing the motion structures internally obtained by a trained policy function implemented as a deep neural network remains challenging. It is known from biomechanics and related fields that locomotion control is realized through the repetition of motion phases such as the stance phase and swing phase. In this study, we propose a framework for uncovering latent motion phase structures from trajectories generated by locomotion control policies through interaction with the environment. The proposed method extends the clustering features from state observations alone to augmented features including actions, next states, and next actions, and introduces a method for determining the number of clusters that suppresses self-transitions. Applying the proposed method to three environments -- Ant-v5, HalfCheetah-v5, and Walker2D-v5 -- we successfully identified phase structures with clearer and more regular transition rules than those obtained by the existing method.

</details>


### [175] [Natural Functional Gradients for Smooth Trajectory Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28202)
*Kisang Park,Chanwoo Kim,Kyungjae Lee,Sungjoon Choi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A trajectory optimization framework using natural functional gradients that performs geometry-aware updates in function space to generate collision-free, smooth motions in cluttered environments, with a Monte-Carlo estimator for black-box trajectory evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generating collision-free and smooth motions remains challenging in robotic manipulation, especially in cluttered environments and narrow passages where feasible regions are highly constrained and fragmented.

Method: Proposes a trajectory optimization framework that performs geometry-aware updates directly in function space using natural functional gradients, optimizes a Gaussian-smoothed surrogate objective, and derives a practical Monte-Carlo estimator of the natural functional gradient requiring only black-box trajectory evaluations.

Result: Improves trajectory feasibility and produces smoother motions than representative planning and trajectory optimization baselines in environments with narrow geometric clearances on constrained robotic manipulation tasks.

Conclusion: The method enables effective trajectory optimization in highly constrained environments by leveraging functional gradient updates that decouple trajectory regularity from time discretization and work with black-box evaluations.

Abstract: Generating collision-free and smooth motions remains a central challenge in robotic manipulation, particularly in cluttered environments and narrow passages where feasible regions are highly constrained and fragmented. We propose a trajectory optimization framework that performs geometry-aware updates directly in function space using natural functional gradients. The method optimizes a Gaussian-smoothed surrogate objective that regularizes the optimization landscape through smooth trajectory perturbations while preserving trajectory-level structure. Because the updates are defined intrinsically in function space, trajectory regularity can be controlled independently of a particular time discretization. We derive a practical Monte-Carlo estimator of the natural functional gradient that requires only black-box trajectory evaluations, making the method applicable when analytic gradients are unavailable or unreliable due to collision checking and contact-rich simulation. Experiments on constrained robotic manipulation tasks demonstrate that the proposed method improves trajectory feasibility and produces smoother motions than representative planning and trajectory optimization baselines in environments with narrow geometric clearances. Additional results, videos, and implementation details are available at the project page: https://kisangpark.github.io/natural-functional-gradient/

</details>


### [176] [ProgVLA: Progress-Aware Robot Manipulation Skill Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28231)
*Seungsu Kim,Jinyoung Choi,Seungmin Baek,Jean-Michel Renders*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ProgVLA is a compact VLA model for robot manipulation that uses a two-stage Perceiver resampler to compress multi-modal sequences and offline RL-based progress heads to estimate task progress, achieving competitive or superior performance on long-horizon tasks with only 0.1B parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable reliable robot manipulation under tight compute and memory budgets by efficiently processing long multi-modal sequences and maintaining explicit task progress representations over extended horizons.

Method: (1) A multi-modal encoder with a two-stage Perceiver resampling scheme compresses visual, language, and proprioceptive streams into fixed context tokens. (2) Auxiliary progress heads trained with offline RL objectives jointly learn critics over normalized remaining-horizon targets, enabling advantage- and success-weighted flow-matching imitation learning.

Result: A 0.1B-parameter ProgVLA achieves success rates competitive with substantially larger pretrained baselines, and exceeds them on long-horizon and harder task tiers. Ablations show the context resampler and task-adaptive visual fine-tuning are the largest contributors, with progress-aware training providing additional gains on long-horizon and multi-object tasks. Validated in real-world toy-kitchen environments.

Conclusion: Compact VLA models with explicit progress estimation can match or outperform much larger models, especially on long-horizon tasks, suggesting parameter-efficient architectures with progress-aware training are a promising direction for practical robot manipulation.

Abstract: We present ProgVLA, a compact vision-language-action (VLA) model designed for reliable robot manipulation under tight compute and memory budgets. The model specifically focuses on efficiently processing long multi-modal sequences by maintaining an explicit representation of task progress over extended horizons. To this end, ProgVLA integrates two key components. First, a multi-modal encoder with a two-stage Perceiver resampling scheme compresses variable-length visual, language, and proprioceptive streams into a fixed set of control-ready context tokens, substantially reducing sequence length while preserving cross-modal grounding. Second, an auxiliary set of progress heads is trained with offline reinforcement learning (RL) objectives to jointly learn critics over normalized remaining-horizon targets. This provides the policy with an internal estimate of task progress and enables advantage- and success-weighted flow-matching imitation learning. On two well-established multi-task robot manipulation benchmarks, a 0.1B-parameter ProgVLA model reaches success rates that are competitive with, and on long-horizon and harder task tiers exceed, substantially larger pretrained baselines. Ablations indicate that the learned context resampler and task-adaptive visual fine-tuning are the largest single contributors, while progress-aware training provides a consistent additional gain that is concentrated on long-horizon and multi-object tasks. We further validate the approach in real-world toy-kitchen environments.

</details>


### [177] [POINav: Benchmarking and Enhancing Final-Meters Arrival in Real-World Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28237)
*Ruiyan Gong,Meisheng Zhang,Yuxiang Zhao,Mingchao Sun,Yanfen Shen,Zedong Chu,Zhining Gu,Wei Guo,Xiaolong Cheng,Qiming Li,Kangning Niu,Yanqing Zhu,Xiaolong Wu,Tianlun Li,Mu Xu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: POINav-Bench is the first benchmark for closed-loop evaluation of real-world POI-goal navigation, featuring 11 commercial areas reconstructed via 3DGS with 163 POIs. The POINav Brain-Action Framework combines POI-grounded reasoning with continuous waypoint prediction to address the "final-meters" challenge in navigation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VLN benchmarks for POI-goal navigation suffer from coarse granularity and significant sim-to-real gaps due to generated scenes, failing to address the critical "final-meters" challenge of reaching precise POIs in real-world navigation.

Method: Created POINav-Bench using 11 real commercial areas reconstructed via 3D Gaussian Splatting (126,398 m², 163 POIs) with traversability-aware annotations and reference trajectories. Proposed POINav Brain-Action Framework where Brain module performs POI-grounded reasoning to guide Action module for continuous waypoint prediction. Curated POINav-Dataset with 70K real-world signage-entrance pairs.

Result: The POINav Brain-Action Framework provides a viable path toward refining real-world POI-goal navigation, validated through experiments on the POINav-Bench benchmark.

Conclusion: POINav-Bench bridges the sim-to-real gap for POI-goal navigation evaluation, and the Brain-Action framework offers an effective approach for precise real-world POI navigation.

Abstract: Real-world navigation is fundamentally driven by Points of Interest (POIs), yet reaching a precise POI remains a critical "final-meters" challenge. Existing Vision-Language Navigation (VLN) benchmarks of POI-goal navigation often suffer from coarse granularity or significant sim-to-real gaps due to generated scene. To bridge this gap, we present POINav-Bench, the first benchmark designed for closed-loop evaluation of real-world POI-goal navigation. It comprises 11 commercial areas reconstructed from real-world captures using 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS), covering 126,398 $m^{2}$ in total and spanning 163 distinct POIs. With traversability-aware annotations and reference trajectories, POINav-Bench enables high-fidelity evaluation of navigation agents in realistic, POI-rich real-world environments. Building on this, we propose the POINav Brain-Action Framework where a Brain module performs POI-grounded reasoning to guide an Action module in predicting continuous waypoints for real-world execution. We further curate the POINav-Dataset, containing 70K real-world signage-entrance pairs. Experiments show that our framework provides a viable path toward refining real-world POI-goal navigation.

</details>


### [178] [Natural Locomotion: Principle and Method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28254)
*Mirado Mortel,Luc Jaulin,Lionel Lapierre,Simon Rohou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper formulates natural robotic locomotion as an exchange principle where motion is mediated by environmental constraints. It introduces the Natural Locomotion Manifold (NLM) based on zero mean Propulsion-Oscillator Exchange (POE) power, develops a closed/open construction method, and demonstrates it on two nonholonomic systems, showing that natural locomotion becomes a design question about passive architectures supporting certified NLM families.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To formulate a principled framework for natural robotic locomotion that leverages passive dynamics, compliance, and resonance rather than prescribed trajectories, by treating locomotion as an internal exchange with environment-mediated propulsive channels.

Method: A closed/open construction method: (1) close the propulsive channel to reveal an effective internal oscillator with scalar action-angle structure (single DOF) or nonlinear modal sectors (multiple DOFs); (2) reopen the channel, reconstruct pose, and accept cycles preserving internal recurrence and zero mean POE power.

Result: Demonstrated on Chaplygin-sleigh/pendulum-driven car and a three-body extension. For scalar case, POE closure equals missing internal return condition with theorem-backed NLM computation. For multi-DOF case, POE closure plus modal identity, internal return, dynamics consistency, fixed passive architecture, and nonzero displacement are required.

Conclusion: Natural locomotion reframes as a design question: which passive architectures support no, one, or several certified Natural Locomotion Manifold families.

Abstract: Robotic locomotion can become efficient when mechanisms exploit passive dynamics, compliance, and resonance rather than track prescribed trajectories. This paper formulates natural locomotion as an exchange principle for systems whose motion is mediated by environmental constraints or interactions. A motion is natural when an internal oscillator returns periodically, the body pose drifts, and the mean Propulsion--Oscillator Exchange power (POE power) vanishes over one cycle. The selected family is a Natural Locomotion Manifold (NLM). We develop the conservative realization of this principle for continuous ideal environmental constraints: the constraints do no external work, total mechanical energy is conserved, and zero mean POE power is an internal exchange with the environment-mediated propulsive channel, not external energy input.
  The method is a closed/open construction. The propulsive channel is first closed to reveal an effective internal oscillator, organized by scalar action-angle structure in one effective degree of freedom or by nonlinear modal sectors in several degrees of freedom. The channel is then reopened, pose is reconstructed, and accepted cycles must preserve internal recurrence and zero mean POE power.
  We demonstrate the principle on two ideal nonholonomic no-slip systems: a Chaplygin-sleigh / pendulum-driven car and a three-body extension. In the scalar case, POE closure is equivalent to the missing internal return condition, giving a theorem-backed computation of the NLM family. In the multi-degree case, POE closure remains necessary but must be completed by modal identity, internal return, dynamics consistency, same fixed passive architecture, and nonzero displacement. Natural locomotion becomes a design question: which passive architectures support no, one, or several certified NLM families?

</details>


### [179] [IMU Propagation as Preintegration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28279)
*Jianzhu Huai*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: IMU preintegration and propagation are mathematically equivalent; one can wrap propagation routines to obtain preintegrated measurements and vice versa, enabling code reuse and cross-validation between implementations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: IMU preintegration is widely used in sensor fusion but is often implemented separately from conventional IMU propagation, leading to redundant code and potential inconsistencies across frameworks.

Method: The authors formally prove equivalence between IMU preintegration and propagation, propose a wrapping method to derive preintegrated measurements, bias Jacobians, and covariance from existing propagation routines (and vice versa), and validate with RK4 propagation vs. GTSAM's preintegration modules on random IMU sequences.

Result: Close agreement between RK4-based propagation and GTSAM's tangent and manifold preintegration modules in recovered Jacobians, covariances, and transition matrices.

Conclusion: IMU preintegration and propagation are equivalent; practitioners can reuse existing propagation code for preintegration tasks, simplifying implementation and enabling consistency checks across different error-state definitions.

Abstract: IMU preintegration is widely used in factor-graph-based visual--inertial, lidar--inertial, and radar--inertial state estimation, yet it is often treated as a specialized implementation separate from conventional IMU propagation. This note shows that IMU preintegration and propagation are equivalent realizations of the same underlying computation. We present a convention-agnostic view in which the preintegrated measurement, bias Jacobians, and covariance can be obtained by wrapping an existing IMU propagation routine, while a preintegration module can conversely recover state-transition matrices and propagated covariances. This perspective simplifies the reuse of existing propagation code, supports translation across different error-state definitions, and provides practical consistency checks for preintegration implementations. Experiments with random IMU sequences demonstrate close agreement between an RK4-based propagation implementation and GTSAM's tangent and manifold preintegration modules in the recovered Jacobians, covariances, and transition matrices.

</details>


### [180] [EventShiftFlow: Towards Hardware-efficient FPGA-based Flow Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28312)
*Arianna Alonso Bizzi,Fernando Cladera,C. J. Taylor*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A streaming velocity estimator for event-based vision using fixed-width integer logic (shift registers, counters, comparators, LUT-mapped multiplies) with no dividers, no DSP blocks, no floating-point, and no frame reconstruction. Achieves 99.5% directional accuracy on real data, uses <2 kB storage, and is prototyped on an Artix-7 FPGA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Event-based vision sensors provide asynchronous, high-temporal-resolution measurements for low-latency robotic perception, but existing motion estimation methods are computationally intensive and difficult to map to FPGA hardware.

Method: Discretizes asynchronous events into fixed-duration time bins, constructs a 1-bit spatial occupancy grid, and evaluates multiple velocity hypotheses in parallel using fixed-width integer logic (shift registers, counters, comparators, small LUT-mapped multiplies) with no dividers, DSP blocks, floating-point arithmetic, or iterative optimization.

Result: On noisy synthetic data, recovers both magnitude and direction (magnitude most challenged with intersecting objects). On real event-camera sequence, directional accuracy reaches 99.5% across four motion segments. Performance robust across 10-40% occupancy densities. Requires <2 kB of storage; single-axis prototype implemented on Xilinx Artix-7.

Conclusion: The method trades dense sub-pixel optical flow for sparse, quantized velocity estimates per pixel, suited to low-latency tasks like reactive obstacle avoidance on SWaP-constrained platforms.

Abstract: Event-based vision sensors offer asynchronous, high-temporal-resolution measurements that are attractive for low-latency robotic perception, but many event-based motion estimation methods are computationally intensive and difficult to map to FPGA hardware. We present a streaming velocity estimator that discretizes asynchronous events into fixed-duration time bins, constructs a 1-bit spatial occupancy grid, and evaluates multiple velocity hypotheses in parallel using only fixed-width integer logic - shift registers, counters, comparators, and small LUT-mapped multiplies - with no dividers and no DSP blocks. It requires no frame reconstruction, no floating-point arithmetic, and no iterative optimization. The method deliberately trades dense sub-pixel optical flow for a sparse, quantized velocity estimate at each active pixel, suited to low-latency tasks such as reactive obstacle avoidance on size-, weight-, and power-constrained platforms. On noisy synthetic data with known ground-truth velocities, the method recovers both magnitude and direction, with magnitude estimates being most challenged when objects of different velocities intersect. On a real event-camera sequence, directional accuracy reaches 99.5% across all four evaluated motion segments, with performance remaining robust across occupancy densities in the 10-40% range. We characterize the algorithm's density-dependent behavior, present a parameter sensitivity analysis, show that the proposed datapath requires less than 2 kB of storage, and implement a single-axis prototype on a low-cost Xilinx Artix-7.

</details>


### [181] [Identifying Explicit Parsimonious Piece-wise Polynomial Relationships in Industrial time-series: Application to manipulator robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28320)
*Mazen Alamir,Sacha Clavel*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes an algorithm that converts parsimonious implicit polynomial relationships into explicit piece-wise polynomial representations for anomaly detection. It demonstrates the framework on a 6-axis robot's inverse model and tests generalization against DNNs on a 4-axis robot under unseen contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the challenge of identifying parsimonious explicit piece-wise polynomial relationships from a large number of raw features, building on prior work on implicit relationships for anomaly detection and localization.

Method: The algorithm extends a prior identification method that yields parsimonious implicit relationships by deriving explicit piece-wise representations from the set of polynomials involved in the implicit representations.

Result: Applied to a 6-axis manipulator robot's inverse model and tested on a 4-axis robot, showing generalization capability compared to state-of-the-art DNNs under unseen contexts.

Conclusion: The explicit piece-wise polynomial representations obtained from implicit relationships show competitive generalization performance against DNNs, particularly when models face unseen operational contexts.

Abstract: This paper addresses the problem of identifying parsimonious explicit piece-wise polynomial relationships that might involve a relatively large number of raw features. The algorithm leverages a recently proposed identification algorithm that yields parsimonious implicit relationships enabling to derive normality characterization in the context of anomaly detection and localization. The algorithm proposed in this paper goes a step further by deriving explicit piece-wise representations that are built using the set of polynomials involved in the implicit representations. The framework is illustrated on the problem of identifying parsimonious explicit representations of the inverse model of a 6-axis manipulator robot. Moreover, further experiments on a 4-axis robot are also shown which are designed to investigate the generalization capability of parsimonious models compared to state-of-the-art DNNs structures, when models face unseen contexts of use.

</details>


### [182] [Chance-Constrained MPPI under State and Dynamic Object Prediction Uncertainty and the Evaluation of Collision Risk Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28330)
*Benjamin Serfling,Konrad Doll,Kati Radkhah-Lens*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper identifies that chance-constrained MPPI control suffers from miscalibrated upstream uncertainties (localization & perception), causing overconfidence (safety violations) or underconfidence (conservative freezing).
