The authors propose DUCCT-MPPI, a real-time risk-aware planning architecture integrating localization uncertainty via Unscented Transform and dynamic obstacle prediction uncertainty via Monte Carlo aggregation, achieving 28% higher navigation success than baselines in cluttered environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing chance-constrained MPPI methods implicitly assume well-calibrated upstream uncertainties from localization and perception. In practice, estimators are miscalibrated, leading to overconfidence (safety violations) or underconfidence (freezing/probability dilution). There is a lack of rigorous evaluation methodology for the statistical validity of predicted collision risks during closed-loop execution.

Method: The paper contributes (1) a rigorous evaluation methodology using proper scoring rules to assess statistical validity of predicted collision risks during closed-loop execution, and (2) DUCCT-MPPI, a real-time risk-aware planning architecture that jointly integrates localization uncertainty via a one-tube Unscented Transform (UT) approximation and dynamic obstacle prediction uncertainty via Monte Carlo aggregation.

Result: In highly cluttered environments, DUCCT-MPPI outperforms established Monte Carlo MPPI baselines by nearly 28% in navigation success rate, while recording the lowest travel times and minimizing induced social forces. The framework demonstrates robust failure-mitigation, transitioning to safe conservative maneuvering without functional deadlocks.

Conclusion: Reliable probabilistic safety in autonomous navigation requires not only expressive risk models but also statistically valid uncertainty estimates throughout the entire autonomy stack.

Abstract: Chance-constrained Model Predictive Path Integral (MPPI) control is increasingly adopted for navigation in dynamic environments to explicitly bound collision risk. However, these probabilistic guarantees implicitly assume that upstream uncertainties from localization and perception are well-calibrated. In practice, estimators are often miscalibrated, inducing characteristic closed-loop failure modes: overconfidence leads to systematic safety violations, while underconfidence triggers overly conservative freezing or probability dilution. To address this critical gap, our primary contribution is a rigorous evaluation methodology applying proper scoring rules to assess the statistical validity of predicted collision risks during closed-loop execution. Concurrently, Dual-Uncertainty Chance-Constrained Tube MPPI (DUCCT-MPPI) is proposed as a real-time, risk-aware planning architecture. DUCCT-MPPI jointly integrates localization uncertainty via a one-tube Unscented Transform (UT) approximation and dynamic obstacle prediction uncertainty via Monte Carlo aggregation. Through extensive physics-based simulations, the framework demonstrates robust failure-mitigation, seamlessly transitioning to safe, conservative maneuvering without succumbing to functional deadlocks in highly cluttered environments. In highly cluttered environments, DUCCT-MPPI achieves superior robustness, outperforming established Monte Carlo MPPI baselines by nearly 28\% in navigation success rate, while simultaneously recording the lowest travel times and minimizing induced social forces. Ultimately, these findings establish that reliable probabilistic safety in autonomous navigation dictates not only expressive risk models but statistically valid uncertainty estimates throughout the entire autonomy stack.

</details>


### [183] [Magnet-Based Soft Robotic Skin Using a 3D-Printed Multi-Lattice Structure and CNN-Based Tactile Super-Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28352)
*Yunseong Bang,Joowon Park,Suan Sim,Youngjun Ryu,Sukho Park,Kyungseo Park*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A magnet-based robotic skin with a multilayer soft lattice, Hall-effect sensor arrays, and a tactile super-resolution CNN model enables large-area, high-accuracy contact localization and normal force estimation for whole-body robotic skin and safe human-robot interaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome limitations of existing tactile skins (small sensing areas, blind spots, low spatial resolution) by developing a scalable, conformable robotic skin with minimal blind spots and tunable mechanical compliance.

Method: A multilayer soft lattice embedded with permanent magnets is integrated with distributed Hall-effect sensor arrays. External contact forces deform the lattice, altering magnetic fields detected by sensors with large overlapping receptive fields. Lattice parameters are tunable for joint compliance/transduction adjustment. Fabrication uses implicit modeling and SLS 3D printing. Tactile super-resolution is achieved via a CNN trained on experimental measurements to estimate contact location and normal force in real time.

Result: Experiments validated localization accuracy of the system and demonstrated scalability to larger surfaces, indicating potential for whole-body robotic skin applications.

Conclusion: The proposed magnet-based robotic skin design with soft lattice, Hall-effect sensors, and CNN-based tactile super-resolution is effective for large-area tactile sensing, promising for whole-body robotic skin and safe human-robot interaction.

Abstract: This paper presents a magnet-based robotic skin that integrates a multilayer soft lattice with distributed Hall-effect sensor arrays and a tactile super-resolution model. External contact forces are converted to magnetic field changes by embedded permanent magnets, and the lattice spreads these changes across the sensing domain. This gives each sensor a large, overlapping receptive field and enables a large sensing area with minimal blind spots. Lattice parameters are tunable, enabling joint adjustment of mechanical compliance and transduction characteristics. An implicit modeling workflow and selective laser sintering (SLS) 3D printing support rapid fabrication of conformal, high-complexity structures. A convolutional neural network trained on experimental measurements estimates contact location and normal force in real time. Experiments validate localization accuracy and indicate scalability to larger surfaces, suggesting applicability to whole-body robotic skin and safe human-robot interaction.

</details>


### [184] [Accelerating Robot Path Planning via Connectivity-Preserving Region Proposal Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28362)
*Zhanzheng Ma,Cancan Zhao,Shuai Zhang,Bo Ouyang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CP-RPN is a segmentation-guided model using Deformable Attention Transformer and topological loss functions to predict compact, connected corridor regions for mobile robot path planning, reducing search space by 60.13% and achieving 99.60% success rate with 0.11s average planning time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional sampling-based path planning suffers from large search spaces and latency, while learning-based methods face local region fragmentation and global topological inconsistency.

Method: CP-RPN uses a Deformable Attention Transformer (DAT) with a deconvolutional decoder to segment compact corridor regions; a composite loss (Cross-Entropy + Connectivity-Aware + Topological Continuity via persistent homology) ensures connectivity; Voronoi diagram + A* fallback plans the path within predicted regions.

Result: Reduces candidate region size by 60.13% over MPT baseline, achieves 0.11s average planning time, 99.60% success rate, outperforming traditional sampling-based methods in stability.

Conclusion: The proposed CP-RPN effectively compresses the planning search space while maintaining topological connectivity, enabling deterministic low-latency and high-success-rate path planning.

Abstract: Mobile robot path planning methods are often constrained by vast search spaces, resulting in latency in samplingbased algorithms. Learning-based approaches frequently suffer from local region fragmentation and global topological inconsistency. To tackle the problem, we present the Connectivity- Preserving Region Proposal Network (CP-RPN), a segmentationguided model designed to predict compact and topologically connected candidate regions, significantly compressing the search space. Specifically, we design a segmentation model that leverages a Deformable Attention Transformer (DAT) to capture long-range dependencies for global connectivity, with a Deconvolutional decoder to preserve fine-grained spatial details. To guarantee the connectivity of the predicted mask, we design a composite loss function that combines Cross-Entropy loss for pixelwise supervision, a Connectivity-Aware loss to enhance local coherence, and a Topological Continuity loss based on persistent homology to enforce global connectivity. Building on these highconnectivity corridor-like regions, the Voronoi diagram is used to plan the path, backed by a local A* fallback mechanism to ensure robustness. Experimental results demonstrate that CPRPN reduces the candidate region size by over 60.13% compared to the MPT baseline and achieves deterministic low-latency planning (avg. 0.11s) with a 99.60% success rate, outperforming traditional sampling-based algorithms in stability.

</details>


### [185] [Safety-Critical Adaptive Impedance Control via Nonsmooth Control Barrier Functions under State and Input Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28367)
*Faisal Lawan,Xiaoran Han,Joaquin Carrasco,Barry Lennox,Xiaoxiao Cheng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes an online adaptive impedance control framework for robotic manipulators that ensures safe joint-state constraints and compliant interaction under uncertain dynamics, using a quadratic-program-based safety filter with a non-smooth control barrier function, interval type-2 fuzzy compensation, soft torque constraints, and disturbance-observer-based robustness, with formal safety and stability guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safe physical interaction is critical for deploying robotic manipulators in human-robot interaction and contact-rich tasks, where uncertainty, external forces, and actuator limitations can compromise both performance and safety.

Method: Combines a quadratic-program-based safety filter with a composed position-velocity non-smooth control barrier function (NCBF) for unified relative-degree-one barrier enforcement; online interval type-2 fuzzy logic system for unknown dynamics compensation; soft constraints with exact penalty recovery for actuator torque limits; disturbance-observer-enhanced safety mechanism for robustness.

Result: Simulations on a 7-DOF manipulator with severe parametric uncertainty and external interaction wrenches demonstrate safe constraint satisfaction and robust impedance tracking.

Conclusion: Composite Lyapunov analysis proves forward invariance of the safe set and uniform ultimate boundedness of impedance-tracking error, confirming the framework's effectiveness.

Abstract: Safe physical interaction is critical for deploying robotic manipulators in human-robot interaction and contact-rich tasks, where uncertainty, external forces, and actuator limitations can compromise both performance and safety. We propose an online adaptive impedance control framework that enforces joint-state safety while achieving compliant interaction under uncertain dynamics. The approach combines a quadratic-program-based safety filter with a novel composed position-velocity non-smooth control barrier function (NCBF), enabling joint position and velocity constraints to be enforced through a unified relative-degree-one barrier. Unknown dynamics are compensated online using an interval type-2 fuzzy logic system, while actuator torque limits are handled through soft constraints with exact penalty recovery of feasible solutions. A disturbance-observer-enhanced safety mechanism improves robustness against modelling errors and external interaction forces. Using composite Lyapunov analysis, we prove forward invariance of the safe set and the uniform ultimately boundedness of the impedance-tracking error. Simulations on a 7-DOF manipulator with severe parametric uncertainty and external interaction wrenches demonstrate safe constraint satisfaction and robust impedance tracking.

</details>


### [186] [Tactile-Proprioceptive Sensor Fusion for Contact Wrench Estimation in Whole-Body Physical Human-Robot Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28412)
*Junha Min,Junghyeon Ma,Jiwung Kwon,Sunggyu Bae,Joohyung Kim,Kyungseo Park*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a tactile-proprioceptive sensor fusion framework using pneumatic skin pads and motor-current-based proprioception with a TCN to mitigate friction hysteresis, enabling sensitive contact detection and multi-axis force reconstruction for natural physical human-robot interaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct physical guidance is a natural way to teach and interact with robots, and robotic skins enable sensitive contact sensing. However, ambiguity between frictional residues and applied external forces limits contact detection accuracy.

Method: A tactile-proprioceptive sensor fusion framework is proposed. Tactile cues from pneumatic skin pads serve as contact indicators to bypass friction ambiguity. Motor-current-based proprioception is fused to reconstruct multi-axis contact forces. A temporal convolutional network (TCN) mitigates friction hysteresis during stick-slip transitions.

Result: Validated on a skin-integrated robot arm: multi-axis forces reconstructed in stationary contacts; simultaneous force estimation and kinesthetic teaching demonstrated. Improved sensitivity and responsiveness compared to tactile-only and proprioceptive-only baselines.

Conclusion: Tactile-proprioceptive fusion is a reliable pathway to safe, intuitive physical human-robot interaction, outperforming single-modality baselines.

Abstract: Direct physical guidance is a natural means of teaching and interacting with robots, and robotic skins make a key contribution by enabling sensitive contact sensing and localization. This paper presents a tactile-proprioceptive sensor fusion framework for natural physical human-robot interaction. Tactile cues from pneumatic skin pads serve as contact indicators that bypass the ambiguity between frictional residues and applied external forces, enabling highly sensitive contact detection without explicit friction identification. We fuse these cues with motor-current-based proprioception to reconstruct multi-axis contact forces on the robot surface. To maintain accuracy during motion, we employ a temporal convolutional network (TCN) to mitigate friction hysteresis during stick-slip transitions, reducing uncertainty at contact onset and yielding smooth, responsive guidance. We validate the approach on a skin-integrated robot arm: (i) multi-axis forces are reconstructed in stationary contacts, and (ii) simultaneous force estimation and kinesthetic teaching are demonstrated. Results indicate improved sensitivity and responsiveness across diverse contact conditions compared with tactile-only and proprioceptive-only baselines, supporting tactile-proprioceptive fusion as a reliable pathway to safe, intuitive physical human-robot interaction.

</details>


### [187] [Self-Supervised Online Robot-Agnostic Traversability Estimation for Open-World Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28442)
*Julia Hindel,Simon Bultmann,Houman Masnavi,Daniele Cattaneo,Abhinav Valada*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: COTRATE is an online learning framework for continuous traversability estimation from multimodal, unlabeled robot experience. It uses a robot-agnostic, learning-based online terrain assessment module to infer traversability scores from proprioceptive/inertial signals, then supervises a visual traversability network via an alignment loss. A diversity-aware feature selection strategy mitigates forgetting with minimal overhead, and the representation supports cross-platform knowledge transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-supervised traversability estimation methods either rely on handcrafted proprioceptive scores (limiting robot-agnosticism) or cluster prior data (preventing online learning). Many continual learning approaches incur substantial memory and computational costs hindering onboard deployment.

Method: COTRATE consists of: (1) a robot-agnostic, learning-based online terrain assessment module using proprioceptive/inertial signals to infer robust traversability scores; (2) a visual traversability network supervised via a novel alignment loss that associates visual embeddings with online terrain assessments; (3) a diversity-aware feature selection strategy for compact replay memory to mitigate forgetting during continual learning.

Result: Evaluated on ~50,000 images collected with two robotic platforms across 11 outdoor terrains, and benchmarked on navigation tasks in three representative outdoor environments. Learned representation supports knowledge transfer across different robot platforms with different locomotion kinematics.

Conclusion: COTRATE enables effective online, continual traversability learning without handcrafted scores or prior clustering, supports cross-platform knowledge transfer, and achieves this with minimal memory/computational overhead suitable for onboard deployment.

Abstract: Self-supervised online traversability estimation enables robots to continuously learn from unlabeled open-world experiences and adapt their navigation behavior toward safe and efficient trajectories. Existing approaches either rely on handcrafted proprioceptive traversability scores, limiting robot-agnosticism, or cluster prior data, preventing online learning. Moreover, many continual learning methods incur substantial memory and computational costs, hindering onboard deployment. We introduce COTRATE, an online learning framework for continuous traversability estimation from multimodal, unlabeled robot experience. Our method first infers robust traversability scores using a robot-agnostic, learning-based online terrain assessment module operating on proprioceptiveand inertial signals. These scores then supervise a visual traversability network through a novel alignment loss that associates visual embeddings with online terrain assessments.To mitigate forgetting during continual learning with minimal overhead, we propose a diversity-aware feature selection strategythat preserves performance using a compact replay memory. We further show that the learned traversability representation supports knowledge transfer across different robot platforms with different locomotion kinematics. We evaluate COTRATE on a dataset of \approx 50,000 images collected with two robotic platforms across 11 outdoor terrains, and benchmark it on navigation tasks in three representative outdoor environments. We make the dataset, code, and trained models publicly available.

</details>


### [188] [A Digital Twin Framework for Virtual Visuo-Haptic Teleoperation of Complex-Shaped Optical Microrobots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28448)
*Zongcai Tan,Lan Wei,Dandan Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a digital twin framework for visuo-haptic teleoperation of complex-shaped optical microrobots manipulated by multi-trap optical tweezers. It integrates pose/depth estimation, motion simulation, and model-based haptic rendering. Haptic feedback reduced contact-force variance by 53.2% and improved task success from 30% to 80% in simulated cell-delivery tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visuo-haptic teleoperation frameworks for complex-shaped optical microrobots in multi-trap manipulation scenarios remain underdeveloped, limiting operator awareness of interaction-force cues and trap-stability information in delicate biomedical tasks.

Method: A digital twin framework integrating image-based pose and depth estimation, microrobot motion simulation, and model-based haptic rendering using MSDM model combined with optical-force estimation from the Optical Tweezers Toolbox, all within a ROS-connected bimanual teleoperation system.

Result: Haptic feedback reduced standard deviations of contact-force metric and microrobot-to-trap-center distance by 53.2% and 55.2% respectively, and improved task success rate from 30% to 80% in simulated cell-delivery tasks.

Conclusion: The digital twin framework effectively evaluates visuo-haptic teleoperation strategies for complex-shaped optical microrobots, demonstrating significant improvements in precision and task success through haptic feedback.

Abstract: Optical tweezers (OT) provide piconewton-scale manipulation for delicate biomedical tasks, where visuo-haptic feedback can improve operator awareness by conveying interaction-force cues and trap-stability information. However, visuo-haptic teleoperation frameworks for complex-shaped optical microrobots remain underdeveloped, particularly in multi-trap manipulation scenarios. This paper presents a digital twin framework for virtual visuo-haptic teleoperation of complex-shaped OT-driven microrobots. The framework integrates a digital twin environment, image-based pose and depth estimation, microrobot motion simulation, and model-based haptic rendering within a Robot Operating System (ROS)-connected bimanual teleoperation system. For force modeling, we combine a Multi-Sphere Distributed Manipulation (MSDM) model with optical-force estimation from the Optical Tweezers Toolbox, enabling simulator-driven visuo-haptic feedback. The framework reproduces representative microrobot motion trends and provides haptic force rendering that is numerically consistent with the fitted optical-force model. In simulated cell-delivery tasks, haptic feedback reduced the standard deviations of the contact-force metric and the microrobot-to-trap-center distance metric by 53.2% and 55.2%, respectively, and improved task success from 30% to 80%. These results demonstrate the framework's effectiveness for evaluating visuo-haptic teleoperation strategies for complex-shaped optical microrobots.

</details>


### [189] [Learning a Kinodynamic Trajectory Manifold for Impact-Aware Compliant Catching of Fast-Moving Objects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28462)
*Guorui Pei,Mengshi Zhang,Xi Chen,Jinsong Wu,Jiaming Qi,Peng Zhou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper uses reinforcement learning in simulation to learn a kinodynamic trajectory manifold for fast catching of free-flying objects, enabling direct mapping from object state to reference trajectory without online optimization, with compliant control near contact for improved impact absorption and stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Free-flying object catching is challenging due to short reaction time, impact uncertainty, and kinodynamic constraints.

Method: Reinforcement learning in simulation to collect successful catching trajectories and learn a low-dimensional kinodynamic trajectory manifold. The estimated object initial state directly maps to a reference catching trajectory without online nonlinear optimization. Compliant control is used near contact for impact absorption and stability.

Result: Successful catching trajectories are learned via RL simulation, enabling direct mapping from object state to trajectory without online optimization.

Conclusion: The approach effectively handles the challenges of fast object catching by combining learned trajectory manifold with compliant control.

Abstract: Fast catching of free-flying objects is difficult because of short reaction time, impact uncertainty, and kinodynamic constraints. We use reinforcement learning in simulation to collect successful catching trajectories and learn a low-dimensional kinodynamic trajectory manifold. At run time, the estimated object initial state is mapped directly to a reference catching trajectory without online nonlinear optimization. The trajectory is tracked with compliant control near contact for improved impact absorption and capture stability.

</details>


### [190] [EIT-Pneumatic Hybrid Robotic Skin for Practical and Accurate Force Map Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28468)
*Junhwi Cho,Sunggyu Bae,Junghyeon Ma,Hyosang Lee,Jung Kim,Kyungseo Park*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A hybrid robotic skin combining EIT and pneumatic tactile sensing, fabricated via 3D printing and spray coating, improves force reconstruction accuracy and reduces sensitivity non-uniformity (coefficient of variation from 0.31 to 0.14), validated on a humanoid robot for whole-body tactile sensing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the longstanding limitation of EIT-based tactile sensing — sensitivity non-uniformity across large areas — by combining it with pneumatic sensing for improved force reconstruction capability in robotic skins.

Method: A hybrid robotic skin integrating EIT with pneumatic tactile sensing, fabricated entirely via 3D printing and spray coating; uses Tikhonov-regularized inverse reconstruction with per-pad pneumatic calibration for accurate large-area sensing.

Result: Load-cell indentation experiments showed consistent force reconstruction across locations; coefficient of variation reduced from 0.31 (EIT-only) to 0.14 (hybrid). Pneumatic signals remained reliable during multiple simultaneous contacts on a humanoid robot chest.

Conclusion: The proposed approach provides a practical and scalable path toward accurate whole-body tactile sensing for real robotic systems.

Abstract: We present a hybrid robotic skin that combines electrical impedance tomography (EIT) with pneumatic tactile sensing to improve force reconstruction capability. The developed robotic skin is fabricated entirely by 3D printing and spray coating, making it affordable and easy to build. A Tikhonov-regularized inverse reconstruction, paired with per-pad pneumatic calibration, enables accurate large-area tactile sensing with a simple measurement scheme. For validation, we conducted load-cell indentation experiments; the results showed consistent force reconstruction across locations within a pad. Compared with an EIT-only baseline, sensitivity non-uniformity was also reduced, with the coefficient of variation decreasing from 0.31 to 0.14, indicating that the proposed approach addresses a longstanding limitation of EIT. We further demonstrated chest-mounted integration on a humanoid robot and found that the pneumatic signals remained reliable across diverse contact scenarios, including multiple simultaneous contacts on the same sensing pad. These results indicate a practical path toward accurate, scalable whole-body tactile sensing in real robotic systems.

</details>


### [191] [Mag-VLA: Vision-Language-Action Model for Bimanual Magnetically Actuated Microrobot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28486)
*Yongchen Wang,Kangyi Lu,Lan Wei,Dandan Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Mag-VLA is a vision-language-action model using two robotic arms with magnets for dexterous magnetic microrobot manipulation, achieving up to 90% approach success and 50-80% transport success rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Magnetic microrobot control is challenging due to indirect actuation, limited sensing, and nonlinear magnetic interactions. Bimanual coordination enables capabilities like reorientation but introduces coupled control challenges.

Method: The framework adapts Qwen2.5-VL-7B with LoRA, incorporates a motion-aware phase classifier and phase-conditioned Action Chunking Transformer decoder, and uses a teleoperated dataset covering three task configurations.

Result: ACT-based decoder outperforms alternative generative heads. Real-robot experiments show 90% approach success across tasks; transport success rates of 80%, 70%, and 50% with increasing difficulty.

Conclusion: Hierarchical VLA modeling provides a promising framework for magnetic microrobot manipulation.

Abstract: Magnetically actuated microrobots have been used as wireless, non-contact manipulation tools at microscales, making them promising for minimally invasive applications. However, their control remains challenging due to indirect actuation, limited sensing, and nonlinear magnetic interactions. In this work, we propose Mag-VLA, a vision-language-action (VLA) model for dexterous magnetic microrobot manipulation using two robotic arms with mounted magnets for dynamic magnetic-field construction. Bimanual coordination enables capabilities such as microrobot reorientation that are difficult or infeasible with a single arm, but it also introduces coupled control challenges, as the policy must generate coordinated trajectories for both actuators within a shared workspace. Our framework adapts a Qwen2.5-VL-7B backbone using Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) to process visual observations and language instructions for action prediction. To capture task progression, we introduce a motion-aware phase classifier and a phase-conditioned Action Chunking Transformer (ACT) decoder for temporally coherent multi-step control. We further construct a teleoperated magnetic microrobot manipulation dataset covering three task configurations. Ablation studies show that the ACT-based decoder substantially outperforms alternative generative action heads. In real-robot experiments, Mag-VLA achieves a 90% approach success rate across all tasks and transport success rates of 80%, 70%, and 50% as task difficulty increases. These results demonstrate that hierarchical VLA modeling provides a promising framework for magnetic microrobot manipulation.

</details>


### [192] [What Frozen VLAs Already Know About Success: A Probing Study of Value-Like Structure in Foundation Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28527)
*Jiachen Zhang,Junnan Nie,Junyi Lao,Wei Cheng,Chenghao Liu,Jiaxin Jiang,Songfang Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Frozen VLA policies implicitly encode outcome success information despite being trained only on imitation objectives. This information can be extracted via lightweight linear probes and used at test time for action selection, improving task success rates without retraining the policy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether VLA policies, trained solely on imitation learning, encode latent information about task success/reward that can be extracted and leveraged without additional training.

Method: Train lightweight linear probes on frozen VLA (OpenVLA, Pi0.5, DINOv2, CLIP) features to predict Monte-Carlo outcome targets from mixed success/failure trajectories in LIBERO-Goal. Evaluate probe accuracy under same-task, same-timestep matched comparisons to control for shortcuts. Use probes as test-time selectors over sampled action prefixes from Pi0.5.

Result: VLA features consistently predict outcome success; Pi0.5 probes achieve ~92% pairwise ordering accuracy under stringent controls while label-shuffled baselines remain at chance. As a test-time action selector on push-plate, success rate improves from 26.7% (greedy) to 44.3%; a second positive case on wine-rack is also reported.

Conclusion: Frozen VLAs encode success information their imitation objective never explicitly requires. This can be extracted via simple probes and used for online action selection, though gains are not universal and come with additional inference cost.

Abstract: Vision--language--action (VLA) policies are trained to imitate actions; their loss never asks them to estimate reward, progress, or future success. Their frozen representations nevertheless carry such information, and it can be read out and used to guide action choice without retraining the policy. From mixed successful and failed manipulation trajectories on LIBERO-Goal, we recover Monte-Carlo outcome targets using lightweight linear probes on frozen features. The targets are consistently predictable from OpenVLA, Pi0.5, DINOv2, and CLIP features, and substantially less so from baselines built on progress, time-to-go, task identity, or proprioception. To rule out task and temporal shortcuts, we evaluate the probes under same-task, same-timestep matched comparisons: Pi0.5 probes still reach roughly 92% pairwise ordering accuracy, while label-shuffled controls stay at chance. Used as a test-time selector over sampled Pi0.5 action prefixes, the same probe turns this offline finding into behavior: on push-plate, success rises from 26.7% under greedy decoding to 44.3%, with a second positive case on wine-rack. The gains are not universal and require additional inference compute, but the underlying finding is clean: frozen VLAs already encode information about success that their imitation objective never explicitly demands.

</details>


### [193] [SPRINT: Efficient Spectral Priors for Humanoid Athletic Sprints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28549)
*Yantong Wei,Kaihong Huang,Hainan Pan,Jiawei Luo,Jiawei Zhou,Ziyan Mai,Zhiwen Zeng,Yaonan Wang,Huimin Lu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SPRINT introduces a frequency-adaptive spectral prior framework enabling humanoid robots to sprint at 6 m/s with zero-shot sim-to-real transfer using only five reference motion sequences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The lack of humanoid-viable kinematic reference data and the inability of existing frameworks to maintain stability during sprints hinder humanoid athletic sprinting.

Method: SPRINT uses efficient, frequency-adaptive spectral priors that characterize the fundamental periodicity of human locomotion in the frequency domain from a reference library of five discrete motion sequences, generating kinematically feasible joint trajectories across a broad velocity spectrum and enabling extrapolation beyond the reference distribution.

Result: Achieves zero-shot sim-to-real transfer on the Unitree G1 platform, reaching a peak sprinting velocity of 6 m/s with seamless gait transitions and biomimetic naturalness.

Conclusion: Frequency-adaptive spectral priors serve as a highly data-efficient foundation for humanoid athletic sprints.

Abstract: The pursuit of humanoid athletic sprints is hindered by a scarcity of humanoid-viable kinematic reference data and the inability of existing frameworks to maintain stability during sprints. To overcome these limitations, we introduce SPRINT, a novel framework driven by efficient, frequency-adaptive spectral priors. By characterizing the fundamental periodicity of human locomotion in the frequency domain using a reference library of five discrete motion sequences, these priors generate kinematically feasible joint trajectories across a broad velocity spectrum, successfully extrapolating to speeds that exceed the reference distribution. Guided by these pretrained priors, the SPRINT policy achieves zero-shot sim-to-real transfer in field experiments on the Unitree G1 platform, reaching a peak sprinting velocity of 6 m/s and demonstrating seamless gait transitions while preserving biomimetic naturalness. Ultimately, this work establishes frequency-adaptive spectral priors as a highly data-efficient foundation for humanoid athletic sprints. The project page is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/w/SPRINT-138A/.

</details>


### [194] [SARAD: LLM-Based Safety-Aware Hybrid Reinforcement Learning with Collision Prediction for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28583)
*Kangyu Wu,Peng Cui,Guoxi Chen,Ya Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SARAD is a safety-aware hybrid framework combining LLMs and DRL for autonomous driving, using RAG-enhanced LLM-guided decisions, an attention discriminator for policy optimization, and a collision predictor to improve safety and efficiency, with significant results demonstrated in Highway-Env simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional DRL suffers from unsafe random exploration and slow convergence, while LLMs have inherent latency in real-time inference. The goal is to address both safety and efficiency challenges in autonomous driving decision-making.

Method: SARAD substitutes DRL's random exploration with RAG-enhanced, LLM-guided decisions from a dynamic expert knowledge repository, integrates an attention discriminator to incorporate LLM prior knowledge into DRL policy optimization, and includes a collision predictor module fine-tuned with historical collision data.

Result: Significant performance improvements in the Highway-Env simulator, validating the effectiveness of the proposed model for autonomous driving.

Conclusion: The SARAD framework effectively synergizes LLMs and DRL to achieve safer and more efficient autonomous driving, overcoming the limitations of both approaches individually.

Abstract: Ensuring both safety and efficiency in decision-making for autonomous driving systems remains a fundamental challenge. Traditional Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL) suffers from unsafe random exploration and slow convergence, while Large Language Models (LLMs) demonstrate inherent latency in real-time inference operations. To address these limitations, this paper proposes SARAD, a novel safety-aware hybrid framework that synergizes LLMs and DRL for autonomous driving. SARAD substitutes the random exploration of DRL with Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG)-enhanced, LLM-guided decisions sourced from a dynamic expert knowledge repository. An attention discriminator is proposed to integrate the prior knowledge of LLMs into DRL policy optimization. A collision predictor module, fine-tuned with historical collision data, is further designed to improve vehicle safety. Extensive experiments show that SARAD achieves significant performance improvements in the Highway-Env simulator, validating the effectiveness of the proposed model in autonomous driving.

</details>


### [195] [PrimitiveVLA: Learning Reusable Motion Primitives for Efficient and Generalizable Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28634)
*Yutai Li,Shaohui Peng,Jiaming Guo,Di Huang,Zihao Zhang,Yuxuan Guo,Yunkai Gao,Siming Lan,Ling Li,Xing Hu,Yunji Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PrimitiveVLA shifts VLA models from Direct Instruction-to-Control Mapping to a Primitive-Centric Disassemble & Assemble paradigm, using a Multimodal Canonical Representation to disassemble demonstrations into reusable motion primitives and assemble them via a VLM-based planner and LLM-generated switch module, significantly improving data efficiency and zero-shot generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current VLA models struggle with data inefficiency and poor generalization because they use Direct Instruction-to-Control Mapping, which forces models to memorize entire task-specific trajectories instead of learning reusable motion primitives.

Method: PrimitiveVLA introduces a two-phase paradigm: (1) Fine-tuning-phase Disassembly, which uses an automated pipeline to decompose demonstrations into reusable primitives via a shared Multimodal Canonical Representation (MCR); and (2) Inference-phase Assembly, which employs a VLM-based planner with an LLM-generated switch module for closed-loop execution, recombining primitives to handle novel tasks.

Result: The framework achieves improved data efficiency and superior zero-shot generalization across unseen and long-horizon robotic tasks compared to standard VLA models.

Conclusion: Shifting from monolithic trajectory memorization to primitive-centric disassembly and assembly effectively addresses fundamental bottlenecks in VLA adaptation, enabling reusable motion pattern learning.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models offer a promising paradigm for generalist robotic policies, yet their adaptation is hindered by data inefficiency and poor generalization. We argue that these bottlenecks stem from the prevailing Direct Instruction-to-Control Mapping, which forces models to memorize monolithic trajectories rather than reusable motion patterns, i.e., primitives. We propose PrimitiveVLA, a framework that shifts this paradigm toward a Primitive-Centric Disassemble & Assemble paradigm. Supported by a shared Multimodal Canonical Representation (MCR), PrimitiveVLA unifies two phases: (1) Fine-tuning-phase Disassembly, which uses an automated pipeline to disassemble demonstrations into reusable primitives; and (2) Inference-phase Assembly, which employs a VLM-based planner and an LLM-generated switch module for robust closed-loop execution. By disassembling tasks into reusable primitives, PrimitiveVLA enables VLA models to learn invariant motion patterns instead of task-specific trajectories. Extensive experiments show that our framework improves data efficiency and achieves superior zero-shot generalization across unseen and long-horizon tasks.

</details>


### [196] [Integrated Exploration-Aware UAV Route Optimization and Path Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28654)
*Jimin Choi,Grant Stagg,Cameron K. Peterson,Max Z. Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A UAV route optimization and path planning framework for hazard monitoring under uncertain, evolving prior information, integrating exploration-aware routing, B-spline trajectories, and online replanning that improves KL reduction by 15.9% over offline planning and 48.6% over straight-line traversal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: UAVs face limited flight endurance when monitoring hazardous environments (disaster zones, wildfires, etc.) where prior hazard information is incomplete, spatially imprecise, and changes during execution; existing approaches fail to jointly optimize visiting reported locations and exploring for new information under these uncertainties.

Method: Models environment as a spatial risk belief map; reported hazards are uncertain ROIs. Solves vehicle routing problem over ROIs, augments route with pseudo-nodes for coverage, allocates remaining flight budget across segments, and optimizes dynamically feasible B-spline trajectories. During flight, updates belief map from measurements and replans when new information justifies adaptation.

Result: Across 48 scenario configurations, online replanning achieves 15.9% better average KL reduction than offline optimized planner and 48.6% better than straight-line traversal.

Conclusion: The proposed exploration-aware framework effectively balances visiting known hazard reports with exploring uncertain areas, and online replanning significantly improves information gain under evolving prior information.

Abstract: Uncrewed aerial vehicles (UAVs) are increasingly used for exploration-driven monitoring in hazardous environments such as disaster zones, contaminated sites, wildfire areas, and damaged infrastructure, where limited flight endurance must be allocated between visiting reported locations and gathering new information. In these settings, prior information regarding hazards is often incomplete, spatially imprecise, and subject to change during execution. For example, initial reports may identify a region where a hazard is likely to exist, but the actual hazard may be displaced, partially observed, or entirely unreported. We present an integrated exploration-aware UAV route optimization and path planning framework for hazard monitoring under uncertain and evolving prior information. The environment is represented as a spatial risk map, where each location has an associated belief of hazardous conditions. Reported hazards are modeled as uncertain regions of interest (ROIs) rather than confirmed target locations, requiring the UAV to inspect reported areas while also using its limited flight endurance to explore informative regions. The proposed method solves a vehicle routing problem over reported ROIs, augments the route with auxiliary pseudo-nodes to improve spatial coverage, allocates the remaining flight distance budget across route segments, and optimizes dynamically feasible B-spline trajectories for local exploration. During execution, UAV measurements update a grid-based belief map, and the remaining trajectory is replanned when new information and the remaining budget justify adaptation. Across 48 scenario configurations, online replanning improves average KL reduction by 15.9% over the offline optimized planner and 48.6% over straight-line traversal.

</details>


### [197] [How VLAs Fail Differently: Black-Box Action Monitoring Reveals Architecture-Specific Failure Signatures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28726)
*Krishnam Gupta*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VLA architectures (VQ-BeT, Diffusion Policy, ACT) show fundamentally different failure signatures at the motor-command level. Direction reversal rate is a universal failure predictor across all architectures (AUROC 0.79-0.93), jerk monitoring works only for discrete-token architectures (AUROC 0.41-0.88), and velocity violations are non-predictive everywhere (AUROC 0.41-0.69) despite being the most common safety mechanism. Architecture-matched monitor selection is essential, enabled by SafeContract—a training-free, black-box action monitoring toolkit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To identify whether different VLA (Vision-Language-Action) architectures exhibit distinct failure signatures at the motor-command level, and to determine whether common monitoring approaches (e.g., velocity checking) are universally effective or require architecture-matched selection.

Method: Evaluated three VLA architectures (VQ-BeT, Diffusion Policy, ACT) on 450 episodes across PushT and ALOHA 14-DOF bimanual manipulation. Tested three monitoring signals: direction reversal rate, jerk monitoring, and velocity violations. Used AUROC and p-values to assess predictive power of each monitor per architecture.

Result: (1) Direction reversal rate is a universal failure predictor (AUROC=0.93, 0.79, 0.91; p<0.001). (2) Jerk monitoring is predictive only for discrete-token architectures (AUROC 0.88, 0.69, 0.41, following discrete-to-continuous gradient). (3) Velocity violations are non-predictive everywhere (AUROC 0.41-0.69), yet are the most common safety mechanism in VLA deployment. (4) Continuous-family VLAs show near-zero velocity monitoring signal (AUROC=0.52, 0.41).

Conclusion: Discrete and continuous VLA families produce qualitatively different failure signatures requiring different monitors. No single monitor works universally; architecture-matched monitor selection is essential. This was enabled by SafeContract, a training-free, black-box action monitoring toolkit with conformal calibration.

Abstract: We discover that VLA architectures fail in fundamentally different, predictable ways at the motor-command level. Running VQ-BeT, Diffusion Policy, and ACT on identical evaluation protocols (n=450 episodes across PushT and ALOHA 14-DOF bimanual manipulation), we find: (1) direction reversal rate is a universal failure predictor across all three architectures (AUROC=0.93, 0.79, 0.91; p<0.001); (2) jerk monitoring is predictive only for discrete-token architectures, following a discrete-to-continuous gradient (0.88, 0.69, 0.41); (3) velocity violations alone are non-predictive everywhere (AUROC 0.41-0.69), yet velocity checking is the most common safety mechanism in VLA deployment code; and (4) for continuous-family VLAs, velocity monitoring provides effectively zero predictive signal (AUROC=0.52 on ACT, 0.41 on Diffusion), proving that architecture-matched monitor selection is essential. These results quantify a monitoring consequence of the well-known discrete/continuous VLA distinction: the two families produce qualitatively different failure signatures that require different monitors. No single monitor works universally; architecture-matched selection is required. This finding was enabled by SafeContract, a training-free, black-box action monitoring toolkit with conformal calibration. Code: https://github.com/krishnam94/vla-edge

</details>


### [198] [Imitation Learning for Robot Assistance in Open Surgery: A Multi-Policy Evaluation on Suture Following](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28736)
*Xucheng Wang,Zhizhou Yang,Xiaoman Zhang,Sung Eun Kim,Romain Hardy,Pranav Rajpurkar*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: First evaluation of general-purpose imitation learning for surgeon-robot collaborative assistance in open surgery, focusing on suture following. Four policies (ACT, Diffusion Policy, SmolVLA, π₀) benchmarked across 28 models and 32 configurations. π₀ achieves best results with 92% stitch completion rate in surgeon-robot trial. Depth perception and end-effector design identified as key challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To evaluate whether general-purpose imitation learning can enable effective surgeon-robot collaborative assistance in open surgery, specifically for the grab-pull-release motion in suture following, which has not been previously studied.

Method: Collected 160 teleoperated demonstrations (32,374 frames) on an open-source robot arm. Benchmarked four imitation learning policies (ACT, Diffusion Policy, SmolVLA, π₀) across 28 trained models evaluated in 32 configurations along three clinically motivated dimensions: dataset size, camera viewpoint, and background variation.

Result: Under ideal conditions, all four policies achieve 50-75% task success, with depth error as dominant failure mode. π₀ (with pretrained vision-language backbone) shows superior data efficiency, robustness to background variation, and smoother trajectories. In surgeon-robot trial, π₀ achieves 92% stitch completion rate.

Conclusion: Collaborative robotic assistance in open surgery is a feasible target for imitation learning. Depth perception and end-effector design are identified as key priorities for clinical translation.

Abstract: This study presents the first evaluation of general-purpose imitation learning for surgeon-robot collaborative assistance in open surgery, targeting suture following: the grab-pull-release motion an assistant performs at every stitch. We collect 160 teleoperated demonstrations (32,374 frames) on an open-source robot arm, benchmark four architecturally diverse imitation learning policies (ACT, Diffusion Policy, SmolVLA, $π_0$) across 28 trained models evaluated in 32 configurations along three clinically motivated dimensions: dataset size, camera viewpoint, and background variation. Our results demonstrate that under ideal conditions, the four policies achieve $50$-$75\%$ task success, with depth error as the dominant failure mode across all architectures. Among all policies, $π_0$ achieves the strongest results with a pretrained vision-language backbone, demonstrating superior data efficiency, greater robustness to background variation, and smoother trajectories compatible with surgical workflow. When deployed in a surgeon-robot suturing trial, $π_0$ yields a $92\%$ stitch completion rate. These findings establish collaborative robotic assistance in open surgery as a feasible target for imitation learning and highlight depth perception and end-effector design as key priorities for clinical translation.

</details>


### [199] [Beyond Binary: Sim-to-Real Dexterous Manipulation with Physics-Grounded Contact Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28812)
*Jiahe Pan,Stelian Coros,Jitendra Malik,Toru Lin*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper introduces Center-of-Pressure (CoP) as a physical tactile representation for sim-to-real transfer in contact-rich manipulation, enabling zero-shot transfer on a multi-fingered hand by preserving dense contact information while being robust to the simulation-reality gap. It also proposes a differentiable dynamics-based sensor calibration scheme and demonstrates superior performance over coarse binary-contact and raw-taxel baselines in peg-in-hole insertion and ball balancing tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world data collection for contact-rich manipulation is a bottleneck; sim-to-real RL offers a scalable alternative but the simulation-reality gap prevents effective use of dense tactile modalities. Existing methods simplify tactile data into coarse low-dimensional features, sacrificing richness needed for complex manipulation.

Method: (1) Introduces Center-of-Pressure (CoP) as a physically grounded tactile representation that preserves dense contact information while enabling robust sim-to-real transfer. (2) Proposes a differentiable dynamics-based sensor calibration scheme to estimate taxel orientations without requiring ground-truth force measurements.

Result: CoP-conditioned policies achieve zero-shot sim-to-real transfer on a multi-fingered hand on both peg-in-hole insertion and ball balancing tasks, outperforming coarse binary-contact and raw-taxel baselines. Analysis shows CoP-conditioned policies encode task-relevant physical properties like object mass as an emergent byproduct of control.

Conclusion: CoP is an effective tactile representation that bridges the sim-to-real gap for dense tactile sensing, enabling zero-shot transfer in challenging contact-rich manipulation tasks while preserving more information than prior simplified representations.

Abstract: A primary bottleneck in contact-rich manipulation is the difficulty of collecting real-world data. Sim-to-real reinforcement learning offers a scalable alternative, but the simulation-reality gap prevents information-dense modalities like touch from being effectively used. Existing sim-to-real methods often mitigate this gap by simplifying tactile data into coarse low-dimensional features -- sacrificing the richness required for complex manipulation. In this work, we introduce Center-of-Pressure (CoP), an effective tactile representation grounded in physical principles that preserves dense contact information while maintaining robustness for sim-to-real transfer. To support this representation, we propose a sensor calibration scheme based on differentiable dynamics, enabling the estimation of taxel orientations without requiring ground-truth force measurements. We evaluate CoP on two blind, challenging contact-rich manipulation tasks: peg-in-hole insertion and ball balancing. Across both tasks, policies conditioned on CoP achieve zero-shot sim-to-real transfer on a multi-fingered hand, and outperform both coarse binary-contact and raw-taxel baselines. Analysis of learned policy states further suggests that CoP-conditioned policies encode task-relevant physical properties, such as object mass, as an emergent byproduct of control.

</details>
